## Volume I:2 — Opening invocation and praise
*Original PDF physical page 15 · connected_upper_first_pass · NOT independently certified.*

In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Compassionate. Praise be to the One who guided us to the path of Islam: praise commensurate with His favours. Thanks be to the One who granted us success in refining theological discourse and bringing its objectives within reach: thanks adequate to His increase of bounty. May blessings be continually invoked, for as long as the traces of the Islamic benefits endure upon the pages of the days, upon our master and Prophet Muhammad, whom God sent to all people and chose to subdue error and raise the banner of guidance; and upon his pure family. [The invocation continues at the beginning of the next leaf.]

**Source anchors (selected original Arabic, not a full diplomatic transcription)**

الحمد من هدانا إلى طريق الإسلام · حمدا يوافي نعمه · وشكر الذي وفقنا على تهذيب الكلام وتقريب المرام · على سيدنا ونبينا محمد

**Reading caution:** Highly ornate rhymed and partly metrical prose; uncertain ornamental epithets are not presented as a certified verbatim transcription. Middle and lower printed commentary are separate voices.

## Volume I:3 — Sanandajī names himself and explains the need for a commentary
*Original PDF physical page 16 · connected_upper_first_pass · NOT independently certified.*

[Blessings also upon] his honoured and elect Companions. To proceed: the servant who stands in need of the kindness of his Lord, who is rich beyond all need, 'Abd al-Qadir, son of Shaykh Muhammad al-Sa'id al-Takhti, says: The section devoted to kalam in the Tahdhib, composed by the exemplar of scholars, that second great master Sa'd al-Din al-Taftazani—may his spirit be sanctified—combines firmness of construction and powerful concision with unusual intellectual insights that the passage of time has not effaced, and precious subtleties whose true beauties have not yet all been disclosed. Although I was immersed in the encumbrances of my time and its disturbances, someone whose wishes I could not properly oppose, and with whom agreement was incumbent on me, urged me to explain it and to disclose what it contains. I therefore undertook to draw out its intended meanings and the secrets of its expressions, in response to that request. [The syntactic conclusion of this appeal continues on I:4.]

**Source anchors (selected original Arabic, not a full diplomatic transcription)**

فيقول العبد الفقير إلى ألطاف ربه الغني · عبد القادر ابن الشيخ محمد السعيد التختي · لما كان قسم التهذيب من الكلام · مشتملا على غرائب أفكار

**Reading caution:** The original has rhymed phrases, compressed literary descriptions and a continuation into I:4; English retains the argumentative movement, not every rhyme.

## Volume I:4 — Purpose and modesty of the commentary; praise of his teacher
*Original PDF physical page 17 · connected_upper_first_pass · NOT independently certified.*

[I undertook] to elucidate its expressions and bring its meanings close enough that their difficulties could be made intelligible, like pearls released from their shells. The result, by God's praise, was a commentary that would suffice for its intended aim: a clarification of rare points, an opening of the obscurities and a removal of difficulties, as far as the writer was able. I hope it will be found free of damaging defects and deficiencies, but I submit it to the reader's examination rather than insist on my own judgment. If you remain doubtful about what has been claimed, look again and return your gaze: do you see any flaw? All of this I ascribe to the overflowing instruction of my father and teacher, whose counsel and benefit lie behind this work. May God continue to grant him happiness and increase his standing. [The accompanying verse of supplication and the naming of the work continue onto I:5.]

**Source anchors (selected original Arabic, not a full diplomatic transcription)**

توضيح عباراته · فجاء بحمد الله ذلك شرحا · خالياً عن شوائب الخلل والنقصان · فانظر ثم ارجع البصر هل ترى من فطور

**Reading caution:** The source is intensely ornate. The sentence beginning “فانظر ثم ارجع البصر” echoes Qur’anic wording; it is presented as the author’s quotation/allusion, not as independent proof of textual perfection.

*Original PDF physical page 18 · connected_upper_first_pass · NOT independently certified.*

## Volume I:6 — The theological section of the Tahdhīb and why the preliminary material belongs here
*Original PDF physical page 19 · connected_upper_first_pass · NOT independently certified.*

“This”—that is, the material which follows—is the science of the section of kalam in the book called the Tahdhib. The expression “section” is used with some latitude, because what is mentioned here may belong either to that which has to precede the investigation of kalam—the first chapter of the introduction—or to the science itself. Once the preliminary discussions have been distinguished from the inquiries particular to the science, the material can be distributed among its proper chapters. The continuation specifies the several subjects that fall under kalam and its preliminary topics. [The list of chapters passes into I:7.]

**Source anchors (selected original Arabic, not a full diplomatic transcription)**

هذا أي ما سيأتي علم · قسم الكلام من الكتاب المسمى بالتهذيب · وهو الباب الأول في المقدمة

**Reading caution:** Only a short upper paragraph appears on this leaf; the middle and especially lower discussion of the title and nomenclature is substantial and is not included here.

## Volume I:7 — Chapters on existence, accidents, substances, prophecy and the divine
*Original PDF physical page 20 · connected_upper_first_pass · NOT independently certified.*

[Among the inquiries are] existence, the subject of the second chapter on general matters; or something particular to the possible existent that does not subsist by itself but in another, which is the third chapter on accidents; or the possible existent which subsists in itself, which belongs to the fourth chapter on substances. As for the Necessary Existent, exalted is He, the inquiry may consider Him with reference to His sending messengers and commissioning prophets; this is the sixth chapter, on the matters known from revelation. Or it may consider Him without that specific respect, and this is the fifth chapter, on divine matters. The preceding chapter on preliminary principles is thus not simply one more species of existent: it prepares for the discussions that follow.

A printed heading then introduces “The first chapter of the introduction.”

**Source anchors (selected original Arabic, not a full diplomatic transcription)**

الباب الثاني في الأمور العامة · الباب الثالث في الأعراض · الباب الرابع في الجواهر · الباب السادس في السمعيات · الباب الخامس في الإلهيات

**Reading caution:** This upper page is mainly an enumeration of chapters. The lower long note debates why logic and preliminary knowledge count as an introduction.

## Volume I:8 — Why kalām begins with knowledge, investigation and proof
*Original PDF physical page 21 · connected_upper_first_pass · NOT independently certified.*

The inquiries of kalam generally begin with the discussion of knowledge, rational investigation and demonstration. For the acquisition of doctrinal convictions through investigation and inference, and the reply to those who deny the attainment of knowledge altogether, or its derivation from investigation either absolutely or specifically in divine matters, depend upon these inquiries. They are therefore placed first, in accordance with what is customary as a prolegomenon to any science.

“Kalām is the science of doctrinal convictions”—that is, of judgments whose intended end is the heart's assent to them; this is in contrast with the practical rulings whose intended end includes performance. The “doctrinal convictions” are thus not defined merely by the external subject matter of every proposition about religion, but by the kind of cognitive adherence the science seeks. [The definition continues on I:9.]

**Source anchors (selected original Arabic, not a full diplomatic transcription)**

مباحث العلم والنظر والدليل · لأن تحصيل العقائد بطريق النظر والاستدلال · الرد على منكري حصول العلم مطلقا · علم الكلام هو العلم بالعقائد

**Reading caution:** The phrase “علم الكلام” and definition are embedded propositions; the surrounding explanation is not all a direct quotation from Taftāzānī. The lower gloss distinguishes knowledge and belief at length.

### Separate lower commentary — author UNASSIGNED

A question may be raised: what is meant by saying that these preliminaries are peculiar to the science of kalam? The intended peculiarity is comparative rather than exclusive, as the author states in the Sharh al-Maqasid. The other Islamic sciences may have reasons to employ some of these discussions; the issue is their special bearing upon the purpose of kalam. One may also say that when the same discussions occur in logic, they are considered insofar as they concern conception and division, or as preparation for explaining logic's own subject. Here, however, their function is to decide whether knowledge is possible, whether it is acquired by consideration, and whether consideration is productive of knowledge without qualification. Under that aspect the inquiry is required for the *commencement* of kalam; the discussions in logic alone do not furnish what is needed for these theological objections.

On the expression “judgments whose intended end is merely their belief by the heart”: the word “judgment” here means a complete assertoric relation, not a positive legal ruling in the technical usage of jurists. It comprises such propositions as obligation, possibility and other predicates, insofar as they are considered for assent. The intended end is the assent itself, whereas in practical disciplines the purpose is also the manner of acting. This does not entail that a theological proposition can have no effect on action, nor that a practical proposition does not require belief. The distinction is what is primarily sought in the respective disciplines. [The note continues at the end of I:8 into the following page; its first and final compressed expressions need further collation.]

**Textual caution:** This is a connected selective translation of substantial printed lower argument, NOT an all-clauses diplomatic rendering and not a positive historical identification of its author.

## Volume I:9 — Doctrinal, religious and certain: qualifications in the definition of kalām
*Original PDF physical page 22 · connected_upper_first_pass · NOT independently certified.*

The intended “convictions” are those connected with religion, as distinct from the rulings of jurisprudence, whose end is practice. They are called *religious* because they are attributed to the religion brought by our Prophet Muhammad—God bless him and grant him peace—whether the doctrine actually belongs to that religion, as in the discourse of those taken to be right, or is only attributed to it, as in the discourse of the heterodox sects discussed by the author. It is attribution to the religion, rather than the truth of every claim made in its name, that supplies the immediate delimitation here.

If “knowledge” were intended in the entirely unrestricted sense of any assent, whether it corresponds to reality or not, it would also include error. The additional qualification “from certain proofs” would then have to specify the kind of epistemic grounding intended. The text accordingly examines how the reference to *certain evidence* should be understood and whether it refers to what genuinely produces certainty or to what the disputant regards as certain. [The problem is developed on I:10.]

**Source anchors (selected original Arabic, not a full diplomatic transcription)**

العقائد الدينية · سواء كانت من الدين في الواقع ككلام أهل الحق · أو لا ككلام الفرق المبتدعة · عن الأدلة اليقينية

**Reading caution:** Literal terms “الفرَق المبتدعة” describe the historical author’s classifications, not editorial endorsement. The lower commentary questions whether belief and demonstrative knowledge have been illicitly conflated.

## Volume I:10 — Certainty, evidence, and the scope of the discipline
*Original PDF physical page 23 · connected_upper_first_pass · NOT independently certified.*

The intended sense must be knowledge possessed with certainty. For a proof properly called certain is one that yields certainty, rather than a proof merely supposed to do so by the individual offering it. But it may be answered that the description “certain” is being applied here to the evidence as regarded by the investigator; it does not by itself resolve the status of every opponent's argument.

A further difficulty arises from the scope of the definition. If one speaks simply of knowing doctrinal propositions, the knowledge of God and the Messenger about those propositions, and even the adherence of one who merely follows another, may seem to fall under the expression. Yet the science of kalam is concerned with acquired knowledge obtained through demonstrative consideration, not every occurrence of correct assent or an infallible source's knowing the truth. The role of knowing the arguments is therefore essential to the intended definition. [The concluding qualification passes into I:11.]

**Source anchors (selected original Arabic, not a full diplomatic transcription)**

الدليل اليقيني · في الاعتقادات · علم الله وعلم الرسول بالاعتقادات · وكذا اعتقاد المقلد

**Reading caution:** Portions of the upper text are compressed in the scan. The lengthy lower text debates the epistemic status of the imitator (muqallid) and degrees of certainty. Not translated here.

## Volume I:11 — The subject of the science: anything considered in relation to doctrine
*Original PDF physical page 24 · connected_upper_first_pass · NOT independently certified.*

This applies also to the knowledge of the Companions, although their discourse was not called *kalam* in that earlier period, just as the names of other disciplines were not always in use at the time of their first practice. A discipline is not made to depend upon the historical introduction of its later technical name.

“Its subject is the known, insofar as it bears upon that [end].” That is, the things considered within the science are not confined in advance to a single class of existents. They are taken up under the respect in which knowledge of them bears upon establishing religious doctrine. [The explanation of the subject continues on I:12.]

**Source anchors (selected original Arabic, not a full diplomatic transcription)**

علم الصحابة · وموضوعه المعلوم من حيث يتعلق بذلك

**Reading caution:** The main upper text on I:11 is very short. Most of the leaf is a separate extended lower objection concerning subject matter, acquired knowledge and justification.

## Volume I:12 — The scope of the known: being, nonbeing and intermediate claims
*Original PDF physical page 25 · connected_upper_first_pass · NOT independently certified.*

The respect in question is the connection with religious convictions. For some inquiries concern states or predicates that rule out beginninglessness or unqualified unity; others concern body and accident, origination and composition, or substantiality, individuation and other categories. Still others concern impossibilities and things that do not exist, such as the impossibility of a second deity. All these matters may serve the establishment of religious doctrine, even though not every one of them is itself an article to be believed as an independent dogma.

If the subject is described as “the known” (*al-maʿlūm*), this is intended to include what exists and what does not exist, and even what would be regarded as a thing under discussion despite its impossibility. The subject of kalam is therefore broader than a genus of corporeal things, or even than actually existent things. The inquiry remains governed by the regard in which these matters function in the science. [The text continues on I:13.]

**Source anchors (selected original Arabic, not a full diplomatic transcription)**

أي بالعقائد الدينية · أحوال المانع من القدم والوحدة · أحوال الجسم والعرض والحدوث والتركيب · أحوال المحال والمعدومات · المعلوم المتناول للموجود والمعدوم

**Reading caution:** The upper list is compressed and the reference to impossible/nonexistent states needs further collation. Avoid automatically translating “maʿlūm” as “existent.”

## Volume I:13 — What counts as a theological question: ends and explanatory bases
*Original PDF physical page 26 · connected_upper_first_pass · NOT independently certified.*

Under the respect just stated, the subject takes in the predicates of the questions treated by the science. Thus the intended religious doctrines themselves are included, together with the means by which they are established and the principles upon which their proofs depend. It is not required that every individual question should be one of the doctrines in its final form: the matters leading to their establishment also enter the science under the relevant consideration.

The end of all this is success in the felicity of the two abodes. The commentator adds that different disciplines may refer to that ultimate end while differing in their proximate subjects and methods. Kalam is distinguished by the doctrinal aim that governs the way its objects are discussed, not by treating every useful religious proposition as a question of kalam. [The account of ends begins to be distinguished further on I:14.]

**Source anchors (selected original Arabic, not a full diplomatic transcription)**

من الحيثية المذكورة يتناول محمولات مسائله · العقائد الدينية · الوسائل الموصلة إلى تلك العقائد · غاية الكل الفوز بسعادة الدارين

**Reading caution:** Mixed main text with Taftāzānī lemmas. The marginal analysis of the existence/nonexistence subject is not translated completely.

## Volume I:14 — The end of kalām and whether knowledge must be acquired
*Original PDF physical page 27 · connected_upper_first_pass · NOT independently certified.*

The benefit just mentioned is an ultimate end, in relation to which a closer end is sought through the discipline. Knowledge is not merely the presence of a formula in memory: it is the attainment of the appropriate relation to what is known. A question arises whether every instance of knowledge must be acquired by investigation.

The Imām al-Rāzī favoured the view that some knowledge is necessary rather than acquired, for two reasons. First, a person's awareness that he exists is not normally the result of constructing a demonstration. Second, if every act of knowledge had to be acquired from an earlier act of knowledge, there would be no starting point for learning: either the acquisition would proceed in a circle or it would regress indefinitely. The author distinguishes these contentions from an unqualified claim that *all* theoretical knowledge is immediate. [The remainder of the discussion of necessary and acquired knowledge passes to I:15.]

**Source anchors (selected original Arabic, not a full diplomatic transcription)**

فيكون غاية كغاية الغاية · والعلم اعتقاد لموضوعه · واختار الإمام الرازي لوجهين · أن علم كل أحد بأنّه موجود ضروري

**Reading caution:** The lower writer offers detailed alternatives on the meaning of “necessary.” The upper is a connected source reading, not exhaustive all-register translation.

## Volume I:15 — Conception, assent and the distinction between acquired and necessary knowledge
*Original PDF physical page 28 · connected_upper_first_pass · NOT independently certified.*

The objection assumes no distinction between having knowledge and conceiving what knowledge is. But the necessary and immediate item may be the actual occurrence of a particular cognition—such as the cognition of one's own existence—whereas one's *conception* of that cognition is something different. The claim that the conception of knowledge requires an earlier conception of knowledge does not follow merely from the occurrence of the cognitive act itself.

For this reason, the argument from immediate self-awareness must not be made to prove more than it proves. The reality of a cognitive state and one's reflective apprehension of that state are different considerations. Likewise, the regress objection needs to distinguish the knowledge being acquired from the conditions that permit one to acquire it, lest it shift covertly from a claim about knowing a thing to a claim about conceiving the nature of knowing. [The text carries the distinctions into I:16.]

**Source anchors (selected original Arabic, not a full diplomatic transcription)**

على عدم التفرقة بين تصور العلم وحصوله · والضروري حصول علم جزئي بوجوده · وهو غير تصوره · توقف تصوره على تصوره وغير تصور علمه

**Reading caution:** Beginning has inherited context from I:14. The source refers to opponents’ objections and answers; the bracketed articulation in English is explicitly explanatory.

## Volume I:16 — What “knowledge” designates: apprehension, judgment and certainty
*Original PDF physical page 29 · connected_upper_first_pass · NOT independently certified.*

A second route to determining the meaning of knowledge is by division. Belief may be firm or not firm; a firm judgment may correspond to reality or fail to correspond to it; and a judgment corresponding to reality may be secure or unstable. By these divisions one seeks to separate knowledge from mere conviction, from error and from a judgment held only on unstable grounds.

The terminology is not uniform. In some usages “knowledge” (*ʿilm*) means the apprehension of a simple object and “recognition” (*maʿrifa*) that of something composite; others reverse or qualify that distinction. Some speak of knowledge as the occurrence of the form of the thing in the intellect; others as firm, reality-corresponding, stable assent. The science is thus confronted with more than one definition and needs to distinguish the intended mode of cognition from the differences introduced by technical usage. [The subsequent proposed definition is examined on I:17.]

**Source anchors (selected original Arabic, not a full diplomatic transcription)**

والثاني طريق معرفته عنده القسمة · فإن الاعتقاد إما جازم أو غير جازم · وإما مطابق للواقع أو غير مطابق · فإن العلم إدراك البسيط والمعرفة إدراك المركب · وحصول صورة الشيء في العقل والاعتقاد الجازم المطابق الثابت

**Reading caution:** This leaf includes a long lower controversy over necessity and the division of knowledge. The precise sequence of all propositional cases is still a working reading.

## Volume I:17 — Knowledge as complete disclosure and the act of rational consideration
*Original PDF physical page 30 · connected_upper_first_pass · NOT independently certified.*

Another definition makes knowledge an attribute through which an object is disclosed or becomes manifest to the knower, with complete disclosure. The expression “complete” is meant to exclude the uncertainty that belongs to opinion: on this usage knowledge is opposed to conjecture. An apprehension of a simple or particular thing may be called “recognition,” but this is a matter of terminological convention, not a discovery that the reality of knowledge changes according to the names given to it.

The purpose of mentioning these conventions is not to pretend that the essence of knowledge has thereby been definitively captured. They explain how different authors use the words while leaving the question of a proper real definition open.

“The reality of consideration [*naẓar*] is a movement of the soul”: a transition of the intellect from what it already knows toward something not yet known, by arranging cognitions in such a way as to reach the sought result. [The following page explains the transitions in this process.]

**Source anchors (selected original Arabic, not a full diplomatic transcription)**

أو صفة يتجلى بها · انكشافا تاما · عند القائلين بهذا التعريف مقابل للظن · ويسمى إدراك البسيط أو الجزئي معرفة · تنبيه على اختلاف الاصطلاحات · وحقيقة النظر حركة النفس

**Reading caution:** The alternative definition and whether it covers simple/compound objects require careful technical comparison. The tiny separate middle gloss is not merged into this upper translation.

## Volume I:18 — The stages of consideration and the place of the unknown conclusion
*Original PDF physical page 31 · connected_upper_first_pass · NOT independently certified.*

Consideration takes place among intelligible objects. The intellectual movement has stages: first, attending to what is sought and what is lacking; then turning toward the known matters from which the sought result might be obtained; then arranging those cognitions in the required order; and finally obtaining the previously unknown conclusion. The movement is called a movement of the soul, not because the intellect moves in physical space, but because it proceeds from a present cognition to another act of cognizing.

The familiar descriptions of a movement *towards* the principles and a *return* from them must not be taken to imply two different physical journeys. They describe the dependence of the result on premises, and the mind's use of those premises in eliciting a conclusion. Since the question is how to acquire what was not already known, a consideration that simply repeats the result from the outset would not perform its purported task. [The lower objections to this arrangement, and a further upper qualification, continue on I:19.]

**Source anchors (selected original Arabic, not a full diplomatic transcription)**

في المعقولات · وعودا · الحصول · المجهول · وكونه · لا يقصد أصلا

**Reading caution:** The main upper on this leaf begins mid-sentence and describes the ordering of mental transitions; several tiny connectors remain uncertain; this is not a certified literal reconstruction.

## Volume I:19 — Acquired knowledge requires productive consideration; theological exceptions
*Original PDF physical page 32 · connected_upper_first_pass · NOT independently certified.*

[The prior example concerns] the judgment that one is half of two. Although an act of investigation may be invoked in explaining how an evident proposition is considered, it does not follow that any sequence of propositions whatsoever yields a previously unknown result. Where investigation is said to generate knowledge, the conditions under which its premises genuinely supply a new cognition must be satisfied.

“In divine matters” the difficulty is sharper, because some disputants deny that consideration can produce knowledge there, whether from the nature of the objects or from the limits of inference. The author distinguishes what is known immediately from what requires a proof, and asks whether the necessity attached to knowledge in some cases supports a sweeping denial of proof in theology. The question is not settled by the mere occurrence of disagreement: disagreement may arise even around propositions asserted to be necessary. [The final clause crosses into I:20, and the lower writer's extensive objection is not fully translated.]

**Source anchors (selected original Arabic, not a full diplomatic transcription)**

وكون ذلك الواحد نصف الاثنين · وفي الإلهيات · ولا يقتضي العلم إلا إذا تحققت الحقائق · وبدون العلم · ضروري · الاحتياج إلى بيان

# Direct-source continuation I:20–23 (incomplete marginal translation)

## I:20 — The possibility of disagreement over necessities
**Physical PDF 33.**

### Upper running text (continuing I:19; continues I:21)

For even a necessary matter may become the subject of disagreement, whether through obstinate refusal or inadequate understanding. Otherwise, the divine realities [the sentence is completed on the following page] …

### Lower commentary — unassigned (on «فإن الضروري قد يقع»)

One may choose to say that the judgment that investigation produces knowledge is itself theoretical, and that it is established by *a particular investigation*, without any resulting circle or contradiction. For example, when we say, “The world is changing; and everything changing is originated,” that, as is known, is a form of investigation. It produces knowledge that the world is originated. It follows that investigation is something that produces knowledge, as the Imām maintained. Something of this device is indicated in the author's marginal observation: it enables a response to someone who asserts the universal negative proposition [that investigation yields no knowledge whatever].

Nor does it follow that the two instances are the same in the respect at issue. The same thing can differ according to the consideration under which it is taken, and consequently differ in what can be predicated of it. Thus what is required is an investigation concerning investigation *as a means of knowledge*, not simply the performance of any operation called investigation.

There is, further, a distinction between what is assumed in the inference and what the inference establishes. In an investigation, the propositions that serve as premises must be known; their being known is presupposed in the inference. But the conclusion concerning the efficacy of investigation need not already have been assumed as a premise. Otherwise every demonstrated conclusion would be declared circular merely because its demonstration depends upon something already known.

More precisely, the disputed point is the particular property of an investigation by virtue of which it stands as an instrument leading to knowledge. The statement that a thing is known by virtue of evidence does not require the evidence and the thing established to be identically known in the same respect. [The original here moves through the terms *mawḍūʿ*, *makhṣūṣ* and the ‘subject’ of the judgment; the compressed syntactic chain still requires a second literal collation.]

The same point is illustrated through the familiar example of things called “originated.” We may say that the world is originated, or that every thing whose existence is preceded by nonexistence is originated; but the word may also be applied under a restricted description. From the use of one expression for these cases we cannot simply infer the very feature which is under dispute. In this way, the answer to the objection that a particular theoretical judgment must depend upon its own prior establishment is that the dependency is not in the same respect.

The glossator then returns to the distinction between a universal judgment and its instances: to have knowledge of one instance is not necessarily to possess the very universal conclusion whose possibility is disputed. Treating the particular and universal as identical would make knowledge of a particular member of a kind into knowledge of the whole kind without the required inference. [The last few compressed lines about the universality and particularity of the judgment and a subsequent lemma are left for exact re-reading; they are not counted as fully translated.] 

## I:21 — The teacher's authority and scepticism about perception
**Physical PDF 34.**

### Upper running text (continued from I:20; continues I:22)

… cannot be conceived in their very quiddities. Even if it is conceded that they can, the conceptions in question stand in opposition [to the proposed alternative proof]. Furthermore, the guidance of a teacher does not benefit [us] except after his truthfulness is known. His truthfulness is either known through investigation—in which case investigation is sufficient for knowledge—or through that teacher's own statement, which is circular, since his statement that he is truthful cannot benefit us until his truthfulness has already been known. Or it is known through the word of another teacher, in which case a regress follows.

**[Embedded lemma and reply.]** The one who rejects [the possibility of knowledge] through obstinacy is a stubborn opponent whose allegation is not heard. Likewise the Sophist who denies sensible truths, or first principles, or both, is a disputant whose claims are not admitted. The printed author glosses the name *Sophist*, explaining the Greek components as signifying knowledge and error, and presents the group as a class of philosophers. [The school-etymology is reported as printed and is not independently endorsed.]

### Lower commentary — unassigned (on «لا يفيد إلا بعد العلم بصدقه»)

It has been objected that we do not make the reception of knowledge depend upon an independently established recognition of the teacher's truthfulness. Rather, the point may be taken to be that the knowledge thereby conveyed is itself truthful or certain. But if the intended way of gaining knowledge is investigation, it remains necessary to distinguish a recognition gained through the instruments of inquiry from a recognition independently guaranteed by the teacher's report. The alleged teacher cannot provide precisely what he is being asked to establish simply by asserting that he provides it.

The marginal writer then questions the wider supposition that inquiry is indispensable to all cognition. The appeal to the need for inquiry in obtaining knowledge is liable to overlook instances in which someone comes to possess recognition without an explicit investigation. Likewise the claim that no such knowledge is possible has to contend with the many cases of persons already knowing what the sceptic sets out to deny. [Several small-type examples of persons and their states of knowledge are compressed and need independent reading; their identity is not supplied here.]

A further consideration is that the senses in which a person is described as knowing must not be conflated. What is presented as someone's attainment of faith or recognition may not have involved a demonstrative inference of the sort now being discussed. A universal claim about the necessity of demonstration cannot simply be extracted from a case where a person first learned something from another's speech.

### Lower commentary — separate note on «اسم طائفة من الحكماء» (begins here)

The name is given to a group of people who took pleasure in sophistical arguments. The writer reports that different schools among them were distinguished according to what they denied: some denied primary intellectual truths, some denied sense knowledge, and some challenged both. The distinctions are not all treated as one thesis; they prepare the upper text's separate refutations. [The printed historical anecdote and continuation at the foot of I:21 carry into I:22 and remain to be collated literally.]

## I:22 — Attacks upon sense and upon first principles
**Physical PDF 35.**

### Upper running text

Among them are some who object to sensibles by saying that the judgment of sense sometimes goes wrong: a stationary thing may be seen as moving, and conversely, as one sees a ship as stationary when it is moving, while the shore appears to move though it is stationary. Hence, they argue, a judgment of sense in any particular case is liable to error and ought not to be accepted.

The answer is that immediate evidentness excludes the supposition of error in *certain* sensible matters. An error in some cases does not rule out conforming, certain judgment in many other cases: for example, in judging that the sun illuminates and that fire burns, and similar judgments.

Others attack the first principles, arguing that the most evident of them is our assertion that affirmation and negation do not occur together and do not both fail to occur: that is, a thing either is or is not. Yet, they say, even this is not beyond doubt, since knowing this proposition presupposes forming the conceptions of existence and nonexistence—being and not-being—[continued on I:23].

### Lower commentary — first two small notes

**On «والجواب أن البداهة»:** This means the immediacy of the *intellect's* recognition, not that of sense [invoked to prove itself]; otherwise the answer would be an attempt to establish the authority of sense through sense itself.

**On «إلى غير ذلك»:** That is, other instances of propositions known with customary certainty. [The closing abbreviation does not independently identify the writer.]

### Lower commentary — longer objection, unassigned

The investigators observe that there has scarcely been anyone among the learned who wholeheartedly adopted this position [of denying all sensible and primary truths]. Instead, it is often said to characterize extreme Sophists, whether through their mistaken conception or as a polemical designation. The marginal writer connects the name of the group with one who denies sensibles and then places the issue within the reports of particular schools and philosophers.

On the objection concerning sensible things: it is the proper starting point to confine the objection to the sensible judgments alone, rather than already to extend it to the whole of intellectual cognition. To claim that the sense faculty errs in certain judgments is not to have shown that all judgments of sensation fail. [The note further discusses the appearance of error and the possibility of attributing certain deceptive cases to another faculty's judgment; the precise assignment of the examples in the print requires a second pass.]

On the mention of *first principles*, the question is whether the supposedly self-evident propositions are themselves put under suspicion. Some have interpreted the denial of sensible things as secondary to a denial of fundamental judgments, since the correctness of certain sensory claims presupposes the mind's acceptance of an evident distinction. But the denial of one class does not of itself entail the denial of every other. [The following argument continues on I:23.]

## I:23 — Can knowledge of noncontradiction depend upon noncontradiction?
**Physical PDF 36.**

### Upper running text

… and upon grasping the meaning of a thing's being a subject and of its being a predicate. But the opponent says that understanding these notions inevitably depends upon the reality of the very proposition being disputed, since it is one of the first principles upon which all the others depend. For instance, when considering the proposition that the whole is greater than the part, we see that if the whole were not greater, the part would have to be greater than the whole; and that is impossible. It is alleged that an appeal of this kind already employs the principle of noncontradiction. Thus, if understanding the principle were made dependent upon that reasoning, circularity would result.

The reply is that the intellect judges this proposition decisively, without investigation or inferential proof. Those who attack both sensibles and first principles have thereby rejected what is immediately evident, and their objection does not defeat the original judgment simply by calling it into question. [The closing compressed clause about the opponent's admission and the kinds of evidence requires a second literal check.]

### Lower commentary — continuation and separated lemmatic notes

**On «وعلى تحقق معنى كون الشيء موضوعا»:** What is meant is the intellectual grasp of a thing as *subject*, and of something else as *predicate*. But it is open to objection whether this understanding itself needs the judgment whose priority is being alleged.

**On «توقف لا محالة»:** It is better to construe the clause as the answer to the hypothetical condition, rather than as an attribute describing investigation. Otherwise it might misleadingly suggest that there is some investigation which need not terminate in the relevant first principle. On this construal, the consequent of the supposition is the following statement, “Then circularity follows.”

**On «قوله لازمة بها»:** This refers to the proposition, that is, to the predicative relation it contains. The writer's point is that judging affirmatively or negatively presupposes the ability to distinguish the two. This does not prove that every explicit piece of reasoning about that distinction must precede the immediate understanding of it.

**On «من غير نظر واستدلال»:** That is, without making judgment of the first principle depend on a further investigation. From the fact that assent to the first principle is immediately given it does not follow that the *conceptual terms* involved in a later demonstration must each have been established through it as a prior conclusion. On this account the apparent circle is removed by distinguishing direct grasp from the inferential use of a prior judgment.

The gloss also suggests construing the phrase about “first principles” with reference to the immediacy of the subject-predicate judgment. A reader should not take the text to mean that *every* concept preceding assent is already an inferential conclusion. The qualification is required to keep the case of direct conceptual apprehension distinct from the later deduction. [The final several lines, discussing ‘direction of relation’ and responses to the denial of first principles, remain provisionally rendered and are not fully diplomatically established.]

# Source-checked continuation I:24–33 (still requires a second read)

## Printed I:24 — Is knowledge after investigation a matter of customary conjunction or generation?

### Larger upper text (the running argument)

[What has been discussed] earlier, in reply and argument. **“And is it”**—that is, the investigation's being productive of knowledge—**“by way of custom”**? This is on the assumption that all possible existents are referred to God, exalted is He, directly and from the outset, and that He is able to choose. Thus nothing must proceed from Him of necessity. **“Or”** is it **“by way of generation”**, on the assumption that [not] everything is attributed to Him immediately? [The words about direct and mediated action are continued on the next original page.]

### Lower text, first printed gloss: «قال المصنف رحمه الله بطريق العادة»

The author, God have mercy on him, said, **“By way of custom.”** This is the Ashʿarī position: knowledge of the conclusion occurs following correct investigation through the operation of custom. That is based upon the principle that all possible things, in their entirety, are referred to God directly, and that no one of them has an independent part in bringing another into existence. Rather, God may bring some things into existence after others, without their being necessitated by Him, for He is a choosing agent. If He repeatedly brings one after another into existence, that is called **custom**; if it does not recur, it is called a departure from custom. Knowledge obtained after investigation is, without doubt, a possible and recurrent event. Thus it is referred to custom. [The note ends with a conventional pious closing.] 

### Lower text, second printed gloss: «قال رحمه الله أو التوليد»

He said, God have mercy on him, **“Or generation.”** This is what the Muʿtazilites hold. It follows from their principle that our voluntary acts proceed from us: either **directly**, when they do not proceed from us through another of our own acts, or **by generation**, when they do proceed through another act of ours. Accordingly they claim that the knowledge occurring after investigation is an act proceeding from us through the mediation of investigation; thus its proceeding from us occurs by generation. [The printed note ends with a pious closing.]

### Lower text, further short lemmatic notes

**On “by way of custom”:** That is, through the fact that this regularly occurs, while its failing to occur remains possible as a departure from custom.

**On “so nothing is necessitated by Him”:** Because, as will be stated, the effect of a choosing agent is not necessary. Those who hold this position include people who assign it exclusively to divine power, without the servant's power attaching to it, and others who make it an acquisition (*kasb*) which is also within the servant's power.

**On “or generation”:** On their position, the knowledge obtained from investigation is among the servant's acts and among the things within the servant's power; so is investigation itself. However, the occurrence of the first act is followed by the second. It is reported that some of our associates, after rejecting generation generally, argued against it here too: if investigation generated knowledge, recollecting the investigation would generate knowledge just as initiating it does, since there is no relevant difference. This is answered by saying that the argument does not produce certainty, since it returns to an analogy of a disputed sort, and a difference between the two cases is apparent. [The last words describing the analogy's kind and its answer should be checked at greater magnification.]

---

## Printed I:25 — Direct production, *tawlīd*, and emanation by necessity

### Larger upper text (beginning within the I:24 sentence)

[For] He, exalted is He, is an agent who acts directly. The meaning of **generation** is that an act issues from an agent **through another act issuing from him**, as with the hand's movement [causing] the key's movement: the key's movement issues by reason of the hand's movement. Its opposite is **direct action**, which is the issuance of an act from him without an intermediary. It is objected that knowledge does not belong to the category of *action*. The response is that **the other act** in this case is *imparting knowledge* (*ifādat al-ʿilm*), which is an action. **“Or”** it occurs **“by way of necessity,”** on the assumption that the effusion of originated things from the overflowing principle takes place of necessity when the receiving subject is completely prepared. [The criticism of this theory begins on I:26.]

### Lower text, first printed gloss: «قال قدس سره أو الوجوب»

He said, may his spirit be sanctified, **“Or necessity.”** This is the view of the philosophers. It is based on their principle that the overflowing principle, with respect to the existence of originated things, necessitates [its effects] by essence, although their effusion from it depends upon the presence of complete preparedness. It is known that the knowledge arising after investigation is an originated thing, and so it is included under that rule. The author has omitted a **fourth** position: it is the view chosen by Imām al-Rāzī, al-Ghazālī and the Imām of the Two Sanctuaries. Its purport is that the occurrence of knowledge after sound investigation is necessary by a rational necessity, while it is not generated from the investigation. [The note closes with a prayer.]

### Lower text, second printed gloss: «قوله واعترض بأن العلم»

**On “and it was objected that knowledge ...”:** The *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid* says, in substance, that investigation itself, on the more precise view, is not an item in the category of action either. But by **action** the disputants intended what is produced through an agent, not the agent's influencing as such. They have indeed agreed that the motion of the hand and the motion of the key are [ascribed to] the agent, although motion is not, strictly speaking, a member of the category of action.

**On “the other act here is imparting knowledge”:** I say that this “other act” cannot simply be assumed to be the generated act of the servant. Even if imparting knowledge is granted to be an action, the question remains: is it the action of the one performing investigation, or [what is described as] the action of the investigation? The distinction matters for whether this constitutes generation in the technical sense. [The source's compressed objection here is not yet textually secure in every word.]

**On “from the overflowing principle”:** They intend by this the **Active Intellect**, which they describe as impressed with the forms of the generated things, the forms overflowing from it upon our souls according to their preparedness. They maintained that **the Spirit** and **the Clear Book** in the language of revelation are expressions referring to it. This is reported in the commentary on the *Maqāṣid*. [The historical attribution is translated, not endorsed.]

**On the last distinction:** [A final short objection about whether the alleged generation is truly established and whether one can validly infer the later event merely from an earlier occurrence follows. Its tightly compressed printed syntax needs an independent check. It has **not** been replaced by an invented completion.]

---

## Printed I:26 — The fourth position and the difference between rational entailment and productive causation

### Larger upper text

This [account of necessity] is built on the denial of God's freedom of choice—exalted is He exceedingly above that. **“There is disagreement about it”** among the Ashʿarites, the Muʿtazilites and the philosophers, in the order already mentioned. **“And investigation for knowledge of God, exalted is He”**—that is, for the sake of obtaining certainty [the predication “is obligatory” is printed at the start of I:27].

### Lower text, separate short gloss: «قوله لأجل حصول اليقين»

**On “for the sake of obtaining certainty”:** This indicates that, in the explanation, *knowledge* and *recognition* mean **certain assent**, because knowledge concerning a particular object may occur by sensing it, which itself entails certain assent to its existence. [The printed note ends with the customary abbreviation.]

### Lower text, second long gloss: «قوله فيه خلاف بين الأشاعرة»

**On “there is disagreement among the Ashʿarites”:** He has mentioned that there is here another position, chosen by Imām al-Rāzī. The Proof of Islam [al-Ghazālī] states that it is the position of the majority of our associates. It is that **investigation entails knowledge of the conclusion by way of necessity**, without investigation being a cause of it as in the philosophers' doctrine, or a generating act as in the Muʿtazilite doctrine.

But the assertion of necessity must be made clear. If what is meant is merely that the knowledge usually follows the investigation, then the account reverts to the first [Ashʿarī] doctrine. What is intended is **rational entailment**: one who knows the investigation [as an inference] cannot fail to know the conclusion, just as is the case with necessary implications generally.

The meaning is not, however, that God ceases to be a choosing agent. If what is meant is that God could refrain from bringing about the knowledge by not attaching His power and choice to it, then the account becomes the first doctrine again. Nor does the necessitation described here necessarily contradict the knowledge's being an effect of a powerful and choosing agent, who is able to act and to refrain. For **refraining** can mean His not bringing about the act, without requiring that, while He produces the entire cause with all its conditions, He also produces an effect apart from it.

The possibility of refraining is wider than the absence of every intermediary. Consequently, the occurrence may be conditioned upon the absence of an impediment; and that condition too may be within divine power. The source draws a comparison here with products of generated actions in the account of the Muʿtazilites, who assign them to the servant's power. What contradicts the possibility of leaving an action undone is **necessity in the sense of the absolute impossibility of separation from the effective cause**. It is this last sense, rather than every sense of rational entailment, that is under dispute.

Accordingly, on this fourth account the entailment of theoretical knowledge is **rational**, without this in itself denying the choice of the agent. On the first account the connection is **customary**, in the sense explained above. [The note closes at the foot of I:26; the detailed interpretation of one conditional connective in the middle requires a second grammatical collation.]


---

## Printed I:27 — Whether the prerequisite of an absolute obligation is obligatory

### Larger upper text (one very short line, completing I:26)

**“... is obligatory for [establishing] His existence.”** [The subject is rational investigation for knowledge of God, introduced on I:26; the printed upper line occupies only the top of this leaf. Everything after the rule is lower commentary, not Sanandajī's main text.]

### Lower commentary — one long argument, author unassigned

**On «واجب».** That is, [investigation is] obligatory by revelation if the obligation of the unconditionally obligatory act is established by revelation, and by reason if it is established by reason, as in the *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid*.

Know that there is disagreement over this well-known premise, and confusion in the ways of setting it out. Most writers hold that the **prerequisite within one's power, upon which an absolute obligation depends, is also obligatory** by reason of the obligation of that act—absolutely, whether it is a *cause* or a *condition*. They argue in one fashion that, if the prerequisite were not obligatory, leaving aside what the obligation depends upon would be permitted, and the originally obligatory act would consequently not be obligatory. They argue in another that, if the prerequisite of an unconditioned obligation were not obligatory, one could lawfully leave it undone while the obligation of the primary act continued. Yet the act without its prerequisite is impossible. Obliging the person to perform it in that condition would therefore be impossible.

Others maintain that the prerequisite does **not** inherit the obligation in an absolute way. Against them it is said that the indication obliging the act is silent concerning the obligation of its prerequisite; and some qualify the principle by saying that the prerequisite is obligatory if it is a *cause*, but not if it is a *condition*, on the ground that the cause has a closer connection with the effect than the condition has with what is conditioned.

Another group argues that where the thing upon which an obligatory act depends is a condition, the condition must be treated differently: the lawgiver has not necessarily made the condition an object of the command merely by commanding the conditioned act. One must first decide whether the presence of the condition itself was required. The absence of a condition can mean that the obligation has not become operative rather than that a presently binding obligation cannot be performed.

The question is not exhausted by contrasting causes and conditions. A cause too may stand in a relation to the effect that is different from the relation of a means one is able to provide. The intended prerequisite is one by whose procurement a presently binding act can be performed. What is entirely outside the agent's power does not fall under the requirement to procure it in the same way. Much of the controversy concerns precisely how the parties define the *prerequisite* and the *unconditional obligation*.

It is also necessary to distinguish what is *obligatory in itself* from what becomes obligatory by virtue of another obligation. When the command is directed to an end, an indispensable means may be required **for that end**, without being intended as an independent end for which the lawgiver has issued a second, separately worded command. In that case, the question whether omitting the means amounts to a distinct culpable omission has to be settled rather than assumed.

If some writers describe an absolute obligation as obligatory notwithstanding the absence of its prerequisite, while others say that an impossible thing cannot be commanded, this should not immediately be taken as a contradiction. One may mean that the obligation is in force and the person must procure the means, while the other means that performance without the means is impossible. The two assertions differ in their object.

There remains a difficulty with the alleged proof from silence. The text indicating that an act is obligatory may say nothing expressly about its prerequisite. Yet silence as to an express *separate* obligation does not prove that the prerequisite need not be procured. Conversely, merely pointing out that the end was commanded is not, without more, an explicit command to perform every particular means that might contribute to it.

The glossator returns to the common interpretation that an unconditioned obligation requires its indispensable means when these lie in the agent's power. He notes that many of the verbal disagreements in the books are better explained by distinguishing the ways in which the obligation of the prerequisite is meant, rather than declaring the familiar premise true or false without qualification. The final portion of his comparison with al-*Maqāṣid* and some later theologians runs on to I:28. [One passage about the exact distinction between **cause** (*sabab*) and **condition** (*sharṭ*) remains difficult, and this paragraph is an interpretive translation of its argument rather than a verified diplomatic rendering.]

---

## Printed I:28 — Revealed proof and the prerequisite of obligatory knowledge

### Larger upper text (continuing the line on I:27)

**“By the text”**—that is, the Book and the Sunna—**“and by consensus,”** namely the community's agreement on its obligation; **“and because it”**—namely rational investigation—**“is within one's power, and a prerequisite of the obligatory knowledge”** in the case under discussion.

### Lower commentary — continuation of the long I:27 argument

[The writer continues comparing the earlier explanations.] These explanations do not make the statement of someone denying the obligation stand as the straightforward contradictor of the other statement, although the disputants have treated them as contradictories—as is plain to anyone who examines the books. Further, the explanation offered for the position associated with Imām al-Ḥaramayn indicates that by this premise they mean the obligation of a prerequisite **which the Lawgiver requires the person to obtain**, not merely every item without which the commanded thing cannot exist under any possible description.

The material already cited contains more than one meaning of the *absolutely obligatory*. Some treatments make it a legal question specifically, while others formulate the rule in general terms. The more evident interpretation is that **the act's being obligatory does not automatically imply a separately stated obligation of its prerequisite**. The claim that this would require commanding the impossible is answered by distinguishing two things. What is impossible is to command the performance of a thing **without the existence of what it depends upon**. What actually follows from the disputed position is only that the act may be commanded **without an explicit declaration that its prerequisite is obligatory**. This is not impossible: silence about the prerequisite at the time the primary act is made obligatory does not establish that the prerequisite is not obligatory.

Furthermore, the obligation of the act entails the obligation of the *available prerequisite*, considered as a prerequisite—that is, something upon which the thing's existence depends, whether rationally or in another respect. This is the sense in which the argument should be considered. [Here the writer calls attention to his own way of resolving the dispute.]

**On «أمراً مقدوراً».** The word “within one's power” is broader than an *act* directly subject to power. Something may be possible for an agent to procure even if it is not itself an act of the same kind, since the agent's power may attach to bringing it about or to its causes. For that reason the wording says **“a thing”** (*amr*), rather than simply **“an act”** (*fiʿl*).

**On «للمعرفة الواجبة».** It has been objected that knowledge's being obligatory depends on the possibility of *commanding* knowledge, and this possibility is disputed. If a person already has the knowledge, commanding him to acquire it would be demanding what is already present; if he has none, the demand seems to be addressed to someone unaware of what is demanded. But the response is that its being commandable is immediately evident once the relevant distinction is made. A person to whom the summons has not reached, or who has received the words but does not comprehend their meaning, is not the same as a legally accountable person who has understood the demand but does not yet know its object. Such a person can be charged with coming to know it. [The source's final phrase distinguishes the addressee's awareness of the **command** from possession of the **knowledge commanded**; its exact last few words need a second reading.]


---

## Printed I:29 — Does the command to know God imply an unconditioned obligation to investigate?

### Upper register (one printed line, continuing I:28)

… that is, by text and consensus, and [the prerequisite] which lies within one's power, which is a prerequisite on which the unconditionally obligatory act depends.

### Lower text: brief lemma on «الواجب المطلق»

By “the unconditionally obligatory” is meant an act whose obligation is *not* conditioned on the thing upon which its performance depends—as the obligation of almsgiving, for example, is conditioned on the possession of the minimum amount (*niṣāb*). [That comparison explains why not every prerequisite must be procured under an already binding command.]

### Lower text: objection and reply on «أي بالنص والإجماع»

It has been denied that a proof has been established for the obligation of gaining knowledge through investigation. As for **scriptural text**, one cannot simply cite the Almighty's words, “Know that there is no deity but God,” because the indication is not decisive: a command may be used for something other than obligation. As for **consensus**, its evidentiary force is questioned in this matter; the opponent instead maintains that **following authority** (*taqlīd*) suffices. For the Companions, may God be pleased with them, accepted Islam from people who followed them without imposing upon every one of them the requirement of critical verification and inferential demonstration.

It has been answered that the question concerns the obligation of investigation according to revealed law, and that accepting an outward profession of Islam does not establish that the person's inward recognition is based solely on unreasoning imitation. The source discusses the possibility that knowledge is reached by proofs whose possession is not formally expressed as an argument. The words “not demanding a demonstration” must therefore not be made equivalent to “declaring demonstration forbidden or unnecessary in every case.” [Two clauses about transmitted versus inferential evidence remain to be checked in the lower printed text.]

### Lower text: a second explanation of «الواجب المطلق»

The *unconditionally obligatory* does not mean what is obligatory under **all imaginable supposition and states**. Otherwise no obligatory act at all would be unconditionally obligatory, for every obligation is qualified by certain conditions—including the agent's not being incapable of it. What is intended is rather that, once the prescribed obligation is present, its being obligatory is not conditioned on the presence of **the prerequisite under consideration**. Thus the obligation of an act can remain operative even when the means to perform it has not yet been provided.

It does not follow that all things whose absence would render performance impossible are therefore separately enjoined as independent duties. But where the prerequisite is within the agent's power, neglecting it can frustrate a duty already binding. The example of almsgiving is again instructive: obtaining the minimum property threshold is not commanded merely on the ground that almsgiving becomes obligatory when the threshold exists, whereas seeking water for an obligation of purification already binding may stand differently.

Accordingly, the conclusion “investigation is obligatory because it is the prerequisite of obligatory knowledge” must show that the *knowledge* is already binding on the addressee and that inquiry is the required available route to it. If the knowledge were not obligatory, its means would not acquire obligation merely from being a means. [The gloss closes by promising a further discussion of the difference between the obligation of a prerequisite and the condition of an obligation.]

---

## Printed I:30 — The Muʿtazilite argument from fear of punishment

### Upper register

**Among the Muʿtazilites**, investigation directed to knowledge of God is obligatory, because it is a prerequisite of the knowledge that is obligatory, **since knowledge averts a feared harm**, namely the apprehension of punishment. On our account the obligation is established by the **revealed law**; on theirs it is **rational**.

**They say:** If the obligation of investigation depended on a command of the revealed law—such as the Prophet's, peace be upon him, call to consider the miracle, and in general whatever must be investigated before his truthfulness can be known—then [the existence and authority of] the law would have to be established first. Yet establishing [the authority of] that law depends on investigation into the miracle and the signs of prophecy. Consequently, it would not be right to say that one must investigate the miracle only after the law's authoritative command had been established. That would be circular.

**We say in reply:** Investigation is not obligatory prior to the establishment of the law **by virtue of a legal command**. The fact that one may have to investigate the signs of prophecy before accepting the law as authoritative does not establish that the legal obligation to do so already existed at that earlier stage. [The end of the upper argument and its reply continue into I:31.]

### Lower note on «خوف العقاب»

That is, fear of punishment on account of *leaving* the recognition of God, exalted is He, and of the harm expected from doing so. If the fearful expectation is removed by the attainment of knowledge, then the means leading to knowledge is said to be obligatory under the rational principle of avoiding probable harm. [The note does not itself establish that the feared harm necessarily occurs.]

### Lower note on «وفي جميع ما يتوقف ثبوته على ثبوته»

That is, in everything whose establishment depends upon the establishment of the revelation's authority. The law's being authoritative is itself dependent upon establishing the truthfulness of the Lawgiver's messenger. In turn the proof of the messenger's truthfulness rests upon knowledge of the miracle and what makes it a sign. These steps are themselves matters of investigation. If the legal obligation of that very investigation were claimed to depend upon the already established law, the difficulty would recur.

The glossator notes the difference between asserting that something **cannot be known without** a prior proof and asserting that there must already be a **legal command to perform** that proof. A rationally intelligible inference about which knowledge comes first is not yet a demonstration that religious law imposed an obligation before its own authority was acknowledged. [The last small-type lines develop the rejoinder that a revealed obligation of inquiry may arise only once revelation is established; the precise syntactic relation of one clause remains uncertain.]

---

## Printed I:31 — How could revelation command the investigation which authenticates it?

### Upper register

The revealed law is not established for one who has not investigated, because its proof is itself theoretical. **It is answered**, first, that the fact that [the law's authority] is not yet known to a certain person does not entail the impossibility of a rational act of investigation. The opponent's inference from lack of established legal obligation to lack of all access to knowledge is not valid.

Secondly, the obligation of investigation in the first instance does not require that the law already have been accepted by the very addressee as a conclusion. Its evidential basis can be established through another route before he knows it. [The phrasing across the first few lines is compressed, and the complete shape of the first answer should be collated from I:30–31.]

The opponent also claims that, because the obligation of the principal knowledge is rational, investigation must be obligatory. But one must distinguish the **knowledge which is sought** from the **investigation which leads to it**. The former may be obligatory, while the latter is the way to reach that obligation; the same inferential relation does not prove that every legal command's authority exists before the demonstration which establishes it.

On the obligation of investigation, the text then places before the reader another inquiry: **“What is the first obligation?”** Some have said it is **knowledge [of God]**. Some have said it is **investigation**, as the means of knowledge. Others have said it is the **intention to investigate**, because intention precedes investigation. The distinction is between what is first *as the sought end* and what is first *in the order of performing what leads to the end*. [The next printed page resumes the dispute about intention.]

### Lower short lemma on «بأن المتوقف على ثبوته»

There is an objection to the assertion that the law depends upon inquiry: on the account already given, establishing the authority of revelation is not the same as establishing an already binding legal obligation to investigate. The author of the note again distinguishes a proof's epistemic priority from a command's binding force.

### Lower gloss on «ولا يثبت الشرع عند من لم ينظر»

The sense of the objection is that if someone has not yet investigated, revelation's authority is not established *for that person*, so how can he be charged by it with performing the very inquiry that he has not yet performed? The answer appeals to the possibility that the Lawgiver's demand be known or made intelligible prior to the full inferential recognition, and it warns against assuming that every way of understanding a command requires completing the same theological demonstration.

The alleged circularity can therefore be dissolved by distinguishing the person who has heard and understood an invitation to investigate from the person who has already affirmed the Prophet's authority through a completed proof. The invitation can be understood as a demand before the addressee has the final assent whose acquisition is its object. [The writer then qualifies this on the ground that a command's being binding is not simply the same as its being intelligible; the remaining printed clause on this leaf requires exact collation.]

---

## Printed I:32 — The first duty: knowledge, inquiry, or intending to inquire?

### Upper register

The scholars who maintain the obligation of knowledge and of inquiry have differed concerning the **first duty**. Some state that the first duty is knowledge itself, and others that it is investigation, because it is the condition of reaching knowledge. Others say that it is **the intention to undertake investigation**: the act of inquiry cannot be performed intentionally unless one first directs oneself toward it. But this proposal has to be distinguished from an endless regress of intentions before intentions.

The author then gives the evidential basis for considering a question of this kind. **“The proof”** is that [the matter about which inquiry is commanded] must admit of cognition, and the investigation must lead toward a **decisive judgment** (*ḥukm jāzim*) or toward its contrary; the discussion also mentions **guesswork** or **probability** (*ẓann*) and whether a demanded assent is grounded in sound evidence.

It is indicated that what is properly demanded in theological inquiry is a **decisive judgment conforming to reality** (*al-jāzim fī muqābalat al-amāra* [printed phrase to be rechecked]). If what was called knowledge were merely a thought appearing likely, one would not yet have what the dispute calls certain knowledge.

The possible sources of evidence are then divided into intellectual and transmitted: **reason** and the **true report**, with the indispensable intellectual principles that make the employment of a transmitted proof possible. One cannot dispense entirely with the reasoning required to understand what revelation reports, even when revelation itself is the authoritative testimony.

### Lower notes — source reading, not yet a complete lower-text translation

**On «بل القصد»:** The intention to investigate is proposed as the first duty because the performance of inquiry rests upon that intention. The text distinguishes an unending regress of intentions from the first determination to enter an act of investigation; one need not suppose that every intention requires another prior intention.

**On the alleged obligation of the means:** Some criticisms say that the premise concerning the prerequisite of a duty only establishes that investigation is necessary *after* the obligation of knowledge is established. On the other hand, some opponents deny that investigation is always necessary for knowledge, since one may reach knowledge through a different route. The relation between the legal command and epistemic priority must therefore be demonstrated, not simply stipulated.

**On intending to investigate:** The gloss questions whether the intention is obligatory as a separate act or whether an already willed act of investigation includes the requisite intention. It warns against arguing in a circle: knowledge's being obligatory is invoked to establish investigation's obligation, and investigation's obligation to establish the obligation of its preceding intention. [The rest of the lower argument crosses the physical boundary into I:33 and requires separate segmentation; several lines are not transcribed or translated here.]

---

## Printed I:33 — Authentic opening of Book Two: general matters

### Upper register: closing the preceding discussion

[The text concludes the treatment of] beliefs and the establishing of other discussions that depend upon the beliefs. Accordingly, it is proper now to enter upon the intended theological subjects. We say:

### Authentic printed division

**BOOK TWO — ON GENERAL MATTERS**  
**الباب الثاني في الأمور العامة**

### Upper register: beginning of Book Two

General matters are those **which encompass all existents**, including the Necessary Being, substance and accident, such as **existence** and **unity**. For every existent, even if it is many, is one in some respect. [Other general considerations apply to] most existents, such as **specific possibility** (*al-imkān al-khāṣṣ*), **temporal origination**, **necessity through another**, **multiplicity**, and **being an effect**. The general matters are placed before the remaining subjects because they serve as principles for them. The investigation of existence is placed first because of its nobility. Hence the author says: **“The conception of existence ...”** [continued at I:34].

### Lower gloss: why a book of general matters is needed

The gloss explains that, because matters peculiar to the Necessary Being, to substance and to accident are given their own places, a separate book is needed for **conditions shared** among them: either among all three, such as existence and [a certain general use of] causality, or among two, such as quiddity and causedness. Nonexistence is studied because it stands opposed to existence; impossibility because it pertains to nonexistence; and necessity and beginninglessness because they are conditions of existence. [Some items in the printed list and the note's concluding connective require further collation; no additional divisions are supplied from elsewhere.]

### Lower commentary: what kind of preliminary knowledge is required?

The marginal writer resumes a qualification of the preceding logical account. When the purpose is **decisive knowledge**, the matters on which it depends must be established accordingly. But when what is sought is **only probable judgment**, the statement of one person or of several people whom the investigator considers truthful may suffice. If the knowledge derived from mass-transmission (*tawātur*) is treated as theoretical and inferential, as some maintain, even that should not be confused with the particular rational demonstrations required to establish God's existence and the truthfulness of prophets. These must be considered under their own conditions of certainty.

**On “the Necessary, substance and accident”:** The gloss clarifies that “existents” here denotes the great divisions of what exists, not an assertion that every property belongs equally to every member of every division. [The printed lower discussion continues on I:34, where its exact scope can be fixed.]

---
