# NEW SOURCE-CHECKED CONTINUATION: I:34–44

**Direct source-to-English working translation, 6 November 2026.** Original scan: `taqribmaram(2).pdf`, printed I:34–38 = physical PDF 47–51. The dividing rules in the original distinguish **the upper register** (Taftāzānī's parenthesised lemmata within Sanandajī's commentary) from **the printed lower registers**, whose individual writers are not identified by the layout. Text in square brackets is editorial supply or a notice that exact wording remains unresolved; it must not be mistaken for original Arabic. This is a continuing working translation, not a critical edition.

---

## I:34 — Is the conception of existence self-evident?

**Upper register — first-pass translation of the printed sequence**

> **“Necessary [or self-evident].”** For our assertion, “A thing is either existent or nonexistent,” is a self-evident assent; and that assent depends upon conceiving *the existent* and *the nonexistent*. The conceptions of both must, accordingly, be self-evident.
>
> **Objection:** But if you claim that the conceptions of all the constituents of this judgment are self-evident, that does not follow. For *existence* is one of its constituents; and even if you claim that the judgment here is necessary only *after* both terms have been conceived, that does not exclude either one of them—namely, existence—from being acquired through investigation rather than being self-evident. [The end of this sentence continues at the upper edge of I:35.]

**Selected original wording:** «فإن قولنا الشيء إما موجود أو معدوم تصديق بديهي»; «وأنه يتوقف على تصور الموجود والمعدوم»; «وإن زعمت أن الحكم في هذا التصديق ...». The exact grammar of the compressed last upper sentence requires reinspection together with I:35.

**Lower register — continuing gloss from I:33, unassigned writer; closely translated source clauses**

> One might object: The discussion in this book has also concerned things that do not apply to an existent at all, such as **impossibility**, and things particular to the Necessary, such as pre-eternity and necessity. It is answered that the investigation of the former is incidental, because it stands opposite possibility; whereas the latter two concern divisions of necessity and of that which is not preceded by nonexistence. Being necessary either through itself or through another, and not being preceded by nonexistence, belong to the general matters. As for necessity, that is clear. As for pre-eternity, it is likewise so according to the philosophers who maintain that certain substances and accidents are beginningless. According to the mutakallimūn, however, it is not [general] in that sense, for their inquiry extends to whether the predicate is to be affirmed or denied. Reflect on this.
>
> On **“and it depends on conceiving the existent”**: If what is intended is dependence on conceiving [existence] *in its reality*, the premise is denied. If what is intended is dependence on conceiving it *in some sense or other*, it does not establish the required conclusion, namely that the conception of existence in its reality is self-evident.
>
> On **“thus it is self-evident”**: [A marginal authority, whose name is compressed here,] writes: One might say that this proves the self-evidence of conceiving the **derived expression** (*al-mushtaqq*), not the **source from which it is derived** (*al-maʾkhadh*), which is what we are discussing. The answer is: what is intended in the assent mentioned is the source from which the expression is derived, not the additional meaning of the derived expression. Accordingly, if [the relevant conception] is self-evident, that conception, too, is self-evident. [The reading of this annotation’s signature is unverified.]
>
> On **“that would beg the question”**: The point is that by *assent* here one may mean either, according to the Imām’s view, something **composite**, or, according to the philosopher's view, **the judgment itself**. On the first interpretation, either the self-evidence of all its parts is intended—in which case there is a begging of the question—or the self-evidence of only some of its parts, or of it without further specification, is intended—in which case the claimed conclusion does not follow. On the second interpretation, either the self-evidence of the judgment alone or its self-evidence without specification is intended, which likewise does not establish the conclusion; or the self-evidence of the judgment **together with all its objects of reference** (*mutaʿallaqāt*) is intended, in which case the argument begs the question again. It would be better, therefore, to speak of its **objects of reference** rather than its **parts**, as in the *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid*. Reflect.

**Reading status:** This lower register has been checked sentence by sentence against the visible scan, but some small-type authorial markers and the disputed use of *qidam* remain uncertain. It is a **provisional source translation**, not a certified diplomatic transcription. The marginal writer remains unnamed.

---

## I:35 — Collective self-evidence and the self-evidence of the constituents

**Upper register — continuation from I:34**

> [The previous question continues:] It may be acquired, and yet the *assent* is self-evident without qualification, and there is no begging of the question; for its being self-evident without qualification, considered in the thing itself, does not mean that our knowledge of its self-evidence is independent of all the preliminary conceptions. [This sentence continues at the head of I:36, which supplies the explicit qualification.]

**Lower register — long continuous objection and response; source-led translation**

> On **“this assent is self-evident without qualification”**: That is, with all its parts, as stated in the *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid*. On **“and there is no begging of the question”**: The substance is that we choose the position that this judgment is self-evident together with all its related terms. There is no begging of the question, because although the self-evidence of the whole depends upon the self-evidence of each individual part, the purpose here is **not** to infer the self-evidence of the whole from the self-evidence of that part, which would indeed beg the question. Rather, the purpose is to infer, from our **knowledge that the whole is self-evident**, that the **part is self-evident**. This does not beg the question, for knowledge of the self-evidence of the whole does not depend upon knowledge of the self-evidence of its parts; it leads to that latter knowledge. The second may thus be derived from the first: when one knows that the whole is self-evident, then whichever of its parts one attends to, one knows that it is self-evident.
>
> This is the substance of what the author of the *Mawāqif* established. The author of the *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid* objected to it. The substance of his objection is this: knowledge of the whole is either **the same as knowledge of the parts** or **obtained through them**. It is therefore necessary that knowledge of the whole not be the prior source from which knowledge of a part is obtained. If it is said that one can apprehend a composite without attending to its parts in detail, we answer: this might be granted with a **real composite**, but not with a merely **notional composite**. To apprehend a notional composite is nothing but to apprehend the several items for which a single name has been established.
>
> Even if that were granted regarding *conception*, it would not be granted in the case of *assent*: there is no meaning to affirming that a composite is self-evident in all its parts other than affirming that this part of it is self-evident, and that part is self-evident, and so on. Even if this were granted, moreover, the feared begging of the question would not follow in every formulation. A proof may be known generally without one already possessing separate knowledge of the particular constituent that is identical with the conclusion. [This last concession is compressed in the print.]
>
> **I say:** The inference from the self-evidence of the composite under discussion to the self-evidence of its constituent **existence** resolves into the following: “Existence is a part of this composite; every part of this composite is self-evident.” This is a syllogism in the **first figure**. Once its premises are conceded, it yields decisively that existence is self-evident. There is therefore no objection at all [on that supposition]. It may be that the author intended, by his objection, to point instead to the difficulty set out in the writings of the earlier scholars about the first figure. If so, the criticism is not particular to this place: it would apply to any form of argument which seeks to establish a property of a part from a property of the whole. And even if all parts were held to be evident, what prevents the conclusion from being derived in this way?
>
> [The final words turn to a different objection: whether the evident *conception of existence* has been assumed in the very proof that seeks to establish it. The sentence continues on I:36.]

**Reading status:** All principal objections and responses on this leaf have been translated as an argument, not replaced with a topical digest. A few compressed connectives and the exact reference to the earlier logical objection remain for a subsequent literal collation. The voice remains a **lower-register writer of unconfirmed identity**.

---

## I:36 — Immediacy of assent; the word and the reality defined

**Upper register — continuation from I:35**

> [The self-evidence of the judgment] in itself depends upon the self-evidence of its constituents *in the thing itself*. But our knowledge that the judgment is absolutely self-evident does not depend upon our knowing that every one of its constituents is self-evident. Otherwise the conclusion would be obtained only by first conceding the very point that is to be proved. One must therefore distinguish the **actual necessity of the assent** from **the way we recognise its necessity**.
>
> **“And definition”:** He means by it an explanation of the meaning indicated by the expression, not the disclosure of an unknown essence through a real definition. Thus the explanation “by *being* or *occurrence* or *realisation*,” in terms of verbal usage, is something different from a real definition that would make the quiddity known. [The subsequent words continue the distinction of definition and verbal explanation.]

**Separate short marginal note, upper of two lower blocks**

> On **“knowledge of the whole does not depend …”**: Knowledge may be either summary or detailed. Obtaining the former does not depend upon obtaining the latter. Accordingly, it is permissible to regard the obtaining of the knowledge of the whole as evidence for the self-evidence of those components through which the conception of existence is acquired. [The end of the note is compressed.]

**Long lower note — partial source reading of the continuation from I:34–35, unassigned writer; NOT a complete literal translation**

> The conception of the existence of a particular thing is indeed necessary. But it is even more apparent that [certain inferential claims] do not establish that the *general conception of existence* is necessary without qualification. The argument being used here is more limited than its apparent conclusion.
>
> The author insists that one should distinguish the existence of a thing from apprehending its *quiddity*. Even if a particular existing thing is known, it does not follow that its whole nature is known. Likewise, one may judge “this thing is either existent or nonexistent” while the precise conception of what this thing is remains only imperfectly attained. If the judgment could not occur until a complete quiddity was apprehended, familiar judgments would be impossible.
>
> Thus, if one says, “existence is self-evident because the alternatives existent/nonexistent are self-evident,” the argument must not quietly introduce a detailed quiddity of existence into one of the premises. The assent can be necessary without the terms being presented through full philosophical definitions. But neither may one infer from the possession of some use of a term that the *reality* indicated by the term is known in its essence.
>
> What is relevant to assent is the understanding of the subject and predicate so far as they enter into the affirmation or denial. It does not require knowledge of all the hidden constituents of either concept. To that extent, the argument can retain its intended force; it does not demonstrate all that a later analysis of the concept of existence might claim. [The last sentence continues into the next author's discussion on I:37.]

**Editorial caution:** The upper distinction between *taʿrīf lafẓī* (verbal explanation) and *taʿrīf ḥaqīqī* (real definition) is source-grounded. The full lower gloss still needs a literal alignment pass; the English above is a working translation of its argumentative clauses rather than certified exhaustive coverage.

---

## I:37 — Is existence shared in meaning or merely in name?

**Upper register — argument 1, opening**

> They have differed over whether the things that exist share **existence** by a single meaning or only by a common word, and whether existence is additional to quiddity or identical with it. To establish that existence is **shared in meaning**, the author offers three considerations. Their presentation is an alert to a meaning that is already evident; the arguments remove what might obscure it.
>
> **First:** the soundness of the division of **existence** into the existence of the Necessary and the existence of what is other than the Necessary. What is divided must be shared among the divisions, for division in its proper sense is the joining of what is specific to a class to what is common to the divided subject. Thus division is not the division of a word that happens to have different unrelated meanings, as occurs when a shared sound is used for a [homonymous term]. [The discussion continues onto I:38.]

**Lower register — selected source-based gloss, not fully collated**

> What the author states is most clearly expressed as follows. There is one common conception of existence among all existents, whereas their particularisations are distinct. One may assert the existence of something while remaining uncertain which of its special modes it has. When we consider the Necessary Existent, we may affirm existence while hesitating over some of the characteristics attributed to it. Likewise, in considering a possible existent, we may affirm that it exists without yet determining whether it is a substance or an accident. What is common to the different considerations is therefore not exhausted by their special characteristics.
>
> Someone may object that the concept “existence” would then be shared in the same way as “nonexistence.” But this does not follow without a separate account of the respective meanings and their logical divisions. If a single concept may be applied to existents of radically different kinds, the reason must be made precise, not replaced with the assumption that the kinds are identical.
>
> An additional dispute concerns whether the commonality lies **in the concept** alone or also in the extra-mental reality of what is predicated. It is one thing to say that “existent” is understood by a single common meaning, and another to say that the existence of the Necessary and the existence of the possible are numerically one thing. The first is what the division is offered to establish; the second does not follow from it.

**Second, short lower gloss beginning near the foot of I:37**

> The phrase **“a meaning”** is attributed to the Shaykh Abū al-Ḥasan al-Ashʿarī as a point of contrast with those who treat the existence of the Necessary and the existence of a possible existent as distinct in meaning. The transmitted school attribution must be checked independently; this note gives the competing positions rather than verifying a historical consensus. [Its continuation is at I:38.]

---

## I:38 — Arguments 1–3 for a common concept of existence

**Upper register — completion of division, then arguments 2 and 3**

> The use of this division does not depend upon a stipulation of linguistic convention and knowledge of that convention. It is therefore not altered merely by variation among languages, unlike an attempted division based only on the ambiguous use of a shared word.
>
> **Second:** one can make a decisive judgment of existence while **remaining uncertain about the particular determination** among the types and individuals of existents. If existence were not common in meaning, but had in each case a meaning wholly other than in the next, one could not affirm existence while hesitating about the special determination. Consider a judgment that something exists while one remains uncertain whether it is a substance or an accident. A common content is affirmed although the specific type is not settled. [The original gives further qualifications about whether a special determination belongs intrinsically or accidentally to what is conceived.]
>
> **Third:** the permissibility of withholding or denying existence in relation to a quiddity. Were existence simply identical with the quiddity, to deny existence of that quiddity would be to deny a thing of itself. But one can meaningfully consider a quiddity and ask whether it exists, without thereby cancelling the conception of the quiddity. [The upper text carries this last proof forward at I:39.]

**Lower register — selected argumentative clauses, not a full literal collation**

> On **“even though it has one meaning”**: Even when existence is shared as a word, its being shared in meaning must be argued, for two expressions may have different referents under the same utterance. If the shared meaning is established, it does not follow that the particular properties of existent things are the same. Nor does the hesitancy about the particular properties force one to remain hesitant about the general proposition that the thing exists.
>
> The denial of existence in relation to a thing can be understood in more than one way: denial of an extramental instance, denial of a particular actuality, or denial of the specific determination with which one had conceived it. These should not be assimilated without explanation. The claim that one conceives a quiddity while being unsure of its actual existence establishes only that the two are distinguishable in thought. It remains a separate question whether existence is something additional in an extramental thing.
>
> In this connection the gloss mentions what would follow if “quiddity” and “existence” were taken as interchangeable terms, and why the philosophers' distinctions between *wājib* and *mumkin* cannot be reduced to a merely verbal disagreement. [The rest of this long note continues at I:39.]

---

The text above replaces earlier site-level topic summaries for **five specific printed leaves** with source-inspected connected English. **Do not count these as five completed all-register leaves:** several longer lower passages are translated only to the extent that their argumentative clauses have been securely read, and some upper joins are marked. The original images and exact physical page numbers are preserved separately. The immediate next source span is I:39 onward, with I:34–38 available for re-collation as a single continuous argument.

## I:39 — Is existence additional to quiddity *in thought*?

**Upper register — continuation and three arguments**

> [Completion of the discussion of existence's common meaning.] Otherwise an existing thing might be qualified as existent *in another sense*, and so would not be nonexistent, yet neither would it be existent in the meaning we had intended.
>
> **“And [he indicates] its addition to quiddity in the mind”:** That is, the intellect can attend to existence without attending to the quiddity, and attend to the quiddity without attending to existence. There are three grounds for this.
>
> **First: “The validity of denying it of [the quiddity].”** That is, of denying existence of a quiddity. For if existence were identical with the quiddity, such denial would be impossible: the denial of a thing of itself is impossible.
>
> **Second: [the informativeness of predication].** [The words introducing the second argument occur at the lower end of this upper passage, and their explanation starts on I:40.]

**Selected Arabic:** «على زيادته على الماهية ذهناً»; «لأن العقل أن يلاحظ الوجود دون الماهية والماهية دون الوجود»; «صحة سلبه ... عنها»; «لامتناع سلب الشيء عن نفسه».

**Lower register — source-translated opening lemmas, writer not identified**

> [Continuing the preceding proof by division:] The exhaustive division is also at issue, for [an argument drawn merely from naming] could depend on the conventional generalisation of a figurative usage, which in turn depends upon linguistic convention. But the intended division should be exhaustive *intellectually*, without depending upon those conventions.
>
> On **“a thing may be characterised by existence in another meaning”**: This does not depend on there being one common conception of nonexistence. If the meaning of nonexistence were itself multiple, the absence of an exhaustive division would be still clearer: it would be possible for a thing to be described as nonexistent in another sense, yielding an additional division.
>
> On **“the intellect can attend to existence without the quiddity”**: The purpose of saying that existence is *additional to quiddity* is to distinguish them **according to conception**, not thereby to establish that they are two separately existing extramental things.
>
> On **“one can deny existence of the quiddity”**: An objection is raised in the *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid*: the negation of the **derived predicate** (*al-mushtaqq*), namely “existent,” from a quiddity does not necessarily negate the **source** (*mabdaʾ al-ishtiqāq*) from which that predicate is derived. It is the latter that the question concerns. For to say that a thing is not existent may deny the instantiation of existence without denying every sense in which existence can be conceived. [The distinction continues in the remaining lower lines.]

**Untranslated or unresolved lower span:** The subsequent argument on whether **nonexistence**, as absence of being, can be predicated of a quiddity and whether this already presupposes the conceptual difference of quiddity and existence is lengthy. Its precise sequence of objection, quotation and counter-objection runs to I:40; it has **not** been translated line by line here. It should be segmented before being treated as a completed marginal note.

---

## I:40 — Why predicating existence adds information; existence and the Necessary

**Upper register — completion of arguments 2–3 and the opposing position**

> **Second: “The informativeness of predicating [existence] of it.”** If existence were just the quiddity itself, the predication would convey no information, for a thing's being predicated of itself does not convey information—this is necessary [or evident].
>
> **Third: “One can acquire a conception of [the quiddity].”** Knowledge that existence belongs to it may require acquisition—as when one investigates the existence of jinn or devils—whereas a quiddity's identity with itself does not have to be established in that way.
>
> This is the doctrine of the majority of the mutakallimūn and the philosophers, **except that they regard the reality of the Necessary as a particular existence**. **“Thus, according to them, existence is identical with quiddity in the Necessary.”** They argue: if existence were additional to quiddity in the Necessary, then either that existence would require [the quiddity as its cause] or it would require something else. Dependence upon something else is incompatible with the Necessary's independence, because dependence upon another pertains to what is possible. [The argument about the quiddity as cause continues on I:41.]

**Arabic anchors:** «فائدة حمله عليها»; «لأن حمل الشيء على نفسه غير مفيد ضرورة»; «اكتساب تصورها»; «كوجود الجن والشيطان»; «حقيقة الواجب وجود خاص»; «فالوجود عندهم نفس الماهية في الواجب».

**Lower gloss — direct translation of the first objection and its consequence**

> On **“because predicating a thing of itself …”**: The *predication* intended here is also the **derivational predication** (*al-ḥaml al-ishtiqāqī*), as in saying, “The jinn are existent,” not the **predication by identity or correspondence** (*ḥaml al-muwāṭaʾa*), such as when one says, “The jinn are existence.” If the latter were intended, there would be a dispute about whether the predication is properly made at all.
>
> As for the philosophers, an opponent points to the possibility of a different kind of additionality: that one may conceive a quiddity, form a second conception of existence, and then consider the quiddity as standing in a relation to what is conceived. One must not confuse a *conceptual* additionality with an independently existing attribute superadded to a reality in the world.
>
> If the source's claim is that an extra-mental existence, added to the quiddity, requires an additional cause, the reply depends on what one intends by **“added.”** The proposed regress concerns a real additional existent. It does not necessarily concern an aspect distinct only in the way the mind regards one thing. [The lower writer develops this point over the bottom of I:40 and I:41.]

**Status:** The opening technical contrast, including the two Arabic terms for predication, is translated directly; the remainder of the lower passage is condensed and awaits clause-by-clause review.

---

## I:41 — Does the Necessary have a quiddity other than existence?

**Upper register — continuation of the necessity argument**

> [If the Necessary's existence required something other than itself], it would depend upon another for its existence and would not be necessary through itself. Alternatively, if its own quiddity were what brought that existence about, the quiddity would have to precede its effect in existence. That would entail the quiddity's existing **before its own existence**, which is impossible.
>
> It is replied that [the ordering appealed to here must be specified: the priority of an agent to its effect need not simply be the possession of another, separately existing occurrence of the very existence it causes].
>
> [There is also the philosophers' point that] the cause of existence in what is possible is distinguished from a material or receptive cause. Existence can be said to subsist in a quiddity *in the intellect* while its extra-mental standing must be treated differently. **“They have a different view”:** They say that the existence of the Necessary is not an additional real feature of an independent quiddity, but its very reality; otherwise a need or composition would arise within that which is supposed to be necessary through itself. [The clause involving the alleged priority of the possible quiddity is compressed; it is retained as a source issue instead of silently emended.]

**Arabic anchors:** «الوجود في الواجب زائداً على الماهية» [from preceding leaf]; «والعلة متقدمة بالوجود على المعلول» [context]; «وأجيب بأن العلة متقدمة بالوجود» [opening of reply]; «وهم يقولون إن ما يلزم تقدمه على المعلول بالوجود هو العلة الفاعلة لوجود الواجب» [word boundaries to recheck].

**Short separate lower marginal note**

> On **“it is the efficient cause, not the receptive cause”**: This is based upon existence's inherence in quiddity, **in the possible existent**, being something conceived in the mind, rather than an extra-mental inherence in the sense of a separable accident. [The printed abbreviation does not identify a historical author.]

**Longer lower gloss — selected source reading and interpretive connective material (NOT full translation)**

> The discussion should distinguish a thing's quiddity, considered intellectually, from that which subsists independently outside the mind. The quiddity of the possible need not be regarded as an independently existent bearer waiting for an additional existence. If it were already existent before receiving existence, the difficulty would indeed follow. But that is precisely what the account being defended rejects.
>
> The distinction between **the necessary through itself** and **the possible** is being made with reference to their need for a cause. When one says that the Necessary has no quiddity other than existence, the claim is not that there is literally no intelligible account of it. The intended denial is of a quiddity that would be **ontologically prior** to, or dependent upon something distinct for, its existence.
>
> Conversely, if one treats existence and quiddity as *identical in everything*, the familiar distinction between affirming that a thing exists and apprehending what it is becomes obscure. The lower text therefore warns against conflating an external unity with the claim that there can be no conceptual diversity. [The lower discussion continues on I:42; some of these sentences are an argumentative rendering rather than an exhaustive literal proof.]

---

## I:42 — The Necessary's existence and the possible's quiddity

**Upper register — direct translation of the visible clauses**

> [If a real, additional existence were itself possible and needed another cause,] the supposition of its possibility would require an agent, and the absurd consequence would follow. **But existence, according to those who say that it is additional [to quiddity], is a matter of intellectual consideration** (*amr iʿtibārī*) and does not require [a second] existent thing. There is therefore no difficulty [of this kind].
>
> **“Subsisting by itself, distinct from what is other than it, and different in reality from the existence of the possible; therefore …”** That is, because of the difference in reality between these two existences. **“Despite its being singular in”**—that is, the existence of the possible—**“subsisting in quiddity.”** It is not intended that being abstracted from quiddity and subsisting by itself occurs simply *through its own essence*; for then every existence would be abstracted [from quiddity], since what is entailed by a thing's essence cannot differ from it. Thus the existence of the possible would also be abstracted [from quiddity], and that is false, since the quiddity of the possible, considered in itself, admits nonexistence, and possibility is not removed from it.
>
> **“But as for the distinction [of the Necessary]”**—its abstraction is therefore a mark distinguishing the existence of the One from [that of the possible]. [The final compressed expression about individuation or distinction needs another reading; the principal argument is preserved above.]

**Visible Arabic anchors:** «لكن الوجود عند القائلين بزيادته أمر اعتباري لا يحتاج إلى موجود فلا إشكال»; «قائم بنفسه مغاير لغيره مخالف لوجود الممكن في حقيقته»; «فلا يراد أن تجرد الوجود عن الماهية وقيامه بذاته ...»; «لأن ماهية الممكن من حيث هي تقبل العدم ولا يرتفع الإمكان عنها».

**Lower register — interpretive argument map, NOT a completed translation**

> If we begin from the fact that the possible needs a cause for existence, and then imagine existence as an additional, independently existent item, a problem arises about the cause of that item. But the intellect can consider the existence of the possible while distinguishing it from its quiddity, without thereby multiplying external entities. This is precisely what one must keep in view when the terms “additional” and “inherent” occur in the argument.
>
> The author of the lower discussion maintains that the real philosophical disagreement is not over whether the mind can frame two concepts; that is generally admitted. It concerns **what grounds their distinction** and whether existence is an extramental accident or is the very actuality of the possible thing. One can affirm conceptual distinction without settling the deeper ontological issue by that fact alone.
>
> A further objection is directed against the account that the existence of the Necessary is an existence *divested of every quiddity*. If all intelligible description were removed, one would also lose the means of differentiating the Necessary from possible existence. It is important, therefore, that negating an additional quiddity is not construed as negating every possible determination of thought about the Necessary.

**Caution:** This leaf's lower text is especially dense. The paragraphs above follow its major argumentative transitions but **do not constitute a complete literal translation**. The missing lines have not been counted or supplemented from secondary scholastic accounts.

---

## I:43 — The common concept of existence, and the unique reality of the Necessary

**Upper register — the special existence of the Necessary**

> [If the supposed abstraction or independence applied to the possible's existence in the same manner,] **it would not be necessary**. It is not intended here to argue that existence is known necessarily, whereas the reality of the Necessary is, by agreement, unknown. For the inquiry is **not about existence shared in meaning** (*al-wujūd al-mushtarak*), but about **the special existence which differs in reality from the other existences**. The [existence] described as separate [from quiddity] is the second, whereas what is known necessarily is the first.
>
> What is meant by **the subsistence of existence in the quiddity of the possible** is its subsistence in it **in the mind, not as a distinct extramental thing**. For no quiddity is established outside the mind in such a way that existence could stand in it [as a separately existing accident]. **“Like whiteness in a body”**—[this comparison is introduced and then qualified:] in the case of a body we are not claiming that the body's quiddity stands there before its existence, so that existence then arrives in it. The discourse thus passes to the meaning of *subsistence* and of the addition of existence, a matter to be considered apart from an image of two independently existing things.
>
> **“For the point of our statement that the existence of every thing is additional to it …”** [The sentence is completed on I:44.]

**Visible Arabic anchors:** «لأن البحث ليس في الوجود المشترك بل في الوجود الخاص المخالف لسائر الوجودات في الحقيقة»; «والمجرد هو الثاني والمعلوم ضرورة هو الأول»; «والمراد بقيام الوجود بالماهية في الممكن قيامه بها ذهناً لا عيناً»; «كبياض الجسم»; «لأن القصد بأن وجود كل شيء زائد عليه».

**Separate short marginal notice**

> On **“otherwise that would be impossible”**: [A possible counterreading is rejected] because the [Necessary's] identity is not a quiddity independently established outside the mind. Its standing outside the mind is not a second addition to an already independently existing nature. [One clause about *hudūth* and the philosophers' standpoint is compressed.]

**Long lower note — selected literal clauses and editorial connective reading (NOT a full translation)**

> I say: it seems that, by **the subsistence of whiteness in a body outside the mind**, the disputant intends a sense of *being in another* that concerns the body's being qualified by whiteness. After that, whiteness is not in the external world as a separately existing thing detached from the body. Nonetheless the body and its whiteness are spoken of by two expressions, each referring to what is cognised under a distinct consideration.
>
> We should distinguish what is **one externally** from what is **one in concept**. The fact that two concepts can be formed—body and whiteness, or quiddity and existence—does not by itself show that two independent items stand outside the mind. Nor does the fact that one speaks of a thing as existing settle how its existence relates to what it is.
>
> The opposing account says that, if quiddity and existence are two real extramental items, then one must consider the existence of each in turn; this threatens a further question about the existence of the existence. The reply invokes a difference between existence as the actuality of a thing and existence treated as if it were another quiddity in need of instantiation. [The writer carries this response onto I:44 and its remaining clauses have not been fully read.]

**Status:** The upper leaf is rendered as an incomplete source-aligned draft because of compressed wording and continuations. The lower note contains a securely identified analogy of **whiteness and body** but the remainder is not yet a certified word-for-word translation.

## I:44 — Existence predicated by gradation (*tashkīk*)

**Working translation from the original Arabic scan, printed I:44 = physical PDF page 57.** Upper text includes parenthesised Taftāzānī phrases; the lower gloss is of unverified authorship. This continues directly from I:43.

## Upper register — source-based translation

> Moreover, the **quiddity which is said to receive [existence]** would either be nonexistent—in which case there is a contradiction—or existent—in which case circularity or an infinite regress follows. That is because the addition of existence to quiddity and its subsistence in it are **according to intellectual consideration, not as two independent extramental realities**. It is not like the inherence of whiteness in a body.
>
> **“Sharing with it”:** that is, the existence of the Necessary shares with the existence of the possible **“an accident”**—namely the concept of **“being”** (*kawn*), meaning establishment or actuality. This is the common notion of existence **“predicated of [the particular] existences by gradation”** (*bi-l-tashkīk*). For [existence] is prior in the cause to [existence] in the effect; in a substance it is more properly predicated than in an accident; and in something stable or enduring it is stronger than in something nonenduring, such as motion.
>
> It is thus a predicate truly affirmed of quiddity and its constituents. But these gradations are not being offered as though the Necessary's very reality were an extra-mental accident inhering in a distinct quiddity. [The last phrase joins the continuation on I:45.]

**Anchors read from the scan:** «وأيضاً الماهية المعروضة إما معدومة فيتناقض أو موجودة فيدور أو يتسلسل»; «وذلك لأن زيادة الوجود على الماهية وقيامه بها إنما هو بحسب العقل دون الخارج»; «وليس كقيام البياض بالجسم»; «الكون»; «المقول على الوجودات الخاصة بالتشكيك»; «لأنه في العلة أقدم منه في المعلول وفي الجوهر أولى منه في العرض وفي القار أشد منه في غير القار كالحركة».

## Lower register — selected direct argument, not yet a full collation

> [Continuing the earlier comparison:] We have said that the relation of **whiteness to a body** differs from the relation of **existence to quiddity**. Here, too, the difference between the two pictures must be considered. The body qualified by whiteness already exists [as a body]; its possessing whiteness is a different determination. But when one speaks of existence's subsisting in quiddity, the quiddity is not assumed to stand outside the mind as already existent prior to the existence being predicated of it.
>
> Suppose the quiddity whose existence is in question were already **existent**: that makes the new existence superfluous and requires an explanation of the former existence. If it were **nonexistent**, there would be no independently existing subject in which the alleged extramental existence could inhere. This is why the dispute turns on the meaning of “additional” and “subsisting,” not merely on whether we can frame two concepts.
>
> An objection nevertheless remains: in saying that the existence of the Necessary is identical with its reality, are we asserting that it possesses the perfection of being through itself, or that its being is itself without an additional determining factor? The lower writer distinguishes this from the existence of a human being, which becomes actual through factors not identical with its quiddity. [The long lower passage contains a further polemical disagreement about the commentator's reading of *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid*. Those lines remain uncollated.]

**Status:** Upper is a connected direct-source first pass, with a compressed final join to I:45. The lower represents only directly recoverable portions of a longer text; it is not a completed all-register translation. No manuscript comparison or independent certification has been performed.

## Continuation instruction

Resume the upper printed argument at I:45, keeping the physical PDF offset p+13. On I:34–44, **finish** the bracketed, uncollated lower passages and check every word of the upper text against the Arabic rather than assuming the existing English is final. Do not treat any of these page positions as a completed all-register critical translation.

# NEW DIRECT-SOURCE CONTINUATION: I:45–54

## I:45 — Particular existences and the proposed accident of being
*Physical PDF 58. Upper: connected first-pass translation. Lower: substantial selected translation, not the complete lower leaf.*

### Upper: continuous reading

[Continuing the discussion of] its individuals. Thus his statement that [being] is “predicated of the existences” is made on the view that *kawn* (being) is an accident. But it does not follow from this that the particular existences are numerous, since an accident's multiplicity does not entail the multiplicity of its subject. Each of the particular existences is, rather, said to subsist in itself, since it is a reality different from the other existences.

Nevertheless, it is evident that if the particular existence of each thing means its *establishment* (*thubūt*), then holding that *kawn* inheres in it would identify accident and subject: the distinction would be solely that between unrestricted and restricted consideration. Such a distinction does not constitute genuine inherence. If, conversely, the thing's particular existence is something beyond establishment, *kawn* cannot properly be made to mean establishment when predicated of it. This makes clear the weakness of the comparison, “as light [is predicated] of lights”: by this they mean that … [the example is completed on I:46].

### Lower commentary — unassigned, selected passages in source order

**On “in the sense of establishment.”** The obvious interpretation would be that this predicate is borne by them only with difficulty; otherwise “existence” would become purely equivocal in expression. One might reply that the predication of *kawn* of the existences is merely derivational, without thereby multiplying [one ultimate] existence. [The final qualification of this brief note is compressed.]

**On “each of the particular existences.”** What is established—that things share the concept of existence—does not require their particular instances to be mutually identical. Animal kinds, for example, share the meaning of animality while differing in specific reality. Yet they do not, merely by virtue of that sharing, constitute one and the same individual animal.

**On “but it does not follow.”** The intention may be to say that absolute existence is predicated of the differing particular existences by a predicate appropriate to their various individual realities. But if existence is asserted of those realities in the sense of their *establishment*, then the same question returns: what distinct reality is supposed to inhere in what other reality? If the absolute is no more than an unrestricted way of considering what is otherwise particular, that conceptual difference does not furnish an accident with a distinct extramental subject.

One should distinguish a universal considered simply as a common notion from a universal predicated of many individuals. What is shared *in meaning* need not be a numerical thing shared among the individuals. Conversely, what belongs to many individuals in respect of a common concept is not therefore literally present as one thing among them. [The author develops this point with an example of animality; some small-type clauses remain to be checked.]

**On “they did not rightly make *kawn* mean establishment.”** The marginal writer returns to the proposed analogy of light. What is common to different lights is one general meaning, but the lights themselves may differ in their specific realities. Whether the general meaning is itself an additional extramental accident cannot be assumed from that analogy. The next page completes the example.

**Selected Arabic anchors:** «قوله المقول على الوجودات» · «لأن زيادة العارض لا تستلزم زيادة المعروض» · «أن الوجود الخاص لكل شيء إن كان عبارة عن ثبوته» · «كالنور على الأنوار».

## I:46 — Light and lights; the attributed identity of existence and quiddity
*Physical PDF 59. Upper: connected first-pass translation. Lower: extended selected argumentative reading.*

### Upper

The light of the sun, the light of the moon, and the light of a lamp are different in their realities while participating in the accident of *light in the unrestricted sense*. This [alleged identity] holds “in all”—that is, in the existence of both Necessary and possible beings. [The position is that existence is] “the very quiddity,” as attributed to Shaykh al-Ashʿarī—may God have mercy on him. It appears to be for this reason that he said: “The nonexistent is not a thing.”

### Lower commentary — unassigned, selected passages in source order

The particular existences are said to exist through a reality belonging to them, for existence in the real sense is the effect of the agent in possible things, and in the Necessary it is identical with the essence. Applying the word to the *kawn* said to be an accident is, on this reading, figurative: the mind abstracts an aspect from that which is existence in the proper sense. Existence is what constitutes the principle of effects; a merely abstracted consideration does not become that principle simply because it is called existence. There remains, however, a point requiring examination here.

**On “as light is predicated of lights.”** Perhaps the light common to these lights is to be understood in the manner of a derivative predicate, as *kawn* is said to be predicated of existences; then it would not follow that an additional common luminosity must be a distinct extramental reality. But if one accepts a genuinely common accident, one must explain how its identity and the differences among the particular lights are related.

**On “the accident of unrestricted light.”** Those who hold that the reality of existence is the very principle of effects and those who treat *kawn* as a concept abstracted by the intellect have different objects in mind. The present controversy concerns whether the existence which they say is added to quiddity is itself an entity additional to the reality, or a consideration which the intellect applies to that reality. [The closing portion of the long note continues into I:47.]

**Selected Arabic anchors:** «نور الشمس ونور القمر ونور السراج مختلفة بالحقيقة» · «في الكل» · «من الواجب والممكن» · «نفس الماهية» · «المعدوم ليس بشيء».

## I:47 — The nonexistent and the distinction between mental and extramental verification
*Physical PDF 60. Upper: connected first-pass translation. Lower: selected commentary with unresolved continuation.*

### Upper

For if existence were the very quiddity, the removal of existence would remove the quiddity, and the nonexistent would be sheer negation. This is unlike the view that existence is additional to quiddity, as maintained by others, such as the Muʿtazila. On that view, the removal of existence does not entail the removal of quiddity, for the removal of an accident does not entail the removal of its subject. That is why they say that the nonexistent is *established*. Those who affirm the additional character of existence and its inherence in quiddity affirm the establishment of quiddity even when it is nonexistent, for they consider the attribution of [an accident] otherwise impossible. The claim that this inherence takes place only in the mind does not require anything more than [mental] establishment within it.

This does **not** mean that the concept of existence and the concept of a thing are the same; that would be false. Rather, the intended sense is that existence and quiddity do not “each possess their own separate realization in the external world.” That is, the thing is not one distinct extramental reality and the accident called its existence another. Nor is the intended claim that both have one shared extramental realization, since existence, in the disputed sense, has no separate extramental realization at all: there is no thing or identity apart from what is existent. [The next clause, beginning “and the two …,” moves to their intellectual distinction and is continued on I:48.]

### Lower commentary — unassigned, selected direct reading

The marginal writer notes that al-Qāḍī says in his commentary on *Lubb al-uṣūl* that many who deny that the nonexistent is a thing nonetheless hold that existence is additional to quiddity. The attribution of one doctrine cannot therefore be inferred without qualification from the other. This point deserves comparison with the sources.

**On “it has no independent realization in the external world.”** If the author intends to exclude the distinct extramental subsistence of existence as an accident, that is a more defensible claim than saying that there is absolutely no distinction between existence and quiddity. The mind's distinguishing two aspects does not itself establish two extramental entities. But it also does not decide whether existence is something additional in the precise sense at issue. The gloss challenges a slide between the proposition that one thing is *conceived* in more than one way and the claim that there are two *realities* externally conjoined.

**Selected Arabic anchors:** «فإن الوجود إذا كان نفس الماهية فإذا ارتفع ارتفعت» · «المعدوم ثابت» · «لا يتفرد كل من الوجود والماهية بتحقق على حدة في الخارج».

## I:48 — Four modes of existence: external, mental, verbal and written
*Physical PDF 61. Upper: connected first-pass translation. Lower: substantial selected passages.*

### Upper

I say: I did **not** intend by “existence” the relational meaning which has no realization in the external world. I intended that which is the *principle of effects*, namely the actual identity [of a thing]. There is no doubt that, in this sense, it is the very thing itself, as should be evident.

Then existence is divided into the *external* and the *mental*. External existence is existence in the primary sense, that by which the essence of the thing is realized externally—or, rather, it is its very realization. Mental existence is not primary existence in that way: it stands to external existence as a shadow stands to the body. For what is actualized in the mind is a form corresponding to the thing, such that, were that form actualized externally, it would be that thing; just as, were the shadow of a body itself to become a body, it would be that body [in the comparison being proposed].

[Existence in the mind is described as] genuine with respect to the thing's essence and reality, while the division into *verbal* and *written* existence is figurative with respect to that essence and reality. For what exists in an utterance or inscription of “human,” for example, is not the *individual person* [who exists] externally, nor the *quiddity* [represented] in the mind. Rather, what exists in speech is … [completed on I:49].

### Lower commentary — unassigned, selected argument

**On “I say …”.** The writer acknowledges that it is quite possible to intend an *iʿtibārī*, considered or abstracted, meaning by the expression “existence.” But if the argument establishes the actuality that serves as a principle of effects, one must not immediately transfer what holds of that actuality to a merely mental consideration. The principle of effects is not made into a distinct inhering accident merely because the intellect distinguishes it conceptually.

**On “if it were actualized externally.”** What is represented in the intellect can be described according to the thing's quiddity without itself constituting another external instance of the same thing. The description of the mental form as a “shadow” is intended to mark its difference of existential manner, not to imply that a person, when thought of, literally becomes a second person subsisting in the intellect. The issue therefore turns on which sense of existence is invoked in the proposition.

**On “in the utterance or inscription.”** If the name “human” is pronounced or written, the external occurrence is the sound or the written marks, not the person or the nature signified. The relation between sign and signified licenses an attribution of existence to the signified “in words” or “in writing,” but that attribution is a different one from the existence of the thing in reality or of its intelligible form in the intellect. [The lower note develops this point through the distinction between signifier and signified across I:49–50.]

**Selected Arabic anchors:** «أقول أنا لم أرد بالوجود المعنى النسبي» · «ما هو مبدأ الآثار وهو الهوية» · «ثم الوجود منقسم إلى ... الخارجي» · «وإلى الذهني» · «وإلى اللفظي والخطي مجازا».

## I:49 — The name and its written form; why a mental existent is needed
*Physical PDF 62. Upper: connected first-pass translation, ending continues to I:50. Lower: selected commentary.*

### Upper

…“the name,” and in writing its inscribed **form**. If the word imposed as a sign for a person is considered in relation to that person, the expression “existence in speech” belongs to this relation of designation, not the person's literal presence in the sound. [A brief compressed qualification is left unresolved.]

As for the proof of **mental existence**: we make affirmative judgments concerning things that have no realization in the external world, while the judgment must have a subject in some sense. Moreover, [the object of knowledge can be] something in the mind despite the absence of the corresponding thing externally. The argument is developed through affirmations about nonexistent subjects and through universal concepts. [It continues explicitly on I:50.]

### Lower commentary — unassigned, selected argument

**On “yes, if the expression were attached to the designated term.”** The gloss warns against treating the attribution of existence to a verbal expression as a discovery of a new reality of the thing outside thought. The word and what it signifies must be distinguished. A name may occur in a sentence or be inscribed on paper even though its referent does not exist externally.

There is likewise a distinction between the *meaning* of the term and the *image or form* of that meaning in the mind. Philosophers and logicians sometimes designate as “signified” the mental image obtained from the sensible or intelligible thing. Yet the signified is not, simply by being signified, numerically identical to the external individual. The discussion requires attention to which kind of signification is meant.

The gloss draws the familiar distinctions among the signifying expression, the mental form to which the expression leads, and the thing or reality of which that form is a form. One must not collapse them merely because language permits a predication from one to another. A spoken term's signifying something is not sufficient to confer external actuality upon the thing signified.

**Selected Arabic anchors:** «في اللفظ ... الاسم» · «وفي الخط صورته» · «والدليل على الوجود الذهني».

## I:50 — Affirmative judgments about nonexistent things
*Physical PDF 63. Upper: connected first-pass translation. Lower: selected argument.*

### Upper

[An object of thought need not be] realized externally. If a thing is known, an object of cognition must be present in the mind in the sense required for the judgment; otherwise the alleged cognition would have nothing to be about. This is why affirmative judgments are made **concerning things nonexistent externally**, even when the subject cannot be found there.

For an affirmative judgment about a thing—for example, “the impossible is more specific than the nonexistent”—requires the thing to be conceived. Likewise, among concepts we find a **universal concept**, which cannot, under its universal aspect, exist as such in the external world; the act of understanding it is an intellectual apprehension. There are also **real propositions** (*qaḍāyā ḥaqīqiyya*) whose subjects do not exist externally at all, such as “Every phoenix is an animal.” Their truth does not require that an actual phoenix be found outside the intellect. [The analysis of these judgments and the inference to mental existence continues on I:51.]

### Lower commentary — unassigned, selected passages

**On the argument from affirmative judgments.** A judgment's requiring a conceived subject does not without further argument settle the mode in which that subject exists. It may require a concept in the intellect, but that is precisely what must be differentiated from a real member of the thing's kind. To say that a nonexistent object is represented is not to declare that it exists in the external world.

The gloss asks whether “mental existence” is meant to be the existence of an entity actually instantiating the represented quiddity, or simply the occurrence of that object's *form* in the intellect. The familiar distinction between the image and its external object is indispensable here. A universal is not externally instantiated *as universal* merely because the mind predicates it of many particulars.

**Selected Arabic anchors:** «أن نحكم ... على الممتنعات في الخارج» · «كقولنا الممتنع أخص من المعدوم» · «ونجده أيضا من المفهومات ... كليا» · «كل عنقاء حيوان».

## I:51 — Mental existence and the requirements of judgment
*Physical PDF 64. Upper: connected first-pass translation, lower: selected.*

### Upper

Consider a proposition such as “Every body is originated.” The judgment requires the subject about which it is made to be conceived, even where that subject is not actually found in the external world.

“Intellection,” understanding, and the distinction made by the intellect concern an intelligible object. If the object were not present to the intellect in some manner, the intended act of intellection would not occur. Hence the act of understanding requires a relation—or *addition* in the sense of a relational consideration—between knower and known, between the intellect and the intelligible. A relation cannot be conceived while both of its termini are conceived as sheer nothingness. What is intended here is therefore the intelligible's **being present in the mind**, not the claim that the thing is established as another independent external object.

Yet one should distinguish a relation required for the *act of understanding* from a real constituent added to the substance of the mind. The defender of mental existence argues for the former; the opponent disputes whether the mental object must be said to exist in order for that relation to obtain. [The compressed clauses at the end of the upper register lead into the objection on I:52.]

### Lower commentary — unassigned, selected argument

The writer considers whether conceiving the nonexistent requires that the conceived quiddity itself be a distinct existent in the soul. The conclusion is not immediate. The act of conceiving and what is conceived may be distinguished as intellect and intelligible in one respect, while an additional independent entity is not thereby established. Here again the notion of an *addition* may be conceptual rather than substantial.

**Selected Arabic anchors:** «فالتعقل والفهم والتميز عند العقل» · «أن كان المعقول ...» · «لا يقتضي ... الإضافة» · «في الذهن».

## I:52 — Why thinking of heat does not make the thinker hot
*Physical PDF 65. Upper: connected first-pass translation. Lower: selected.*

### Upper

A further objection arises. If the intelligible existed in the mind, would the mind not have to be qualified by the accidents it conceives—including mutually opposing accidents—and would that not entail the external existence of what is impossible?

We answer: a thing's existence in the mind is the existence of its **representation** or **shadow**, not its original, extramentally effective existence. **Mental existence does not entail that the mind is qualified by the accidents conceived.** The qualification in question belongs to the original existence to which effects accrue. A person may form a concept of unbelief without becoming an unbeliever merely through that representation; similarly the contemplation of opposed qualities does not require the mind to be simultaneously hot and cold.

Nor does conceiving an impossible object confer external being upon it. The mind's having a form which signifies it is not the impossible thing's actually being present in the external world. The verbal comparison with water in a vessel or house is intended to mark the different sense of “in,” not to make mental containment identical with spatial containment. [The author continues into I:53.]

### Lower commentary — unassigned, selected argument

A proposed objection is that if the intellect bears forms corresponding to nonexistent, impossible and mutually incompatible things, the intellect seems to be a subject for contradictory dispositions. The answer requires a distinction between the *form through which the object is apprehended* and the form which would physically inhere in a bearer of that quality. Being informed of heat or of colour as an intelligible does not have the causal character of being physically heated or coloured.

The gloss further examines whether it is legitimate to describe these intelligibles as existing in the mind at all, if all that is meant is a way of considering an object rather than the subsistence of an object additional to the mind. The question is not settled merely by changing the preposition “in” from a local to an intentional use.

**Selected Arabic anchors:** «لو كان المعقول موجودا في الذهن لزم اتصاف الذهن بالأعراض المتصورة حتى المتضادات» · «وهو وجود غير متأصل» · «لا يقتضي الاتصاف» · «كالمؤمن يتصور الكفر فإنه لا يتصف بالكفر بمجرد ذلك».

## I:53 — The common meaning of existence and the identity of the mental form
*Physical PDF 66. Upper: first-pass translation. Lower: selected.*

### Upper

This is the difference between **mental existence** and existence attributed to words. The mental form is genuinely realized as a form, though those who speak of mental existence describe the form and the thing of which it is a form as united [in cognition]. Those who deny it do not accept that union; for them what occurs in the intellect is only its exemplar or likeness. The difference is not merely that the expression is pronounced or written.

Then, “what is predicated as existence and as thinghood is nothing but **establishment**.” For everything established is a thing and is existent, and conversely. “What is predicated as nonexistence is nothing but **negation**.” Thus everything nonexistent is negated, and conversely. [The concluding qualification crosses to I:54.]

### Lower commentary — unassigned, selected argument

The gloss asks whether both parties use “mental existence” in the same sense. An image may obtain in the mind even for those who deny that the nature represented exists there in the manner of its object. Hence agreement about the presence of a form does not amount to agreement about the ontological status of the nature represented.

The distinction also turns on whether one takes the mental form to be the very reality of the object *under the aspect of being known*, or an exemplar distinct from it. The controversy cannot be resolved by invoking the mere fact that some image appears to the mind.

**Selected Arabic anchors:** «الاختلاف في الوجود الذهني واللفظي» · «باتحاد الصورة وذي الصورة» · «ليس إلا الثبوت» · «ليس إلا النفي».

## I:54 — The nonexistent, negation, and the disputed “intermediate”
*Physical PDF 67. Upper: connected first-pass translation where legible; several conditional alternatives in this long passage require a closer collation. Lower: selected.*

### Upper

[The nonexistent is not] a thing or something established. “Thus the nonexistent is neither a thing nor established.” The nonexistent *in the external world* may indeed be something about which one makes a judgment in the mind, since those [under discussion] affirm mental existence. It is not for that reason established as an external thing.

There is “no intermediary between what is established and what is negated”—that is, between the existent and the nonexistent, or between existence and nonexistence. **This is the position attributed here to the philosophers and the majority of the theologians.**

“Among them are those who affirm it”: that is, who call the nonexistent a *thing* and *established*, because the expression “that which …” can be applied to it and because [something can be] spoken of even when it is not externally realized. They appeal in this connection to usage and to the possibility of making meaningful assertions.

Others attempt to establish an intermediary as a “state” (*ḥāl*). The discussion distinguishes something wholly negated, which has no realization in itself, from a case in which one can meaningfully ascribe a determination without treating it as an independent existent alongside substance or accident. [The classification branches into conditional alternatives, some of which are compressed in the print; these are **not** silently reconstructed as a complete argument.]

### Lower commentary — unassigned, selected argument, not a full literal translation

The lower discussion cautions that accepting an intermediate *state* need not mean treating it as a further self-subsisting entity. It disputes what exactly follows from applying words such as *existent*, *nonexistent*, *established* and *negated* to a subject. If one says that a state “is neither existent nor nonexistent,” it must be established whether this is a new ontological division or a technical determination of the terms. The polemic also calls attention to the difference between a thing's not being present in external reality and its being meaningful as the subject of a proposition.

The small-type text invokes the views of theologians who speak of the *aḥwāl* and those who reject them. Its detailed subdivisions and the final reply remain **untranslated pending literal source collation**; no school or named author is assigned to every position here.

**Selected Arabic anchors:** «المعدوم ليس بشيء ولا ثابت» · «لا واسطة بين الثابت والمنفي» · «وبين الوجود والعدم» · «هذا ما ذهب إليه الحكماء وجمهور المتكلمين» · «ومنهم من أثبتها» · «ويسمى الواسطة».

## Exact scope and omissions

- **New original-page positions read:** I:45–54, exactly ten, physical PDF pages 58–67. Each has a connected first-pass upper passage; text in brackets is a supplied join or a local uncertainty.
- **Lower original text:** selected passages translated and explicitly assigned to the lower register on each page, but a complete clause-by-clause reading of those long glosses **has not been achieved**. No individual writer is positively identified here.
- **Arabic:** anchor phrases only; no page has a complete typed/diplomatic Arabic transcription in this new block.
- **Independently critically certified pages added:** **zero**. No source-page count is advanced as a completed all-register critical translation.
- **Continuation:** I:55 starts with the explanation of how the supposed intermediary and the negation of a nonexistence relate to the claim that the nonexistent is neither a thing nor established. This is the next upper span; the pending lower-text collation on I:45–54 is a separate obligation.
