# NEW SOURCE-READING CONTINUATION: I:55–68

## I:55 — Is the alleged intermediary itself existent or nonexistent?
*Original PDF physical page 68. Upper: directly translated first-pass passage; lower: selected portions only.*

### Upper register — Taftāzānī lemmata and Sanandajī commentary

[The claim concerns an intermediary] **between the existent and the nonexistent**, called a *state* (*ḥāl*). The objection against it is that one may **make existence an attribute of it**. If the existence [of that attribute] were itself to exist, an infinite regress of existences would follow, or an infinity of items would have to come together in a single existence; and that is impossible.

**“Or [it would be] unqualified by the contradictory”**—or by what has its contradictory predicated of it. For, on the supposition that there is an intermediary, nonexistence is more restricted than the contradictory of existence, even if the contradictory is called *non-existence*; and this, too, is impossible.

**“We replied”**: We choose the alternative that [the alleged intermediary] exists; but the regress does not follow, **because its existence is itself**. For every description attaching to *another* is additional to that other, whereas, if it is established for the thing itself, it is not an item added to it—just as nonexistence [is not a distinct additional item in the relevant comparison]. [The sentence continues on I:56.]

**Arabic checkpoints:** «بين الموجود والمعدوم حالا» · «يجعل الوجود صفة له» · «لزم اجتماع أمور غير متناهية في وجود واحد» · «ولو عدم اتصافه بالنقيض» · «نختار أنه موجود ولكن لا يلزم التسلسل» · «لأن وجوده عينه».

### Unassigned lower register — provisional interpretation of selected arguments

On the alternative, **“or what the contradictory applies to,”** the gloss distinguishes the *contradictory* of a description from what may properly be described by the contradictory. Where nonexistence is introduced as an intermediary, one cannot assume without argument that anything which is not existence must already be an instance of nonexistence in exactly the disputant's restricted sense. Otherwise the interpretation of *being qualified* in the two cases shifts unnoticed.

On **“and nonexistence,”** the writer insists that the nonexistent may be correctly described in a respect in which *existence* cannot be predicated of it. The reply cannot simply infer the existence of a subject from a statement made about what is nonexistent. [The longer discussion of the two senses of predication is not exhaustively translated.]

A separately ruled lower passage opens on the distinction between **the existent and the nonexistent**. It says that the putative *state* is being treated as a description that is neither existent nor nonexistent *in itself*; its classification cannot be resolved merely by placing it beside common accidents such as blackness and whiteness. The passage continues on I:56, and its author's individual identity is not established.

**Untranslated / uncertain:** The detailed grammatical criticism of *ittiṣāf* in the upper half of the lower register; final words continuing after «الوجود نقيضه»; the remaining ruled lower paragraph.

## I:56 — What exactly is the contradictory of existence?
*Original PDF physical page 69. Upper: connected first pass, ending at a page break; lower: selected response to the mediation claim.*

### Upper register

[The comparison includes] occurrence, unity, plurality, and things of that kind. **“Or [one may reply]”** by choosing the alternative that it is nonexistent: in this case it is not qualified by the contradictory [in the opponent's intended sense], but by *nonexistence*. For **“its contradictory is nonexistence (*ʿadam*), not the nonexistent (*maʿdūm*).”** If, however, *being qualified* is intended in its derivative, grammatical sense, [the writer reopens the difficulty]: every description which subsists in something is an individual [instance of that description]. The following words complete this objection on I:57.

**Arabic checkpoints:** «والحدوث والوحدة والكثرة إلى غير ذلك» · «قد يجاب باختيار أنه معدوم» · «ولا اتصاف بالنقيض بل بالمعدوم» · «نقيضه العدم لا المعدوم» · «ولو أريد الاتصاف بطريق الاشتقاق».

### Unassigned lower register — selected connected passage

The lower writer observes that, according to those who affirm an intermediary, **nonexistence does not simply equal the unrestricted negation of existence**. They introduce an intermediary in addition to existence and nonexistence. In their usage, therefore, nonexistence may be made more restricted than *non-existence* in the unrestricted contradictory sense. If everything that failed to be existent were immediately classed as nonexistent, there would be no room for the proposed intermediary at all.

A difficulty follows. If the intermediary is itself neither existent nor nonexistent, what is the status of the statement by which one qualifies it? Calling it neither one nor the other will seem to require that the familiar contradiction has somehow been escaped. But if the qualification is said to be only conceptual, the opponent can ask whether the intermediary is thereby reduced to a consideration in the intellect rather than a distinct established category.

The gloss records the answer that *being qualified* may be used for what the intellect judges or attributes, even when there is no independently existent accident inhering in a subject. This move requires an account of what the predicate signifies and cannot be accepted as a second external entity merely because it is stated in a proposition. [The closing reply is incomplete in the source reading.]

**Untranslated / uncertain:** Remaining lower refutations, including the discussion of whether the two meanings of *negation* have been held constant.

## I:57 — Are nonexistents distinguishable? Does possibility imply establishment?
*Original PDF physical page 70. Upper: connected first pass; lower: two visibly distinct streams, one short and one extended.*

### Upper register

[The argument involves] particulars of its contradictory, just as blackness inhering in a body is one of the descriptions of that body. The body's being qualified by blackness does not entail that the body is itself blackness or that it is a non-body. Thus there is nothing strange, on the opponent's reading, in a particular way of speaking which describes existence as nonexistent [*in another respect*].

**“They said”**—that is, those who count the nonexistent as a *thing* and as *established*—**“nonexistent things are distinguishable from one another.”** Some are objects of power, others are not; some may be represented and conceived, while others cannot be brought into the intended kind of representation. **“And differentiation cannot be intelligible without establishment.”** That is their inference.

They also maintain that **“every possible thing is established, because possibility is an affirmative attribute.”** A possible thing is described as possible, and what receives such a description must, they argue, have establishment. The distinction between the possibility belonging to something and its not possessing possibility leads into the next leaf. [End of this printed upper span.]

**Arabic checkpoints:** «المعدومات متمايزة» · «بعضها عن بعض» · «بعضها مقدور» · «وبعضها غير المقدور» · «ولا يعقل التميز دون الثبوت» · «وكل ممكن ثابت لأن الإمكان وصف ثبوتي».

### Unassigned lower register — first, shorter ruled note

On the comparison with blackness and a body: **being qualified by nonexistence would imply that the existent in question was not existent**. But being qualified by blackness does not make a body a different sort of thing, still less reduce it to a non-body. Thus the analogy cannot simply be admitted; the alleged cases of qualification are not of the same form. [The note closes with an abbreviation but no positive identification of its writer.]

### Unassigned lower register — opening of the longer note

On **“that the body is a non-body”**: the glossator asks whether the example is intended to deny a body's own corporeal reality or merely to say that a description can be negated of a subject without denying the subject itself. The first would be inadmissible. But, he continues, it does not follow that every expression “existence is qualified by nonexistence” should be construed as the existence of a contrary accident. What the negation is applied to, and what precisely is taken to be negated, must be distinguished. The writer refers to a more extended earlier treatment and proceeds to the argument about possible things on I:58.

**Untranslated / uncertain:** Middle of the extended note on the different senses of *maʿdūm* and the rebuttal to the claim that possible nonexistents are established.

## I:58 — Possibility as an affirmative description
*Original PDF physical page 71. Upper: first-pass translation; long lower reply only selectively translated.*

### Upper register

[The distinction at issue concerns] the possible *in itself* in relation to a given thing. A version of the *Talḵīṣ* is cited for the expression **“the distinction between the establishment of its possibility and its not having possibility.”** The point is that there can be a difference between possibility being established for a thing and possibility being denied of it. [The exact scope of the negation in the printed expression requires reinspection; this is not used to supply an additional argument.]

It does not follow that, for a possible thing to be possible, possibility itself must be an independently existent entity in the possible thing. The challenge turns upon whether this possibility is being treated as an affirmative attribute and what such affirmation requires. **“Its subject is established”** is the opponent's conclusion; for, they say, an affirmative description of what is not established would require the subject to have some kind of existence or establishment. This is precisely the inference contested here, and the discussion continues on I:59.

**Arabic checkpoints:** «الفرق بين ثبوت الإمكان ... ولا إمكان له» · «الإمكان ... ثبوتيا» · «ثبت موصوفه» · «لأن اتصاف غير الثابت بالثابت».

### Unassigned lower register — provisional rendering of an objection

The marginal writer proposes a distinction between the intellect's affirming *that something is possible* and the supposed external subsistence of possibility as an attribute added to that thing. To affirm its possibility is not yet to make possibility itself an independently established item. The contested claim is whether an affirmation about a nonexistent subject entails the establishment of that subject **in itself**, or merely its availability as a subject of a judgment.

The note further questions whether the difference between “possibility is established for it” and “there is no possibility for it” is a difference between two existing attributes. The contradiction can be understood as a difference between what judgments the intellect may make; it need not introduce another external intermediary. [The author's remaining distinctions concerning the object of possibility and its contradictory are not fully translated.]

## I:59 — Differentiation, contradiction, and what exists only in thought
*Original PDF physical page 72. Upper: first-pass passage; lower: selected notes.*

### Upper register

**“We say”**: if by this you intend that differentiation entails **external** establishment, we deny it. That consequence would follow only if the differentiation in question were itself external [in the required sense]. The fact that intellect distinguishes objects does not by itself establish the distinguished items outside the intellect.

Know that there are also descriptions involving mutually incompatible conditions—for example, the assembling of contradictories or a body's occupying two different places at one and the same instant. Such descriptions can be distinguished from each other and from existent things, although the combinations described are decisively impossible. If their distinguishability entailed external existence, the impossible would have to be externally realized.

The objection from **“imaginary composites”** can likewise be answered: their being distinguished in the mind is not a proof that they possess the extramental existence which the argument seeks. [The discussion then returns to the possible and its possibility.]

**“The distinction between the establishment of possibility for a thing and possibility in itself”** is a difference found by considering the proposition. On the supposition of the thing as nonexistent, the possibility is not established *in the external world* as another item simply because reason can consider it. The relevant assertion is made in intellectual consideration. [The final clause continues on I:60.]

**Arabic checkpoints:** «قلنا إن أردت أن التميز يقتضي الثبوت في الخارج فمنعوا» · «واجتماع النقيضين» · «وكون الجسم في آن واحد في حيزين» · «والتركيبات الخيالية» · «والفرق بين ثبوت الإمكان عن شيء» · «غير ثابت في الخارج أصلا بمجرد اعتبار العقل».

### Unassigned lower register — selected rebuttal

On **“it does not follow”**: the writer notes that the subject is the absolutely nonexistent, which has no external existence, not a thing that merely happens to be absent from the current situation while existing elsewhere. To make the object possible in intellectual consideration is therefore not to grant the external existence which was denied at the outset.

On **“in itself”**: if the possibility is not an established attribute inhering in an independently existent subject, it is misleading to infer a second establishment for it. The dispute partly turns on whether one is using *in itself* to mean real actuality or the difference between what can and cannot be coherently conceived. [The remainder of the lower reply is not treated as fully read.]

## I:60 — Predication, external qualification, and contradictory conditions
*Original PDF physical page 73. Upper: first-pass translation; lower: selected argument.*

### Upper register

[If the possibility] is taken as an attribute [of something in itself], the claimed consequence still does not follow merely from its negation. Your statement that **“the establishment of something for something presupposes the establishment of the thing itself”** must be qualified. It is true, for example, of the occurrence of a quality in an external subject: as whiteness belongs to a body in the external world, the subject has to be there for that mode of inherence to take place.

But the *predication* of a thing and the truth of a judgment concerning it are not invariably the same as a quality's inhering externally in an existent subject. If the discussion is about something absent or nonexistent, there may still be a meaningful judgment concerning what it would be were it to exist. The use of affirmation therefore cannot indiscriminately be transferred from external inherence to all meaningful propositions.

**“Then all existence and nonexistence would become impossible”**: this is the absurd consequence alleged by the opponent. They argue that, if contrary descriptions are asserted without fixing the sense of their predication, existence and nonexistence will be gathered together, or the impossible will be described as possible. The reply insists that the judgments are made in different respects and that the disputed inferential step has not been established.

**Arabic checkpoints:** «ثبوت شيء لشيء فرع ثبوته في نفسه» · «كبياض الجسم» · «والصدق عليه» · «اجتماع النقيضين ممتنع» · «الأوصاف الصادقة على التي بعضها ثبوتية وبعضها سلبية».

### Unassigned lower register — translated selection

A distinction must be made between a real affirmative determination, whose presence is being claimed in a subject, and a *negative* determination which rejects something without positing a further independently real accident. Calling something “nonexistent” or “not qualified in such-and-such a way” may be an instance of the second kind. The exclusion of an affirmative attribute is not itself an additional affirmative attribute of the same kind.

The gloss argues that this is the source of the contradiction alleged by the disputant: he has moved from a judgment in which absence is denied or a qualification is withheld to one in which the contrary is supposed to exist as a positive attribute. Such a move must be justified. [A long lower passage comparing possible, impossible and contrary propositions remains untranslated.]

## I:61 — Identity of subject and predicate: the conditions of informative judgment
*Original PDF physical page 74. Upper: unusually long, connected first pass; lower: selected argument.*

### Upper register

[The judgment requires] **the union of the two terms in the actual thing (*dhātan wa-huwiyyatan*)**: that what the subject-expression applies to outside [the mind], and what is denoted by the predicate-expression, are the same thing in the respect asserted. Otherwise the judgment “this is that” would not be valid, since one already knows that it does not hold of things that are entirely different in their individual identities.

But there must also be **“a difference between them in meaning.”** That is, the concept signified by the subject-expression must differ from the concept signified by the predicate-expression, so that the judgment conveys something: namely, that two descriptions different *as concepts* are united *in the thing*. One gains nothing by saying merely “the earth is earth” or “the sky is sky.” Those sentences assert what is already contained in the way the subject is presented.

One may answer that, although a subject cannot fail to be either qualified by the predicate or qualified by its contradictory, the person who conceives the subject does not thereby know *which* of the two holds. It is therefore not a contradiction to need a further judgment, nor must the predication be useless. A proposition may be **true**, corresponding to the thing, or **false** and invalid.

The truth of a judgment is not simply its correspondence to what is externally present, since its two terms may fail to be realized externally. Nor is it correspondence to whatever happens to be represented in the mind; the intellect may represent false judgments as well. Rather, the truth of a judgment is its correspondence to **what is the case in itself** (*mā fī nafs al-amr*). This expression is explained in the next leaf.

**Arabic checkpoints:** «الجهل الإيجابي إلى اتحاد الطرفين ذاتا وهوية» [reading of the introductory wording uncertain] · «تغايرهما مفهوما» · «أن هذين المتغايرين بحسب المفهوم متحدان بحسب الذات» · «الأرض أرض والسماء سماء» · «قد تكون صحيحة أي صدقا خارجيا وقد تكون فاسدة أي كذبا وباطلا» · «ليس صدق الحكم وصحته مطابقته إلى الأعيان».

### Unassigned lower register — provisional rendering of selected distinction

If the subject and predicate are said to be united in external existence, that does not by itself mean that their *concepts* are identical. The intellect may form a conception of the subject under one description and then learn something else by predicating a distinct description of it. Conversely, a mere diversity of phrases does not make a judgment genuinely informative unless the cognitions conveyed differ in the relevant way. The lower text separately considers the referents of the expressions and the way those referents are grasped in intellect. [This lower argument is not reproduced clause for clause.]

## I:62 — The meaning of *nafs al-amr*
*Original PDF physical page 75. Upper: short continuing conclusion; most of the leaf is a lengthy lower gloss.*

### Upper register

[Truth is] **correspondence to what is in the thing itself** (*mā fī nafs al-amr*). By *al-amr* is meant “the thing” and by *nafs* its “self” or essence. Its meaning is what one understands when saying, **“This matter is thus in itself”**: that is, as the thing stands in its own right, **“apart from the judgment of the one judging and the apprehension of the one apprehending.”**

**Arabic checkpoints:** «مطابقته لما في نفس الأمر» · «والمراد بالأمر الشيء وبالنفس الذات» · «ومعناه ما فهم من قولنا هذا الأمر كذا في نفسه» · «مع قطع النظر عن حكم الحاكم وإدراك المدرك».

### Unassigned lower register — selected opening of the extended note

The note questions how correspondence can be conceived in judgments whose subjects are not instantiated among external things—for example, judgments concerning merely possible, impossible, or universally considered objects. If correspondence were restricted to independently existent external objects, such judgments could not be true. The gloss therefore distinguishes **the state of affairs according to which a proposition is true** from the mere occurrence of an act of judging it or the occurrence of a particular form in someone's understanding.

The text explores whether a predicate's belonging to a subject in this sense has to be *in external existence*, or whether the correspondence may hold of an intelligible without the independently existing subject stipulated by the opponent. [The subsequent discussion of the precise meaning of self-identity and the alleged contradiction is not exhaustively translated.]

## Interim audit: I:55–62

The **eight original printed upper spans** have received new source-inspected first-pass English (with local uncertainties stated); their lower registers have only partly translated phrases and interpretive renderings, not complete translation. **Zero** of these pages is independently certified or completed in all registers. The longer source-based argument continues at I:63 with the authentic new chapter headed **فصل في الماهية**.


## I:63 — A new chapter: what quiddity answers
*Original PDF physical page 76. Authentic printed heading **فصل في الماهية**; brief upper opening, extensive lower commentary.*

### Upper register — new printed chapter

**“Chapter concerning quiddity”** (*faṣl fī l-māhiyya*). He places the inquiries into quiddity after those into existence and nonexistence, although the inquiry into existence and nonexistence presupposes something to which either can be ascribed, and therefore their consideration is posterior to quiddity in one respect. [The phrase explaining the order of presentation is compressed and requires further proof against the source.]

**“Quiddity is that by which an answer is given to the question, ‘What is it?’”** The word *māhiyya* is connected with the interrogative *mā huwa* (“what is it?”), as when someone asks after the whatness of a thing. [The following example, beginning with quantity, continues on I:64.]

**Arabic checkpoints:** «فصل في الماهية» · «أخّر مباحثها عن مباحث الوجود والعدم» · «الماهية ما به يجاب عن السؤال بما هو».

### Unassigned lower commentary — directly translated opening (continuation and remainder outstanding)

On **“Chapter concerning quiddity,”** the writer reports the substance of what is said in the *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid*: **When quiddity is considered together with its being actualized, it is called a *dhāt* and a *ḥaqīqa*.** Thus one does not say “the essence and reality of the phoenix” in precisely that actualizing sense. **When it is considered together with individuation, it is called a *huwiyya*.** The term *huwiyya* can also be applied to individuation itself or to external existence, and *dhāt* may be applied to individual instances of which a quiddity is predicated. **Moreover, a quiddity is accompanied by its accidents, whether inseparable or separable.** [End of the cited statement in the printed gloss.]

Then comes a question: **If quiddity is included among general matters, is that not incorrect?** For the general matters are states and attributes that are not confined to one particular division of existence. Quiddity, by contrast, is being treated here as the answer to the question about what a given thing is. [The ensuing objection and reply about the different meanings of *māhiyya* extend over the rest of this long lower note; a complete literal translation has not yet been made.]

**Checked Arabic in the lower note:** «قال في شرح المقاصد ما حاصله أن الماهية إذا اعتبرت مع التحقق سميت ذاتا وحقيقة» · «وإذا اعتبرت مع التشخص سميت هوية» · «وقد تطلق الهوية على التشخص أو الوجود الخارجي» · «وقد يطلق الذات على مصدقات الماهية من الأفراد» · «ثم إن الماهية تقارن جميع عوارضها لازمة أو مفارقة».

## I:64 — Quiddity taken with an accompanying condition: *al-makhlūṭa*
*Original PDF physical page 77. Upper: very short complete visible passage up to its final conjunction; lower: selected explanatory lines.*

### Upper register

[The preceding example concerns] what answers the question **“What is it?”** And the quiddity **“may be taken on condition of something”** (*bi-sharṭ shayʾ*): that is, on condition of its being accompanied by accidents. It is then **“called the mixed [quiddity]”** (*al-makhlūṭa*), because it is considered together with accidents. **“There is no obscurity in its existence”**: the existence of individual concrete things outside the mind is evident, as in Zayd and ʿAmr, who are individuals of humanity. **“And [it may be ...]”** [a second mode of consideration follows on I:65.]

**Arabic checkpoints:** «قد تؤخذ بشرط شيء» · «أي المقارنة للعوارض» · «تسمى المخلوطة» · «لخلطها مع العوارض» · «ولا خفاء في وجودها» · «وجود الأشخاص في الخارج بين كزيد وعمرو من أفراد الإنسان».

### Unassigned lower commentary — provisional interpretation of selected distinctions

The writer asks why the quiddity is divided in this fashion. A divided item should, it seems, be one of the members of the division; but the same quiddity can be considered with or without the accompanying conditions. The division therefore concerns **ways of considering one nature**, not necessarily three realities independent of one another.

The distinction can be applied both to the individual's additional properties and to attributes whose attribution follows the nature under a particular condition. The gloss observes that the question **“what is it?”** can be answered by something indicating the nature itself, whereas the individual named in the example has a quiddity accompanied by individuating characteristics. The latter are not for that reason parts of the definition of humanity. [The long subsequent dispute over the division's logical status is not translated in full.]

## I:65 — Quiddity taken on condition of no accompaniment: *al-mujarrada*
*Original PDF physical page 78. Upper: continuing first-pass translation; lower: opening technical distinction translated.*

### Upper register

[Quiddity may] **“be taken on condition that there is no thing [accompanying it]”** (*bi-sharṭ lā shayʾ*): that is, [on condition of] being free of accidents and concomitants. It is then **“called abstracted”** (*al-mujarrada*), because it has been abstracted from them. **“It has no existence in minds, let alone among external individuals.”** For existence in the mind, whether the accidents from which one abstracts are taken without restriction or restricted to **external** accidents, is itself among the accidents [that follow a quiddity]. Being in the mind is a determination belonging to the mental form; hence a quiddity stipulated to have no concomitant at all cannot, under that very stipulation, acquire mental existence. [The passage continues on I:66.]

**Arabic checkpoints:** «تؤخذ بشرط لا شيء» · «أي الخلو عن العوارض واللواحق» · «تسمى المجردة» · «ولا وجود في الأذهان فضلا عن الأعيان» · «فإن الكون في الذهن من العوارض».

### Unassigned lower commentary — directly translated opening, followed by an unverified remainder

On **“for existence is an accident”**: Know that they divide what is accidental to a thing into two divisions. **One is an accident of existence; the other is an accident of quiddity.** They also make existence [relevant here] twofold: one is what attaches to a thing **on condition of external existence**, such as blackness and whiteness; the second is what attaches to it **on condition of mental existence**, such as universality and particularity. Other concomitants of a quiddity are not all required without qualification, and their conditions of occurrence have to be considered. [The printed lower argument continues with a technical objection about which, if any, of the three ways of taking quiddity includes existence.]

**Checked Arabic in the lower note:** «واعلم أنهم قسموا العارض لشيء قسمين أحدهما عارض الوجود والآخر عارض الماهية» · «وجعلوا الوجود أيضا قسمين» · «أحدهما ما يعرض لشيء بشرط الوجود الخارجي كالسواد والبياض» · «والثاني ما يعرض بشرط الوجود الذهني كالكلية والجزئية».

**Untranslated lower remainder:** The lengthy continuation concerns absolute and restricted accidental determinations and whether *mental being* is itself a condition incompatible with quiddity taken *bi-sharṭ lā*. It has not been translated literally and is not included in this page's completed-text count.

## I:66 — Why the mental consideration does not qualify the nature in the stipulated respect
*Original PDF physical page 79. Upper: short linked translation; lower: selected passage only.*

### Upper register

[One may be taking the nature] in the mind, while **not noticing or regarding that being-in-the-mind as an accompanying accident** of it. Nevertheless, that through which the nature is actually supposed to be present in the mind has mental existence as a determination in fact, even if the one considering the nature does not direct attention to that determination.

As for **judging that [the strictly abstracted nature's] existence is impossible**, a judgment cannot be made about something without first conceiving it. That requirement to conceive the object, however, does not entail that the object has the very kind of existence which the judgment declares impossible. It may be conceived in one respect and denied existence in another; the distinction between the act of considering and what the condition excludes must be preserved. [The statement leads into I:67.]

**Arabic checkpoints:** «الذهن» · «كونه في الذهن من غير أن نعتبر الذهن عارضا له» · «وأما الحكم عليها باستحالة وجودها» · «والحكم على الشيء إلا بعد تصوره».

### Unassigned lower commentary — selected argument

On **“that one may not treat it as an accident,”** the marginal writer distinguishes an object's being conceived from the intellect's **taking its own act of conceiving as part of the object conceived**. The latter would indeed give an additional condition; the former may occur without being explicitly noticed in the concept. Yet the act of conceiving does not abolish the actual mental circumstance under which the thought takes place.

This is why, according to the objection, *negating* mental existence of a quiddity in one respect must not be confused with denying that there is a mental cognition of it at all. If the very act of thinking were incompatible with the judgment, one could not even formulate the statement that the strictly abstracted quiddity is impossible under the specified condition. [The rest of the long lower dispute is not translated clause by clause.]

## I:67 — Quiddity considered absolutely, without stipulating presence or absence of accidents
*Original PDF physical page 80. Upper: connected passage; lower: selected objection.*

### Upper register

[Accordingly, the nature] **can be considered abstracted in respect of itself** and yet, **in respect of its existence in the mind**, fall under the category of the mixed nature. It is thus possible to judge that its existence is impossible **in the respect in which it is strictly abstracted**, without denying that the quiddity is conceived under another consideration.

**“And [it] may be taken without stipulating anything”** (*lā bi-sharṭ shayʾ*)—neither stipulating its being accompanied by accidents nor stipulating its being abstracted from them. **“It is more general than the mixed [quiddity].”** The basis of the assertion is the greater extension of what is taken without this condition. The comparison is pursued on I:68.

**Arabic checkpoints:** «من حيث ذاتها مجردة ومقارنة للمخلوطة» · «ومن حيث وجودها في الذهن قسما من المخلوطة» · «فيصح الحكم عليها باستحالة الوجود من حيث ذاتها» · «وقد تؤخذ لا بشرط شيء» · «وهي أعم من المخلوطة».

### Unassigned lower commentary — selected passage

A celebrated difficulty is invoked about the abstracted nature. If one judges it possible or impossible of existence, is one taking it without a condition when making the judgment, or attaching the very condition under dispute? The two considerations must not be transferred into each other.

The writer distinguishes the quiddity itself from the consideration by which the intellect separates it from accidents, and in turn from the mental determination attaching to the *act* of abstraction. The *non-conditioned* way of taking a nature neither asserts that its accidents accompany it nor asserts that they cannot accompany it. It therefore has a different logical scope from the nature explicitly conditioned by their absence. [The extensive discussion of the two conditions and their relative generality is not completely translated.]

## I:68 — The unconditioned nature in external individuals
*Original PDF physical page 81. Upper: connected first-pass translation, cut off at the physical page break; lower: provisional rendering of opening distinction.*

### Upper register

[The nature taken without a condition is more general than] the strictly abstracted nature, for it may be predicated of what is abstracted as well as of what is qualified or restricted. The unrestricted applies to a restricted instance; that is the point of the proposed comparison.

**“It exists”**—the quiddity taken *without a condition* exists **“in the external world, because it is the very [nature of] the mixed [quiddity] there,”** **“not a part of it.”** The difference is not that two externally subsisting natures have been joined together. Rather, the unconditioned quiddity is predicated of the nature in its concrete, accompanied condition without, in the concept itself, requiring the accompanying determinations.

**“For the human who is not [externally present] as an individual”**: one must distinguish the unconditioned humanity of which the intellect forms a concept from Zayd or ʿAmr, the concrete individuals of whom it is predicated. Existence in the external world belongs to the individual; yet the nature predicated of the individual need not be taken as one separable *piece* alongside the individuating accompaniments. The proposition does not require an isolated pure humanity to subsist independently in the external world. [The last upper clause extends onto I:69.]

**Arabic checkpoints:** «المجردة لصدقها على ما هو مجرد» · «وصدق المطلق على المقيد» · «توجد الماهية لا بشرط في الخارج» · «لكونها نفسها» · «أي نفس المخلوطة في الخارج» · «لا جزءا منها» · «الإنسان الموجود من الإنسان والأفراد» [last reading provisional].

### Unassigned lower commentary — source-based opening translation

On **“because it is the very nature of the mixed [quiddity]”**, the gloss notes that the unconditioned nature should not be described simply as an externally separable part of the particular, since the particular consists of its nature together with what individuates it, not of two independently existing objects. The statement “humanity exists outside [the mind]” is true only when the reference to its actual occurrence in individual human beings is properly understood.

The writer asks whether “absolute humanity” names the nature abstracted from all accidents, or the nature considered *without* making either their presence or absence a condition. If the first meaning is intended, it would be a mistake to infer the existence of the strictly abstracted nature externally. If the second is intended, the existence of individual humans can be the basis for predicating the nature of them. The remaining lower argument is not yet translated fully.

## I:69 — The natural universal and the distinction between the nature and its mental predicate

*Original printed Vol. I:69 · primary PDF physical page 82 · source-inspected first-pass text · not independently proofread.*

### Upper printed commentary (Taftāzānī lemmata + Sanandajī, mixed)

[Continuing the discussion of what is predicated of an external individual:] The difference between the unrestricted and the restricted is [a difference] in the intellect, not something requiring two externally separate existents. **“Then, when [the nature] taken without a condition is considered as receiving the universal”**—that is, the logical universal—**“it is the natural universal.”** What exists of this in the external world is the *subject* to which universality is ascribed, namely the quiddity which is the subject of that attribution, **not the universal as an attribute** added to it. For the subject's external existence is accompanied by individuation.

A question arises: should one not then deny external existence of the subject altogether, since the universality ascribed to it is not externally present? The answer is that the subject does not exist *abstracted from accidents*: among these are universality, genericity, specificity, and similar descriptions, which as such are mental attributes and preclude the stipulated abstracted condition. Consequently, when it is said that the *natural universal exists externally*, the intended meaning requires further explanation: it concerns what the universal is predicated of, not the external existence of universality considered as a mental qualification. [This sentence is completed on I:70.]

### Original Arabic anchors (not full transcription)

الكلي الطبيعي ؛ وإنما يوجد منه المعروض ؛ دون الكلي العارض ؛ مجردا عن العوارض

### Separate lower-register commentary — selected source renderings and interpretive explanations, author unassigned

On the commentary's phrase “in the intellect, not in the external world,” the marginal writer distinguishes difference **in concept** from difference **in what a predicate is true of**. He asks whether “the mixed quiddity” denotes the qualified quiddity itself or the whole of that quiddity and its individuating accompaniments. If the second meaning is intended, then calling the unconditioned nature a *part* of the individual is different from saying that it is identical with the quiddity predicated of that individual. The sense in which “human” is predicated of Zayd cannot be decided simply by treating every accompanying determination as another independently existing part. [The remainder of the objection continues across I:70 and has not been fully translated.]

**Source-and-completeness caution:** The lower printed passage is longer than the selected English here; unresolved clauses and page joins are not silently filled. It remains a separately authored stream and is not a completed ḥāshiya translation.

## I:70 — Only the particularized nature exists outside; the intellectual universal does not

*Original printed Vol. I:70 · primary PDF physical page 83 · source-inspected first-pass text · not independently proofread.*

### Upper printed commentary (Taftāzānī lemmata + Sanandajī, mixed)

What the [logical universal] is predicated of—the nature taken without a condition—possesses existence in the external world, but that existence is attained only when individuation occurs. Therefore what is actually existent is **the mixed [quiddity], “that is, the individuals.”** The feminine pronoun in the cited wording is explained either by regard to its predicate or by reference to the quiddity under consideration.

By contrast, the nature considered **together with the accident of universality** does not exist as such externally. Neither does the compound of the nature and this mental accident—that is, the *intellectual universal*—exist externally. The explanation so far of the quiddity taken *on condition of the absence of anything* is the familiar account. **“It has also been said”**—the writer identifies Ibn Sīnā as the proponent—**“that the quiddity taken on condition of ...”** [The alternative terminological explanation continues on I:71.]

### Original Arabic anchors (not full transcription)

فالوجود حقيقة هي المخلوطة ؛ وهي الأفراد ؛ وأما المأخوذ مع عارض الكلية فلا توجد ؛ الكلي العقلي ؛ والقائل ابن سينا

### Separate lower-register commentary — selected source renderings and interpretive explanations, author unassigned

The marginal gloss summarizes the difference: the mere quiddity taken without a condition is not the logical universal, *except insofar as it is considered to be the subject of that universal*. What becomes present externally is the nature without the mental qualification of universality, and it is present in circumstances involving accidents particular to individuals. The quiddity accompanied by the **mental accident** of being universal has no such extramental existence. This also explains why the gloss insists on separating what has a predicate from that predicate itself. [Other distinctions made here regarding the sense of “individual” remain to be collated.]

**Source-and-completeness caution:** The lower printed passage is longer than the selected English here; unresolved clauses and page joins are not silently filled. It remains a separately authored stream and is not a completed ḥāshiya translation.

## I:71 — Ibn Sīnā’s alternative: matter, genus, and the three conditional considerations

*Original printed Vol. I:71 · primary PDF physical page 84 · source-inspected first-pass text · not independently proofread.*

### Upper printed commentary (Taftāzānī lemmata + Sanandajī, mixed)

**“On condition of no thing”** means, in this alternative account, that what accompanies a quiddity is considered *additional to it*: the accompanying matters are taken as outside the meaning of the quiddity. **“Then it is matter”**—a part of **“the individual”**, understood as the composite of the quiddity and what accompanies it—and **“prior to it in both modes of existence,”** extramental and mental. For a whole cannot be realized without its part. The priority intended is natural priority, not a claim that the material part exists earlier in time. On this consideration the quiddity cannot be predicated of the whole, since the condition of predicability, unity in existence, has been excluded.

For example, if *animal* is taken on condition that the rational [differentia] is not included with it, it is a part and matter for the compound of animality and rationality, and it is not predicated of that compound. If it is taken on condition that the rational is included with it, it is a *species*. If it is taken without stipulating whether something accompanies it, two considerations are possible: one may attend to its difference from what accompanies it, or to their unity. Accordingly, animal taken in the first sense is a part of the human being, prior to him in mental and extramental being, and is not predicated of him. [The corresponding species and genus cases follow on I:72.]

### Original Arabic anchors (not full transcription)

الماهية بشرط لا شيء ؛ فتكون مادة ؛ للشخص ؛ متقدمة عليه في الوجودين ؛ التقدم بالطبع ؛ الحيوان ؛ الناطق

### Separate lower-register commentary — selected source renderings and interpretive explanations, author unassigned

A short, separately ruled marginal note warns that whether the *whole* is itself externally existent has been disputed, though Ibn Sīnā and the account cited here treat a real compound as having a unity. Another marginal argument asks whether *animality* taken without rationality is literally a material constituent of an externally existing human being, or a prior item in intellectual analysis. On the first reading, predication of animality of the man is problematic; on the second, the “part” is a conceptual constituent. The note also presses the question of whether a genus can be genuinely predicated when its accompanying differentia is excluded by stipulation. [The lengthy ensuing qualifications have not been translated in full.]

**Source-and-completeness caution:** The lower printed passage is longer than the selected English here; unresolved clauses and page joins are not silently filled. It remains a separately authored stream and is not a completed ḥāshiya translation.

## I:72 — Animal as matter or species; a marginal discussion of the Avicennian account

*Original printed Vol. I:72 · primary PDF physical page 85 · source-inspected first-pass text · not independently proofread.*

### Upper printed commentary (Taftāzānī lemmata + Sanandajī, mixed)

[Continuing the example:] **“The second”**—animality taken with the relevant condition—**“is the very human being; and the third is a genus predicated of it.”** Then [the cited text turns to] **“the simple quiddity.”** [This is almost the entirety of the upper register on this leaf. Its extended lower argument must be consulted separately.]

### Original Arabic anchors (not full transcription)

والثاني نفس الإنسان ؛ والثالث جنس محمول عليه ؛ ثم الماهية البسيطة

### Separate lower-register commentary — selected source renderings and interpretive explanations, author unassigned

The marginal writer cites the explanation that the quiddity taken *without a condition* can be considered both in its difference from what accompanies it and in its unity with that accompaniment. In the first respect it can be treated as an intrinsic constituent; in the second, it may be predicated of the whole. But the gloss objects that this statement does not simply agree with the alternative account as it is expressed in another commentary.

The writer then returns to the fuller explanation attributed to Ibn Sīnā. A nature can be taken so that, if anything accompanies it, that accompaniment is regarded as outside its meaning. In such a case it cannot be predicated of the composite containing that accompaniment; it constitutes a part or *matter* of the composite. Conversely, the same nature can be taken together with an accompaniment as included in what is meant, and then the predication concerns the constituted whole. A third consideration is possible in which neither the inclusion nor the exclusion of a concomitant is required. Such a nature is able to receive further determination and may function as a genus. [This is a selected connected translation from the marginal argument on I:72–73, not its complete wording.]

**Source-and-completeness caution:** The lower printed passage is longer than the selected English here; unresolved clauses and page joins are not silently filled. It remains a separately authored stream and is not a completed ḥāshiya translation.

## I:73 — Continuation of the lower gloss: genus and species as ways of taking one nature

*Original printed Vol. I:73 · primary PDF physical page 86 · source-inspected first-pass text · not independently proofread.*

### Upper printed text

There is **no new upper printed passage** at this original page position; the page consists of a continuing lower gloss.

### Original Arabic anchors (not full transcription)

*None: no new upper text on this page.*

### Separate lower-register commentary — selected source renderings and interpretive explanations, author unassigned

The text attributed to Ibn Sīnā's *Shifāʾ* is said to have been abridged by the investigator in the commentary on the *Ishārāt*. The glossator warns that the familiar threefold distinction, as taught by later scholars, must not simply be substituted for the particular considerations expressed in that account.

If *animal* is taken in the first manner, its nature is taken independently of the rational differentia, so that animality is part of the human compound and is not predicated of that compound under the excluding condition. In the second manner, animality is taken together with rationality in the constituted whole, and what is spoken of is the human species. In the third manner, the meaning is not confined by either restriction; it is capable of being predicated of the human being and of other species that share animality. In that respect it is a genus.

On this terminology, what is called the *mixed quiddity*, taken with an accompanying condition, is the compound of the quiddity and what accompanies it and thus coincides with the existing individuals. The nature taken without stipulation is open to receiving the determinations of more than one kind; viewed in that way, it is neither simply the whole nor simply a separately subsisting piece of it. The glossator criticizes the easy assimilation of genus, differentia and species to one another merely because they are in a certain respect one in being. Their predicability and their place in the logical constitution must still be distinguished. [The lower page continues with further criticisms of the account and with the question of universal predication; these have not been translated completely.]

**Source-and-completeness caution:** The lower printed passage is longer than the selected English here; unresolved clauses and page joins are not silently filled. It remains a separately authored stream and is not a completed ḥāshiya translation.

## I:74 — Why a compound must ultimately contain uncombined constituents

*Original printed Vol. I:74 · primary PDF physical page 87 · source-inspected first-pass text · not independently proofread.*

### Upper printed commentary (Taftāzānī lemmata + Sanandajī, mixed)

[Continuing the discussion of simple quiddity:] It has no parts, as [the point does not], while what is composed from it, such as a body, has a composition. **“For the absence of composite quiddities”** would entail the absence of **simple** ones. **“But it does not follow that [the series of quiddities] ends in the simple.”** For unity is a principle of multiplicity: just as one cannot have a number, whether finite or infinite, without units, so one cannot have what is multiple without there being in it some unities—that is, items not actually divided, whether or not they are capable of division.

Composition may be **real**: a single reality results from a plurality of things and possesses its own characteristics, concomitants and effects. Or it may be **conceptual**: the intellect regards a number of things as one although there is no such single thing in the extramental world. **“The dependence of some parts upon others in a real compound is necessary.”** For if each part were completely independent of the other, a single real quiddity would not result from them. [The need for parts is further analysed on I:75.]

### Original Arabic anchors (not full transcription)

وانتهائها إلى البسيطة ؛ الواحد مبدأ التعدد ؛ التركيب قد يكون حقيقيا ؛ وقد يكون اعتباريا ؛ واحتياج بعض الأجزاء إلى بعض في المركب الحقيقي ضروري

### Separate lower-register commentary — selected source renderings and interpretive explanations, author unassigned

The lower commentator questions a too-quick inference from the existence of a composed reality to the existence of a simple component *as a separately subsisting entity*. A real whole is indeed conceived through its constituents, but it may be an additional mistake to assume that each of those constituents must exist independently in the manner of a finished individual. The explanatory issue is whether the dependence of a whole upon its components is physical, essential, or solely the product of intellectual analysis. [The lengthy lower development, including the discussion of the one and number, remains untranslated in full.]

**Source-and-completeness caution:** The lower printed passage is longer than the selected English here; unresolved clauses and page joins are not silently filled. It remains a separately authored stream and is not a completed ḥāshiya translation.

## I:75 — Real and conceptual compounds; matter and form

*Original printed Vol. I:75 · primary PDF physical page 88 · source-inspected first-pass text · not independently proofread.*

### Upper printed commentary (Taftāzānī lemmata + Sanandajī, mixed)

[The parts of a real composite] must give rise to **one quiddity and a single genuine unity**. As for dependence, it may run **in one direction**: as in a body composed of the simple elements, or in things constituted through mineral, vegetal and animal forms, where the form is said to need its matter without the converse dependence being exactly the same. Or dependence may be **reciprocal**, as in the relation reported between prime matter and form: matter needs form because its persistence is through form, and form needs matter insofar as its individualization takes place through matter. This is how the position is stated by its proponents.

**“Unlike the conceptual composite.”** Here one cannot properly say that an extramentally existing collective configuration requires its material parts in the same manner, for the alleged collective form is simply an act of intellectual consideration. [The distinction between this thought-dependent unity and a real composition is elaborated by the marginal commentator.]

### Original Arabic anchors (not full transcription)

ماهية واحدة وحدة حقيقية ؛ قد يكون من جانب واحد ؛ كالمركب من البسائط العنصرية ؛ الهيولى والصورة ؛ بخلاف الاعتباري ؛ محض اعتبار العقل

### Separate lower-register commentary — selected source renderings and interpretive explanations, author unassigned

The lower note presses the apparent paradox: if a unity of composition is *merely considered* by the mind, it does not thereby become a second extramental thing over and above the parts. Yet this cannot be taken to deny every genuine composite—for example, one still has to explain whether a body's unity comes from substantial form and how its constituents depend on one another. The writer distinguishes an actual component from something brought into the analysis by stipulation; the same mereological language does not establish an identical mode of composition in each case. [Further objections to how a real form requires matter and whether the whole exists beyond its parts remain to be translated.]

**Source-and-completeness caution:** The lower printed passage is longer than the selected English here; unresolved clauses and page joins are not silently filled. It remains a separately authored stream and is not a completed ḥāshiya translation.

## I:76 — Why a merely considered collective does not subsist; is the quiddity itself made?

*Original printed Vol. I:76 · primary PDF physical page 89 · source-inspected first-pass text · not independently proofread.*

### Upper printed commentary (Taftāzānī lemmata + Sanandajī, mixed)

[A conceptual collective] has no realization as an additional thing in the external world. For an army, outside the mind, consists of the individual soldiers; if the collective configuration were counted as a further constituent of the army, the army would thereby have an element that does not actually exist outside the mind, and one would be driven to absurd conclusions about its external existence.

There is then a disagreement over **possible quiddities**: **are they made (majʿūla) by an agent, or are they not?** What is meant by being made must first be determined. If it means **needing an efficient cause**, the possible existent plainly needs a cause. But is this dependence something belonging to the *quiddity as such*, or does it follow upon its existence? The commentator reports the dispute and the preference expressed by the author of the base text. The issue must not be settled by taking “making” as though it meant merely adding an already existing quiddity to a further independent entity. [The specific alternatives continue on I:77.]

### Original Arabic anchors (not full transcription)

لا تحقق لها في الخارج ؛ إذ ليس من العسكر في الخارج إلا تلك الأفراد ؛ ثم إنهم اختلفوا في الماهيات الممكنة ؛ أهي مجعولة ؛ الاحتياج إلى الفاعل ؛ من لوازم الوجود

### Separate lower-register commentary — selected source renderings and interpretive explanations, author unassigned

The lower gloss explicitly cautions that *being-made* can mean more than one thing. One sense concerns the thing's need for an agent; another concerns whether the quiddity itself is something for which an agent must supply being. A possible essence cannot be treated as though it subsisted by itself and then subsequently received existence without an argument establishing that separation. The writer also distinguishes real compounds—whose unity may require a substantial formal principle—from a conceptual group such as an army, whose abstract collectivity has no independent counterpart beyond the soldiers. [The ensuing detailed reply to the quiddity objection remains unfinished.]

**Source-and-completeness caution:** The lower printed passage is longer than the selected English here; unresolved clauses and page joins are not silently filled. It remains a separately authored stream and is not a completed ḥāshiya translation.

## I:77 — Being made and causal dependence: quiddity versus existence

*Original printed Vol. I:77 · primary PDF physical page 90 · source-inspected first-pass text · not independently proofread.*

### Upper printed commentary (Taftāzānī lemmata + Sanandajī, mixed)

The termination of a body's magnitude is a concomitant of its *existing bodily state*: even if one supposes a body unlimited in magnitude, it remains a body. It is **not** a concomitant of quiddity in the way that **the evenness of four** follows the quiddity of four; if four were deprived of evenness it would no longer be four.

**“Otherwise”**—if the intended meaning of the parties in dispute is not the distinction just explained—**“the possible's dependence upon its cause is necessary.”** To that extent, on the proposed interpretation, the controversy would be verbal. With this the exposition ends the discussions of **existence and quiddity** and turns to their attendant questions. [A new chapter begins on I:78.]

### Original Arabic anchors (not full transcription)

كتناهي الأجسام ؛ لا من لوازم الماهية كزوجية الأربعة ؛ وإلا ؛ فإن احتياج الممكن إلى العلة ضروري ؛ فلنفرغ عن مباحث الوجود والماهية

### Separate lower-register commentary — selected source renderings and interpretive explanations, author unassigned

The marginal writer challenges the claim that dependence on an agent is wholly uncontroversial. The word *made* may refer to what is brought about, while dependence is a different relation requiring specification of the relevant subject. To say “the quiddity needs the agent” may mean that **an existent of that quiddity** needs an efficient cause, not that a quiddity considered independently of being must first be a completed object to which existence is attached. Thus the choice of meaning can make much of the disagreement verbal without erasing the real question of the ontological subject of causation. [The larger dispute is not exhausted by this translated selection.]

**Source-and-completeness caution:** The lower printed passage is longer than the selected English here; unresolved clauses and page joins are not silently filled. It remains a separately authored stream and is not a completed ḥāshiya translation.

## I:78 — New chapter: individuation is not interchangeable with existence or quiddity

*Original printed Vol. I:78 · primary PDF physical page 91 · source-inspected first-pass text · not independently proofread.*

### Upper printed commentary (Taftāzānī lemmata + Sanandajī, mixed)

**Chapter: On individuation (*al-taʿayyun*).** As stated in the *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid*, individuation is other than a thing's **quiddity, existence and unity**, since each of these may be shared by it and another thing, whereas individuation is not shared in that manner. Thus it is correct to predicate of a universal that it has a quiddity, that it is existent [in the relevant sense], and that it is one; it is not correct to assert in the same sense that it is an individuated item. If individuation meant nothing other than those common descriptions, the difference in predication would be unexplained. [The reasoning continues with a disputed inference and a marginal challenge.]

### Original Arabic anchors (not full transcription)

فصل في التعين ؛ غير ماهيته ووجوده ووحدته ؛ لكون كل من هذه الأمور مشتركا بينه وبين غيره ؛ بخلاف التعين

### Separate lower-register commentary — selected source renderings and interpretive explanations, author unassigned

The marginal commentary objects that a common predicate and a genuinely shared individual determination are not the same. Each of several individual existents may be called *existent*, while what makes each this individual is not, for that reason, common to them. The proof needs to distinguish a shared *notion of existence* from the specific existence of one individual, and the shared notion of *unity* from numerical individuation. Otherwise the reasoning risks treating two different senses of “common” as equivalent. [The remaining discussion of the relation between a mental notion and extramental individuation is untranslated.]

**Source-and-completeness caution:** The lower printed passage is longer than the selected English here; unresolved clauses and page joins are not silently filled. It remains a separately authored stream and is not a completed ḥāshiya translation.

## I:79 — Whether individuation is something positive, privative or merely considered

*Original printed Vol. I:79 · primary PDF physical page 92 · source-inspected first-pass text · not independently proofread.*

### Upper printed commentary (Taftāzānī lemmata + Sanandajī, mixed)

One may predicate of a plurality the notion of individuation, since each individual has its own individuating state; yet this does not entail that the **very same individuation** belongs to all. For the individual determinations differ, even when the individuals share a quiddity. Likewise, *being distinct from another* can be predicated in a manner not identical with the thing's intrinsic individuation. Common predication of a word is not automatically identity of its concrete referent.

Accordingly, a further disagreement arises: **Is individuation something existent (wujūdī) or privative (ʿadamī)? Is it real (ḥaqīqī) or a matter of consideration (iʿtibārī)?** Before adjudicating these positions, one must clarify what the respective terms mean. **“The individuals of a kind ...”** [The formulation of the alternatives continues on I:80.]

### Original Arabic anchors (not full transcription)

اختلف في أن التعين وجودي أو عدمي ؛ حقيقي أو اعتباري ؛ أفراد النوع

### Separate lower-register commentary — selected source renderings and interpretive explanations, author unassigned

The lower writer urges that to describe individuation as a special *accident* attaching to a quiddity is insufficient by itself. A thing's being distinct from all others may be compared with a relation, and an absolute or relational predicate can still be said of many. The actual debate is whether, in addition to the quiddity and its existence, a distinct individuating entity must be posited. [The long marginal critique and citation of *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid* have not been fully translated.]

**Source-and-completeness caution:** The lower printed passage is longer than the selected English here; unresolved clauses and page joins are not silently filled. It remains a separately authored stream and is not a completed ḥāshiya translation.

## I:80 — The options: existence, nonexistence, accidents, and individuation

*Original printed Vol. I:80 · primary PDF physical page 93 · source-inspected first-pass text · not independently proofread.*

### Upper printed commentary (Taftāzānī lemmata + Sanandajī, mixed)

[Continuing the dispute over individuation.] The individuating determination must give the individual a character that prevents its being shared. This may be explained as **“the individuating reality or individuation”**, understood as a determination accompanying the quiddity, or as the quiddity's **concomitant accidents**. Yet the question remains what kind of thing such a determination is.

**“Or [something privative]”**: one possible analysis makes individuation an absence, either a simple absence or a relative absence, such as the exclusion of participation. **“Or additional to that”**—a combination of a positive and a negative description might be proposed. The wording asks whether each proposed determinant prevents sharing simply by being predicated of one thing or whether the very refusal of sharing is part of what it means to individuate.

**“Or [an individuating state] existential”**: it might be asserted that individuation coincides with the individual's very external existence, or with something attached to that existence. Alternatively it may be taken as a **mental or conceptual consideration**, and not another independent extramental accident. But if it is only a consideration, there is still the fact that the individual itself is not in reality shared among many. The several possibilities are put forward as disputed alternatives, not as Sanandajī's unqualified commitments.

The following argument asks whether, on any of these alternatives, the quiddity can acquire individuation merely through a condition that would itself admit further predication. [The result continues on I:81.]

### Original Arabic anchors (not full transcription)

التعين وهو تلك الهاذية ؛ الحاصلة بالعوارض ؛ أو ما فقدها ؛ أو عدم قبوله لها ؛ الوجودي ؛ الاعتباري ؛ لا يسمى أن التعين وجودي أو عدمي حقيقي أو اعتباري

### Separate lower-register commentary — selected source renderings and interpretive explanations, author unassigned

The marginal critic distinguishes a *negative* account of individuation, which excludes another individual's sharing this very occurrence, from a *positive* constituent that exists independently. The denial of another's sharing does not, by itself, prove that a further real form must inhere in the subject. Likewise the conceptual act by which the intellect singles something out is not necessarily the same as the thing's being a single subject externally. [The remaining analysis of causes and accidents is still uncollated.]

**Source-and-completeness caution:** The lower printed passage is longer than the selected English here; unresolved clauses and page joins are not silently filled. It remains a separately authored stream and is not a completed ḥāshiya translation.

## I:81 — Can a universal conception ever guarantee individual singularity?

*Original printed Vol. I:81 · primary PDF physical page 94 · source-inspected first-pass text · not independently proofread.*

### Upper printed commentary (Taftāzānī lemmata + Sanandajī, mixed)

A further qualification concerns explanation by negation or conjunction. If one appends an additional description to a universal concept, the intellect can still conceive that fuller description as predicable of many. The same holds however many such conceptual descriptions are accumulated; their conjunction does not of itself necessitate that the resulting concept signify one and only one individual.

Thus individuation may be assigned **“to the free and powerful agent”**—this is the position presented as consonant with the author's account of all possible things depending ultimately upon God's free action. Or it may be assigned **“to external existence”**, as reported from some philosophers, on the ground that a quiddity which has become existent outside the intellect cannot be shared as an individual occurrence, whereas the quiddity conceived in the mind can.

An objection is raised. If existence is one in meaning, would one and the same cause of individuation not produce one and the same individuating result? The reply distinguishes unity of the **notion** of existence from the plurality of its **individual instances**, differentiated through times, places and the other conditions or causes. It is further noted that mere concomitance does not establish a causal explanation. The next objection is this: we apprehend an existent as an individual even while disregarding the additional conditions just mentioned. [The response opens I:82.]

### Original Arabic anchors (not full transcription)

لا يمكن للعقل فرض صدقه على كثيرين ؛ إما استنادا إلى الفاعل القادر ؛ أو إلى الوجود الخارجي ؛ وإنما تقبلها في العقل ؛ الوجود وإن كان بحسب المفهوم واحدا ؛ تتعدد أفراده بحسب الأزمنة والأمكنة وسائر الأسباب

### Separate lower-register commentary — selected source renderings and interpretive explanations, author unassigned

The marginal writer distinguishes **the impossibility of actual sharing** of an external individual from the intellect's ability to form a repeatable *concept* of a similar individual. That a common concept can apply to many is not the same as many individuals participating in numerically the same concrete existence. If the alleged determinant is only “existence” without qualification, the problem recurs, for the concept of existence is itself common. Either the relevant determinant must be specified, or the argument must be understood as concerning actual individual occurrences and not the bare universal notion. [This paraphrase follows a selected stretch of the lower objection, which has not been translated completely.]

**Source-and-completeness caution:** The lower printed passage is longer than the selected English here; unresolved clauses and page joins are not silently filled. It remains a separately authored stream and is not a completed ḥāshiya translation.

## I:82 — Individuality and the alternative of self-individuating quiddities

*Original printed Vol. I:82 · primary PDF physical page 95 · source-inspected first-pass text · not independently proofread.*

### Upper printed commentary (Taftāzānī lemmata + Sanandajī, mixed)

**“We reply: setting a thing aside in thought does not entail its nonexistence.”** Thus, the fact that one recognizes the individuality of an external thing while not consciously considering its various accompanying causes does not show that those causes are absent.

**“Or [individuation may be assigned] to other causes.”** This is given as the philosophers' position in cases where individuation is said to follow **“from the very quiddity”**. Then a kind may be confined to a single individual, the individuation following from the quiddity so understood; another individuation of that same quiddity would be impossible, and it would not be separable from the individuating condition already said to follow it.

But if individuation is taken to be in every case something **additional to the quiddity**, difficulties arise about how the extra item itself is individuated and how the explanation avoids further regress. Conversely, if a quiddity is said to be individuated **in itself**, the next enquiry is what is thereby meant by a nature's being individuated and whether a second individual can be conceived. [The sentence breaks at the end of I:82 and continues on I:83.]

### Original Arabic anchors (not full transcription)

قطع النظر عن الشيء لا يوجب انتفاءه ؛ أو إلى أسباب آخر ؛ نفس الماهية ؛ تنحصر النوع في الشخص الواحد

### Separate lower-register commentary — selected source renderings and interpretive explanations, author unassigned

The marginal text says that an individual existent can have a particular cause or principle of individuation without the intellect attending to it in every act of judgment. The dispute is not resolved by simply pointing to the empirical fact that the thing is one. It is also necessary to distinguish cases where a quiddity admits numerous individuals from the purported case of a species whose very nature allows only one. This last question must not be confused with the claim that all quiddities have only one exemplar. [The rest of the lower leaf remains untranslated.]

**Source-and-completeness caution:** The lower printed passage is longer than the selected English here; unresolved clauses and page joins are not silently filled. It remains a separately authored stream and is not a completed ḥāshiya translation.
