## I:83 — Self-individuating substances and individuation through successive dispositions

*Printed I:83 · physical PDF 96 · first-pass mixed-upper and selected unassigned lower*

### Upper printed argument (Taftāzānī lemma and Sanandajī mixed)

[The philosophers assign individuation] **“either to the very individuated substance.”** In the case of the celestial intelligences, according to the position reported here, every separate substance is individuated through itself and thereby distinguished from every other. **“Or through the accidents accompanying it”**—in particular, **“through the succession of aptitudes.”** An example is the prime matter of the four elements. On the reported account this matter is a single common underlying principle, while the determinations distinguishing its occurrences follow the accidents that attach to it as its aptitudes succeed one another, depending upon nearness to or remoteness from the [celestial] sphere. These are alternatives advanced in the philosophical account, not a single homogeneous cause of individuation.

### Selected Arabic anchors (not a transcription)

أو إلى المادة المشخّصة ؛ بما يلحقها من العوارض ؛ بحسب تعاقب الاستعدادات ؛ كهيولى العناصر الأربعة

### Lower register — selected source rendering / interpretation, writer unassigned

The lower gloss objects that the claim of a separate substance's self-individuation needs more than an assertion that the substance cannot admit another copy. If there is a common quiddity applicable to several substances, the question of what differentiates them returns. If there is instead a special quiddity for each, the dispute has shifted to whether such specifically distinct entities may nonetheless share one intelligible nature. The gloss also challenges the account of one matter successively individuated by differing accidents: if matter's individuation required those accidents, can the same individuated material subject be presupposed as the bearer of them? [The detailed cases remain untranslated.]

**Completeness warning:** Significant lower-printed text remains untranslated; the English is not a diplomatic or independently proofread translation of the entire page.

## I:84 — New chapter: necessity, impossibility and possibility

*Printed I:84 · physical PDF 97 · first-pass mixed-upper and selected unassigned lower*

### Upper printed argument (Taftāzānī lemma and Sanandajī mixed)

**Chapter: On necessity, impossibility and possibility.** These are among the determinations that attach to a quiddity when it is considered in relation to existence. They are compared to predications such as eternity and temporal origination. **“They are intelligibles”**—concepts formed by the intellect from the relation of a simple notion to its obtaining existence in itself, or to its not obtaining it. For when the intellect directs a concept toward existence, the result of this comparison falls under one of the three descriptions: *necessary*, *impossible*, or *possible*.

The author introduces the cases by way of examples. The [Necessary Existent's] existence is necessary; the existence of an impossible combination, such as the conjunction of contradictories, is impossible; and the existence of an ordinary contingent individual, such as a human being, is possible. The text then states that **“these three matters are necessary”**—that is, they are so immediately known that they do not stand in need of proper definition or demonstration in the usual way. They are defined, if at all, through their oppositions: necessity by reference to existence, impossibility by reference to nonexistence, and possibility by reference to the absence of the two necessities. [The last phrase is completed on I:85.]

### Selected Arabic anchors (not a transcription)

فصل في بيان الوجوب والامتناع والإمكان ؛ هي من الأمور العارضة للماهية ؛ معقولات ؛ تحصل من نسبة المفهوم إلى الوجود ؛ هذه الأمور الثلاثة ؛ لا تحتاج إلى تعريف

### Lower register — selected source rendering / interpretation, writer unassigned

The lower writer distinguishes a quiddity considered simply in itself from that same quiddity when compared with external existence. “Necessity” is not an additional material part of a quiddity. Whether it is the existence of the object or its necessary relation to existence that is being considered must be stated. Otherwise, describing a quiddity's existence as necessary risks confusing the object of the judgment with the mental relation introduced by the intellect. [The remaining explanation of the three modal predicates is not translated in full.]

**Completeness warning:** Significant lower-printed text remains untranslated; the English is not a diplomatic or independently proofread translation of the entire page.

## I:85 — What “necessary” and “impossible” mean; intrinsic and extrinsic modality

*Printed I:85 · physical PDF 98 · first-pass mixed-upper and selected unassigned lower*

### Upper printed argument (Taftāzānī lemma and Sanandajī mixed)

[The terms are not offered as strict real definitions but as distinctions] **“of their expressions”** (*lafẓan*), meaning explanations of the words employed. Their meanings are already apparent to the intellect; one must not mistake the verbal explanation for a demonstration of a previously unknown essence.

**“The first two are each divided”**—necessity and impossibility, not possibility—**“into intrinsic and extrinsic.”** Necessity of a thing's existence and necessity of its nonexistence may be considered either from the thing itself or in relation to something else. For example, the Necessary Existent's existence and the impossibility of a conjunction of contradictories are treated as intrinsic. Similarly, evenness belongs necessarily to four, whereas oddness is excluded from it. The impossibility of the negation of one of these properties is not simply the occurrence of an external preventing cause.

But the same division is also employed when existence or nonexistence is necessary **through another**. The source thus distinguishes a necessity arising from what the subject is in itself from one following upon a specified extrinsic condition or cause. [The remaining case division is carried forward to I:86.]

### Selected Arabic anchors (not a transcription)

لفظا ؛ ينقسم كل من الأولين ؛ إلى الذاتي والغيري ؛ وجود الباري ؛ عدم اجتماع النقيضين ؛ زوجية الأربعة

### Lower register — selected source rendering / interpretation, writer unassigned

On the claim that the three modalities are self-evident, the gloss insists that the difference between knowing a general notion and understanding every technical restriction placed on it is real. An explanatory definition may help separate what is necessary *in itself* from what becomes necessary under a condition. The fact that the bare notions are familiar does not license moving without argument from intrinsic necessity to necessity through another. [The extended objection comparing the absence of opposites with an extrinsic condition remains uncollated.]

**Completeness warning:** Significant lower-printed text remains untranslated; the English is not a diplomatic or independently proofread translation of the entire page.

## I:86 — Necessary through itself and through another; impossible through itself and through another

*Printed I:86 · physical PDF 99 · first-pass mixed-upper and selected unassigned lower*

### Upper printed argument (Taftāzānī lemma and Sanandajī mixed)

**“Necessary through itself”** means what is characterized by intrinsic necessity of existence, such that the quiddity under the relevant consideration does not admit nonexistence. The example cited is God—exalted be He. **“Necessary through another”** refers to a thing whose existence follows necessarily through something distinct from it. The text invokes the evenness of four as an example of what follows necessarily from the relevant subject.

**“Impossible through itself”** applies to what intrinsic contradiction excludes from existence, such as the conjunction of contradictories. **“Impossible through another”** is considered when the subject's existence is precluded by an external factor or the failure of a required condition. Accordingly, a thing's being prevented from existence need not mean that its own quiddity is contradictory. The distinction depends on whether the necessity or impossibility belongs to what is considered in itself or follows upon what is other than it.

The passage then introduces a qualification: what is called necessary through another does not cease on that account to be contingent in itself. [The contrast is completed on I:87.]

### Selected Arabic anchors (not a transcription)

واجب الوجود لذاته ؛ وهو الله تعالى ؛ لشيء آخر ؛ كزوجية الأربعة ؛ من الممتنع ؛ الموضوع والمحمول

### Lower register — selected source rendering / interpretation, writer unassigned

A separately ruled lower note remarks that a particular instance of impossibility may be bound up with a necessary relation other than a formal contradiction in the subject's own quiddity. Where a predication is prevented by an added condition, the word “impossible” must not be silently read as meaning intrinsically contradictory. The note also questions whether a simple quiddity can be “necessary through itself” in the same sense in which a proposition containing subject and predicate is necessary. [The larger lower argument remains untranslated.]

**Completeness warning:** Significant lower-printed text remains untranslated; the English is not a diplomatic or independently proofread translation of the entire page.

## I:87 — How necessity and impossibility through another can be predicated of a possible thing

*Printed I:87 · physical PDF 100 · first-pass mixed-upper and selected unassigned lower*

### Upper printed argument (Taftāzānī lemma and Sanandajī mixed)

A thing can be **necessary through another** while being *possible in itself*; it can also be **impossible through another** while its quiddity is not itself contradictory. Thus an external condition can necessitate the presence of an otherwise possible thing, while an opposing condition can prevent its presence. The possibility of the thing as considered in itself is not thereby removed.

**“It is possible in the special sense”** when the two intrinsic necessities—of existence and of nonexistence—are both negated. More generally, possibility can signify that a specified necessity has been excluded, even where the opposite necessity is not thereby excluded. A term of modality must consequently be tied to the intended scope and the respect in which the thing is being considered. [The distinction between the special and general senses is pursued on I:88–89.]

### Selected Arabic anchors (not a transcription)

ممتنع الوجود من عدمه ؛ الإمكان ؛ سلب ضرورة الوجود ؛ سلب ضرورة العدم ؛ الإمكان الخاص ؛ الوجوب والامتناع

### Lower register — selected source rendering / interpretation, writer unassigned

The lower gloss points out that, when necessity through another is in question, the predicated necessity concerns a *relation under a condition*, whereas the nature considered simply in itself may remain contingent. The alleged contradiction between possible and necessary arises only if the two predicates are asserted of the same thing in precisely the same respect. Similarly, to deny necessity of existence is not yet to affirm intrinsic necessity of nonexistence. [The later elaboration of the general and special modal senses is not completely translated here.]

**Completeness warning:** Significant lower-printed text remains untranslated; the English is not a diplomatic or independently proofread translation of the entire page.

## I:88 — Possibility in respect of a future occurrence: the short upper lemma

*Printed I:88 · physical PDF 101 · first-pass mixed-upper and selected unassigned lower*

### Upper printed argument (Taftāzānī lemma and Sanandajī mixed)

[The source introduces another use of] **“possibility in respect of the future”** (*al-imkān bi-l-naẓar ilā l-istiqbāl*), meaning that the thing's existence **may occur in the future**. [The remainder of this leaf is a lengthy lower discussion of how this future-oriented sense is related to the special and general senses of possibility; there is no additional upper exposition.]

### Selected Arabic anchors (not a transcription)

الإمكان ؛ بالنظر إلى الاستقبال ؛ بمعنى جواز وجود الشيء في المستقبل

### Lower register — selected source rendering / interpretation, writer unassigned

The marginal writer questions whether merely saying that something “may occur in the future” can be a genuine explication of possibility. A future event might, as a matter of its complete cause, be necessary, while what is impossible in itself does not become possible by shifting attention to a later time. The discussion therefore distinguishes the **absence of a known necessity** from the **actual absence of necessity**. To say that the future has not yet been settled for us is not an adequate definition of an objective modal status. The writer also observes that the general notion of possibility is not restricted to futurity: one can speak of possibility with regard to a past or present state, provided the intended modal sense is clear. [This selected argumentative rendering does not exhaust the long lower page.]

**Completeness warning:** Significant lower-printed text remains untranslated; the English is not a diplomatic or independently proofread translation of the entire page.

## I:89 — Future possibility and dispositional possibility (*imkān istiʿdādī*)

*Printed I:89 · physical PDF 102 · first-pass mixed-upper and selected unassigned lower*

### Upper printed argument (Taftāzānī lemma and Sanandajī mixed)

A future-oriented possibility is distinguished from possibility considered with respect to past or present occurrences. The future is not yet apprehended as having one of the two opposed states, existence or nonexistence. By contrast, in the past and the present one of those states is already actual in the relevant respect. The discussion then introduces **dispositional possibility** (*al-imkān al-istiʿdādī*).

This latter possibility is counted among **accidental qualities** associated with the preparation of a subject for receiving a form or effect. It is realized in differing degrees of intensity or weakness according to the subject's nearness to or remoteness from the conditions required for the effect. The familiar example is the successive aptitude for human development: what is present in the initial generative material is transformed through later embryonic stages. This is not the bare logical possibility of the human quiddity, for that remains considered in relation to the nature itself. Rather, this aptitude comes into being when some of the requisite conditions are present and ceases or is transformed when the effect previously present only in potency is actualized. [The source's closing attribution continues on I:90.]

### Selected Arabic anchors (not a transcription)

الاستقبال ؛ الإنسانية الحاصل للنطفة ثم للعلقة ثم للمضغة ؛ الإمكان الاستعدادي ؛ عرض ؛ مقولة الكيف

### Lower register — selected source rendering / interpretation, writer unassigned

The marginal criticism develops a problem in the future-oriented definition. If the possibility of future existence is taken to require an absence of an already actual state, it seems to involve the present absence of the effect rather than an account of the future itself. If it is taken to require only that the effect may be produced in the future, that is already a modal premise and does not explain the concept. The gloss therefore separates the *conditions of a subject's preparation* from the modal status of the outcome, while retaining the possibility that a preparatory state can become stronger and eventually be replaced by the effect. [Other lines remain untranslated.]

**Completeness warning:** Significant lower-printed text remains untranslated; the English is not a diplomatic or independently proofread translation of the entire page.

## I:90 — The material subject of preparedness; does a new event require pre-existing matter?

*Printed I:90 · physical PDF 103 · first-pass mixed-upper and selected unassigned lower*

### Upper printed argument (Taftāzānī lemma and Sanandajī mixed)

[Continuing the discussion of readiness for an effect:] it is argued that **matter must precede** an originated thing, for if that matter itself were originated and required a prior matter in the same manner, an infinite regress would seem to follow. Matter is therefore claimed to be **“ancient”** in the relevant sense, serving as the **subject of possibility**. The argument proceeds: before an event exists it is possible, neither intrinsically impossible nor necessary; this possibility is taken to have a real, accidental status and hence to require a subject. That subject cannot be the future event itself, since the event does not yet exist, and cannot be a thing wholly external to it if the potentiality in question is precisely the event's potential existence. It is therefore located in the underlying matter.

The text then adds that the occurrence of actual effects depends upon the successive availability of conditions and causes **“in that matter”**. The proposal is a reported metaphysical argument for a material subject of preparedness, not an independent proof that an eternal matter exists. [The extended marginal refutation, including objections concerning origination, preparedness and regress, remains to be translated in full.]

### Selected Arabic anchors (not a transcription)

قديمة ؛ تكون تلك المادة ؛ محلا للإمكان ؛ الحادث قبل وجوده ممكن ؛ على المادة من الشرائط والأسباب

### Lower register — selected source rendering / interpretation, writer unassigned

The lower commentary challenges the inference from the actuality of an effect's possibility to the necessary existence of a material substratum prior to it. “Possible” can be predicated of an effect considered in itself without thereby establishing a separately existing accidental quality that inheres in matter. Even where one posits an aptitude that genuinely belongs to a material subject, it does not follow that *every* possible thing must have a preparatory material subject in that sense. Otherwise the account risks assuming the very materialist premise it purports to prove. The margin also discusses whether an originated event requires the origination of matter, whether matter's capacity can persist without the effect and what kind of regress would follow if every potentiality needed a prior potentiality. [Only a selected part of this extended rebuttal is rendered here.]

**Completeness warning:** Significant lower-printed text remains untranslated; the English is not a diplomatic or independently proofread translation of the entire page.

# New continuation: Volume I:91–104

The following fourteen positions are new source-inspected English with the authorial registers kept distinct. Uncertain clauses have not been silently completed, and long uncollated marginal arguments remain explicitly incomplete.

## I:91 — The possible thing and its need for an external determinant
*Original printed Volume I:91 · physical source PDF 104 · provisional mixed upper; lower separately marked; no independent proof.*

### Upper register — source-inspected working English

[Continuing the argument from I:90 about a material subject of possibility.] The appeal to *preparedness* (*imkān istiʿdādī*) does not establish the stated conclusion without qualification. It must not be confused with the intrinsic possibility that belongs to a possible quiddity: the latter is opposed to both intrinsic necessity of existence and intrinsic impossibility, accompanies the possible nature under consideration, and is not absent simply because the subject has not yet come to exist. Nor does this intrinsic possibility, merely as such, entail an already existing material locus.

Next comes the judgment that **a possible thing depends upon an efficient determinant with respect to its existence and its nonexistence**. The claim is grounded in the equality of its two sides considered from the nature itself. If neither existence nor nonexistence follows from what the thing is, the priority of one side over the other needs explanation. The author calls it necessary that **one of two equal sides cannot be preferred without a preferrer** (*tarjīḥ aḥad al-ṭarafayn bilā murajjiḥ*). The word “necessary” belongs to the immediately apprehended status of this judgment, not to the intrinsic necessity of the possible thing. [The sentence continues at the beginning of I:92.]

### Arabic anchors (selected words; not a full transcription)

الإمكان الاستعدادي ؛ الإمكان الذاتي اللازم للماهية الممكنة ؛ احتياج الممكن في وجوده وعدمه إلى المؤثر ؛ امتناع ترجيح أحد طرفيه بلا مرجح

### Lower register — separate writer unassigned

**Lower-register argument — first connected selection, writer unassigned.** The objection notes that the opponent himself recognizes a possible thing's possibility before its existence. This possibility is said to attach necessarily to the quiddity, yet the opponent also claims that it requires an existing locus, the matter of the possible thing. Thus, the bare assertion that a determination belongs to the quiddity is not enough to establish that it requires no existent locus. If the aim is to deny such a locus to intrinsic possibility, the more pertinent response is that **the possibility in question is an intellectual consideration (*amr iʿtibārī*)**, not an independently existing accident requiring a corporeal subject. This is different from treating *material preparedness* as a real disposition inhering in matter.

The lower writer then examines an argument that one of two sides of a possible thing must be specified by something external. A condition that belongs to the possible thing itself cannot simply be assumed to be such an externally selecting cause. If each side of the possible is treated as genuinely symmetrical, the relevant argument must establish what breaks the symmetry rather than merely giving that break a new name. [The remaining lower discussion of two equal sides continues onto I:92; its final clauses are not yet translated.]

**Reading caution:** Opening words have been read in conjunction with I:90; a few compressed connectors and the last upper half-line need independent Arabic collation. Lower-note ending remains incomplete.

**Coverage warning:** No complete all-register translation is claimed for I:91.

## I:92 — Why the possibility of existence and nonexistence entails dependence
*Original printed Volume I:92 · physical source PDF 105 · provisional mixed upper; lower separately marked; no independent proof.*

### Upper register — source-inspected working English

[Continuation:] One apprehends the judgment without needing to begin with a separate demonstration: a possible thing, when considered **in itself**, does not require either its existence or its nonexistence. **“Needing”** an external cause means precisely that neither of those two sides obtains through the thing's own essence; each, when it obtains, is accounted for by something beyond that essence.

There is, nevertheless, a challenge here. Could the occurrence of a possible thing be neither through itself nor through an external determinant, but simply occur **by coincidence**? The author draws attention to the apparent self-evidence of rejecting this alternative. Yet a claim's appearing obvious at first sight is not identical with a complete proof; the passage is preparing to test the force of the prohibition on preferring without a preferrer.

**“And this”**—namely, the preference just mentioned and said to be inadmissible—must be distinguished from the selection of an alternative by a genuinely choosing agent. [The example of that selection begins on I:93.]

### Arabic anchors (selected words; not a full transcription)

لا يقتضي ذاته وجوده ولا عدمه ؛ معنى الاحتياج أن كلا من وجوده وعدمه لا يكون لذاته بل لأمر خارج ؛ بمجرد الاتفاق ؛ الترجيح

### Lower register — separate writer unassigned

**Lower-register selection — a reply concerning equal roads.** Suppose a fleeing person must choose one of two routes. It does not follow that there must be an independently determining motive in favor of the particular road taken. If such a motive were required, and if the agent were moved necessarily by that motive, the supposed freedom to take either road would disappear. The preference of **taking a route rather than remaining still** can be explained by the desire to escape, while the selection of **this route rather than that route** need not be attributed to a distinct attractive feature of the chosen road. To confuse the two questions is to make the supposed example prove more than it does.

The lower writer also distinguishes (i) the possibility of a thing considered in itself from (ii) its existence after all conditions for an actual occurrence are present. A thing's being intrinsically possible does not, simply by itself, establish an equal chance of its two alternatives under every specified set of conditions. [The long note continues into the following leaf.]

**Reading caution:** The gloss reports and critiques several distinct attempts to reconcile freely choosing between equal alternatives with the maxim against preference without a preferrer. These are not one uncontested authorial assertion.

**Coverage warning:** No complete all-register translation is claimed for I:92.

---

## I:93 — The fleeing person and two equally effective paths
*Original printed Volume I:93 · physical source PDF 106 · provisional mixed upper; lower separately marked; no independent proof.*

### Upper register — source-inspected working English

[The objection invokes] **the preference of one of two equal alternatives without a specifying factor**. The example is a person fleeing a wild beast: the person takes **one of two roads**, both equally capable of leading to the desired end, namely escape and safety. This is offered as an instance of choosing between alternatives even when there is no prior difference that requires one road rather than the other.

The author replies that **we do not declare such a choice impossible**, much less hold its impossibility to be an immediately necessary truth. Nor does its admissibility imply the admissibility of the preference that was rejected. In the present example, **the act of fleeing has already been preferred to not fleeing**. The agent's will selects one road and thereby leaves the other untaken. The motive of safety explains acting rather than remaining inactive, even if no separate motive attracts the agent to this particular route rather than to its equal. The agent's will can thus determine one option without a further difference in the roads themselves.

**“If it is said ...”** The following objection turns to whether the preferred side must obtain in a way that would make the causal action redundant. [The opening of the objection is carried forward at I:94.]

### Arabic anchors (selected words; not a full transcription)

ترجيح اختيار أحد المتساويين على الآخر بلا المخصص ؛ كالهارب من السبع ؛ يسلك أحد الطريقين المتساويين في الإفضاء إلى المطلوب الذي هو النجاة ؛ فنحن لا نقول بامتناعه ؛ فإن الهارب بإرادته يرجح سلوك أحد الطريقين

### Lower register — separate writer unassigned

**Lower margin — source-dependent argumentative summary, not a diplomatic translation.** The printed gloss presses the distinction between a motive explaining why the subject acts and a determination explaining why one of two outwardly indistinguishable means is chosen. It considers whether the will as such can function as a determining factor without a distinct desirable property attaching to one alternative. The remaining examples and replies in the dense lower text have not been fully deciphered.

**Reading caution:** Read “two roads equal in leading to safety” literally; do not replace with a generic picture of equally desirable objects. The word introducing the speaker's objection at the end is clear; its continuation crosses the page boundary.

**Coverage warning:** No complete all-register translation is claimed for I:93.

## I:94 — Making an effect occur versus obtaining what is already there
*Original printed Volume I:94 · physical source PDF 107 · provisional mixed upper; lower separately marked; no independent proof.*

### Upper register — source-inspected working English

[The objection asks about the supposed influence of a cause upon one of the two contradictory sides.] If the thing already exists, a further production of that very existence would be **obtaining what has already been obtained** (*taḥṣīl al-ḥāṣil*). If it does not exist, how can the alleged cause act upon it so as to make it exist? The difficulty depends on how **causal influence** (*taʾthīr*) and **bringing about** (*ījād*) are understood.

The author distinguishes the act of bringing a thing into being from the incoherent proposal of giving existence again to something already existent under that same description. Nor must the relation between cause and effect be pictured as an alteration of an already existing object. The objection assumes that, if a cause acts, it must find its effect already present in a way that makes the act superfluous, or else act upon sheer nonbeing as though that nonbeing were a receptive subject. The response denies that these are exhaustive descriptions of causal origination.

The final point is that one must be careful about the meaning of an **occurring effect** and the mode in which its coming to exist is predicated. [The compressed concluding clause requires a second textual check.]

### Arabic anchors (selected words; not a full transcription)

التأثير والإيجاد ؛ تحصيل الحاصل ؛ تحصيل آخر غير هذا التحصيل ؛ ويختار أن التأثير حال العدم

### Lower register — separate writer unassigned

**Lower register:** The long gloss discusses the moment at which causal influence is supposed to take place: while the effect is absent, while it is present, or at the boundary between the two. It also questions whether “production” is being defined by mere correlation or by a real act of a productive source. **Only this issue-locating description is supplied here; no full lower-page translation is claimed.**

**Reading caution:** The upper text has a very compact series of alternatives; this English preserves their argumentative structure but does not claim a certified rendering of each compressed connector.

**Coverage warning:** No complete all-register translation is claimed for I:94.

## I:95 — The occurrence of an event is not the whole meaning of its need for a cause
*Original printed Volume I:95 · physical source PDF 108 · provisional mixed upper; lower separately marked; no independent proof.*

### Upper register — source-inspected working English

The contention that causal influence is impossible on the proposed conception of an **originated event** does not by itself establish that temporal origination is the ground of need. One must distinguish the claim that a thing **comes to be after not being** from the claim that it **is intrinsically such that neither existence nor nonexistence is necessary for it**.

An inference that relies on the occurrence of an event must not silently import what is already supplied by its possibility. Nor may the presence or absence of a cause be inferred merely by repeating a verbal description of origination. The discussion cautions against treating a causal determination as a second physical act added to the first production of the event.

The question is now formulated explicitly: **Is what makes a thing need a cause its possibility (*imkān*), or its temporal origination (*ḥudūth*)?** The positions on that question follow in the next section. [The final upper words cross to I:96.]

### Arabic anchors (selected words; not a full transcription)

الترجيح ؛ امتناع التأثير ؛ حدوث ؛ المحوج ؛ الإمكان ؛ الحدوث

### Lower register — separate writer unassigned

**Lower register:** A separately printed objection begins by asking whether an alleged origination must involve a cause acting upon nonexistence, and whether the same account of causal action must be given before, at, and after the event. The gloss then examines whether causal priority can be taken as purely explanatory without assuming a distinct temporal interval. These are selected points of the objection; the original lower clauses are not entirely translated.

**Reading caution:** The upper source is compressed and its opening does not stand independently of I:94. Treat this as an uncertain connected reading until a second direct collation.

**Coverage warning:** No complete all-register translation is claimed for I:95.

## I:96 — Two accounts of the ground of causal dependence: possibility and origination
*Original printed Volume I:96 · physical source PDF 109 · provisional mixed upper; lower separately marked; no independent proof.*

### Upper register — source-inspected working English

**“The ground of need is possibility or origination.”** There is disagreement concerning this: the philosophers, together with some theologians, take **possibility** (*imkān*) to be what makes a thing need its cause, whereas earlier theologians take **temporal origination** (*ḥudūth*) as the ground. **“Each has a direction”**—that is, each position has an argument to offer.

The first account runs as follows. When the intellect considers a thing as **not requiring existence and not requiring nonexistence through itself**, it judges that neither of those sides can obtain without an external factor. This is the meaning of the thing's need for a cause. That judgment can be made whether or not the intellect also considers the thing as preceded by nonexistence. It is therefore possibility, rather than being preceded by nonexistence, that figures in the explanatory ground.

The second account considers the subject in respect of its actual **coming into existence after not existing**. On this view the judgment that it requires an originator attaches to it as originated. The two modes of considering the thing must not be treated as though they were simply the same premise in different words. [The second argument and replies continue on I:97.]

### Arabic anchors (selected words; not a full transcription)

والمحوج هو الإمكان أو الحدوث ؛ ذهب إلى الأول الحكماء وبعض المتكلمين وإلى الثاني قدماء المتكلمين ؛ ولكل وجهة ؛ كونه غير مقتض للوجود والعدم بالنظر إلى ذاته

### Lower register — separate writer unassigned

**Lower register — selected argumentative distinction only.** One question raised is whether the intellect's recognition of the equal relation of the possible quiddity to existence and nonexistence establishes an extramental cause, or merely identifies a conceptual need requiring further proof. The opposing account makes the coming-to-be of the effect central. The printed writer asks whether each side is covertly assuming the causal principle it sets out to explain. [Not a complete translation of the lower page.]

**Reading caution:** The names of the schools reflect the reporting in this printed source; no independent historical verification of each reported attribution is asserted.

**Coverage warning:** No complete all-register translation is claimed for I:96.

## I:97 — Possibility before existence; the intellectual order of the proof
*Original printed Volume I:97 · physical source PDF 110 · provisional mixed upper; lower separately marked; no independent proof.*

### Upper register — source-inspected working English

A quiddity and its existence can be described as **possible** before the quiddity actually comes to exist. Temporal origination, on the other hand, is not predicated of the quiddity or its existence in this way until the thing has entered existence. This is one reason for giving possibility explanatory priority over origination in the proof of causal need.

The source cites the *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid* to clarify that such priority is **according to the intellect**. The point is not that the mind's successive considerations themselves cause the external dependence. Rather, when the intellect apprehends the possibility or origination of a thing, it judges that the thing needs another. The question is how the mind correctly orders the conditions of that judgment.

There remains an objection: if what is meant by dependence is that a possible thing is equally related to existence and nonexistence until something external specifies it, then one must show that the relevant equality belongs to it **in reality**, not merely that our concept of the thing does not specify one side. A conceptual distinction must not, without argument, be substituted for the extramental dependence sought in the proof. [The discussion continues across the next leaf.]

### Arabic anchors (selected words; not a full transcription)

وصف الماهية ووجودها بالإمكان قبل أن تتصف به ؛ وأما الحدوث فلا تتصف به الماهية ولا وجودها إلا حال كونها موجودة ؛ قال في شرح المقاصد ؛ إن العلية إنما هي بحسب العقل

### Lower register — separate writer unassigned

**Separate lower critique, selected passage in close English.** The lower writer insists on keeping distinct (1) the intellect's judgment that a thing is possible, (2) the thing's actual being brought into existence, and (3) the condition under which an object is truly dependent. If “need” is only an intellectual relation, the conclusion about a genuinely independent cause does not follow without qualification. If “need” is a real condition, the conceptual consideration of possibility cannot be its only explanation. The remainder of the long note tests this choice by reference to the case of the possible object before its existence and after it comes into existence. [Several clauses remain untranslated.]

**Reading caution:** The upper passage juxtaposes the two senses of explanatory priority; the second half is more argumentative than a literal transcription and needs close reinspection.

**Coverage warning:** No complete all-register translation is claimed for I:97.

## I:98 — Why the proponents of possibility consider their proof stronger
*Original printed Volume I:98 · physical source PDF 111 · provisional mixed upper; lower separately marked; no independent proof.*

### Upper register — source-inspected working English

As for the **demonstration**, the argument of those who say that **possibility** is the ground of causal need appears more readily acceptable. Its force is that **dependence holds of the possible thing in itself**, even if one sets aside the particular judgment of a person making the inference. One must accordingly locate the explanation of this dependence in what differentiates the possible from the necessary and the impossible.

The possible is not like what is necessary through itself, nor like what is impossible through itself. The equality of its relation to the two sides calls for something that determines which side obtains. This is what the proponents of possibility mean by the need for a cause. On this reasoning, the existence of a possible thing cannot be taken to follow from the mere quiddity, while the absence of any determining condition is still supposed.

The upper comparison is careful: it is **possibility that displays the indication of dependence most directly**, not an assertion that every possible thing is already in an actual state of preparation for being made. [Its specific objections are developed on I:99.]

### Arabic anchors (selected words; not a full transcription)

وأما الإثبات فكلام القائلين بأن المحوج هو الإمكان أظهر بالقبول ؛ لأن الاحتياج ثابت للممكن في نفس الأمر ؛ مع قطع النظر عن حكم حاكم ؛ في الممكن دون الواجب والممتنع

### Lower register — separate writer unassigned

**Marginal qualification (source-checked short selection):** The lower writer questions the phrase “more apparent.” Showing that possibility is conceptually associated with need is different from showing that it is the real *cause of* that need. In particular, an argument from the general possible quiddity to actual causal dependence may be sound only if the possibility is taken in the precise sense excluding both intrinsic necessities. The remainder of the gloss critiques the use of necessity and possibility as if they were attributes inhering in a ready-existing substrate. [Untranslated clauses remain.]

**Reading caution:** A short, separately ruled lower note interrupts the long marginal text on this page. Its precise boundary has not yet been established.

**Coverage warning:** No complete all-register translation is claimed for I:98.

## I:99 — A possible existent can be dependent even if supposed beginningless
*Original printed Volume I:99 · physical source PDF 112 · provisional mixed upper; lower separately marked; no independent proof.*

### Upper register — source-inspected working English

The *possibility* of a thing can be considered **without temporal origination**. Even were one to suppose the possible existent to be beginningless, it would not cease to require an external determining cause, because its quiddity still does not explain why it exists. The inference to dependence therefore does not rest exclusively on its having once been absent.

For this reason the author describes the argument of those who make possibility the ground of need as stronger or more immediately acceptable, reversing the order of preference in the presentation under discussion from the *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid*. An objection is then reported: **if the ground of need is possibility, why would the possible leave its state of nonexistence through a cause?** If possibility persists whether it exists or not, the critic says, that condition alone does not seem to explain the occurrence of existence at one time rather than another.

The reply distinguishes the general fact that the possible **needs another** from the particular determination that **existence rather than nonexistence is produced**. The first is traced to its possibility; the second involves the causal determination of the relevant side. A cause is not made redundant merely because possibility as such continues to belong to the thing. [The objection and its answer lead into I:100's account of different temporal senses of need.]

### Arabic anchors (selected words; not a full transcription)

الإمكان بدون الحدوث لاحتياج الممكن إلى مرجح خارج وإن فرض قدمه ؛ المحوج هو الإمكان ؛ خروج حالة العدم ؛ فيقدم تحصيل الحاصل بحصول سابق

### Lower register — separate writer unassigned

**Unassigned lower writer — only selected terms and argument so far:** The gloss considers whether possibility considered generally can explain *when* the possible exists and whether positing an eternal possible cause entails an eternal effect. It distinguishes the reality of the possibility that is being predicated from the additional determination of one of its sides. The long text has not yet been translated clause by clause.

**Reading caution:** The last Arabic phrase of the objection is compressed in this scan. English brackets the inferred argument instead of asserting a certain reading for every intervening word.

**Coverage warning:** No complete all-register translation is claimed for I:99.

## I:100 — Need at origination and need during continued existence
*Original printed Volume I:100 · physical source PDF 113 · provisional mixed upper; lower separately marked; no independent proof.*

### Upper register — source-inspected working English

The author's question now concerns **the time at which an effect needs an efficient cause**. Does the need concern the occurrence of existence **initially**, the cessation of nonexistence, or the effect's **continuing to exist**? Alternatively, can “need” mean its dependence on the continued presence of a relevant condition or the absence of an opposing one?

The point of the distinction is not to assert a further existence added to the existence of the effect. **“Existence”** and **“nonexistence”** are the two relevant sides; one may consider the condition for bringing a thing from nonexistence into existence, and one may separately ask what it is to persist once existence has been obtained. The question whether the continuing thing needs its cause must be stated in a way that does not demand that the original production be repeated on every occasion as though it had never occurred.

If need **during persistence** is explained as the dependence of the current state upon what maintains or determines it, that is not the same as describing a new production after prior nonexistence. The text insists on keeping the senses separate before drawing an inference about the possibility and origination accounts. [The statement continues into I:101.]

### Arabic anchors (selected words; not a full transcription)

معنى الاحتياج إلى المؤثر ؛ إما وقت الوجود أو العدم ابتداء ؛ أو توقف استمرارهما حال البقاء على أمر ما ؛ وجود العلة وانتفاؤها ؛ حال المقابلين في البقاء

### Lower register — separate writer unassigned

**Lower text:** The note on this page distinguishes what is being said of **the cause** from what is being said of **the effect** at the point of origination and at the point of persistence. It asks whether the proposed expression “the time of need” names a real interval preceding an effect or a conceptual distinction among conditions. The full marginal examination has not been collated here.

**Reading caution:** Printed I:100 is part of a continuous argument reaching I:101. The short alternatives in parentheses are translated provisionally, not all authorially disambiguated.

**Coverage warning:** No complete all-register translation is claimed for I:100.

## I:101 — Persistence and the two sides of the possible
*Original printed Volume I:101 · physical source PDF 114 · provisional mixed upper; lower separately marked; no independent proof.*

### Upper register — source-inspected working English

[Continuing:] The alleged need at the state of persistence is explained by considering whether continued **existence** or continued **nonexistence** depends upon the relevant determining condition. The intended need is a need with respect to the state in question; it does not simply refer to a second origination of the thing after it has already come to exist.

The argument turns again to the general point that the **existence of the possible and its nonexistence are equal in relation to its essence**. If the intellect considers the possible without regard to external determination, neither side has intrinsic priority. The supposed preference of one side therefore calls for a determinant. This is the ground on which those who make *possibility* the cause of need argue, whether one considers initial occurrence or persistence.

**“The claim”** under discussion may also be expressed by saying that the mind apprehends the possible as not intrinsically requiring existence or nonexistence, while the actual obtaining of one side demands something further. The text then asks how far this principle extends: must every possible side be produced by a further cause in exactly the same manner? [The ensuing distinction appears at I:102.]

### Arabic anchors (selected words; not a full transcription)

معنى الاحتياج حال البقاء إما توقف الوجود أو العدم المفروض في الحال ؛ وجود الممكن وعدمه بالنظر إلى ذاته سواء ؛ الإمكان ؛ سلب ضرورة الوجود

### Lower register — separate writer unassigned

**Marginal selection (not complete):** The distinction between an individual positive effect and the negation of that effect creates a special problem. If both sides of the possible are said to need causes in the same sense, one may seem to require an efficient producer even of nonexistence. The gloss seeks to distinguish a cause that produces an actuality from an explanatory condition under which an absence obtains. More follows in the original lower text.

**Reading caution:** Main text refers back to the two senses of the causal need proposed on I:100. Technical clauses in the second half require independent verification.

**Coverage warning:** No complete all-register translation is claimed for I:101.

---

## I:102 — The absence of intrinsic priority, and selection of one side
*Original printed Volume I:102 · physical source PDF 115 · provisional mixed upper; lower separately marked; no independent proof.*

### Upper register — source-inspected working English

**“The absence of intrinsic priority”** means that neither side of a possible thing obtains through the essence itself. If the side of existence is to be preferred, something must give it priority; likewise, the absence of existence does not become intrinsic necessity merely because the thing has not occurred. But the author's argument distinguishes what is known through **the bare possibility** from what is additionally required to specify **a particular event or its time**.

There can be a distinction between the thing's being possible in the absolute sense and its possible **occurrence at a given time**. The latter involves a specified instance of the side of existence; to explain that instance one considers the conditions by which the effect is actually brought about. The presence of those conditions does not turn the quiddity, considered simply in itself, into something intrinsically necessary.

The following objection asks whether a preference at a particular time can be admitted **without an independent external specifier**. If such preference is allowed, will it not conflict with the earlier maxim against preferring one equal side without a preferrer? The response develops by distinguishing an original condition from a fresh, successive cause. [Continued I:103.]

### Arabic anchors (selected words; not a full transcription)

انتفاء الأولوية الذاتية ؛ كون الطرف الذي له الأولوية طرف آخر ؛ يكون بسبب مرجح ؛ وقوع الطرف الأول موقوفا على عدم ذلك السبب ؛ لا يكون أولى بالنظر إلى ذات الممكن

### Lower register — separate writer unassigned

**Lower commentary:** The gloss asks whether absolute possibility and temporal preparedness are being conflated once more. It distinguishes the mere logical non-necessity of an event from the additional readiness of a particular subject to receive it under the requisite conditions. The source writer questions whether invoking the general modal relation, by itself, explains a temporally determinate occurrence. [This is a selected argumentative account; many lines remain untranslated.]

**Reading caution:** Some Arabic is compressed around the parenthesized arguments. Exact wording of the temporal case remains open to re-reading.

**Coverage warning:** No complete all-register translation is claimed for I:102.

---

## I:103 — Why a further preferrer cannot always be demanded
*Original printed Volume I:103 · physical source PDF 116 · provisional mixed upper; lower separately marked; no independent proof.*

### Upper register — source-inspected working English

[The first alternative:] if the preference of one side over the other took place **without an external cause**, one might say that the side had priority solely through the essence. But a preference of this sort cannot simply be asserted, since the subject is the possible thing whose nature, by supposition, is indifferent between the two sides.

On the other alternative, a **further determining factor** is demanded for the preference itself. The author considers the threat of a regress if every determining factor must in turn be made to occur by another, distinct factor in precisely the same way. This demands care over what is being counted as an effect and what as the determining relation. It does not suffice to declare the occurrence of a side inexplicable merely by saying that the other side was previously absent.

The printed argument distinguishes what belongs to the thing in itself from the way a cause specifies its existence **at a particular time**, and from what merely presupposes its prior nonexistence. **Earlier nonexistence**, in itself, is not a positive efficient agent. [The concluding contrast concerning the absence of a previously existing side passes to I:104.]

### Arabic anchors (selected words; not a full transcription)

الذات أحد الطرفين دون الآخر ؛ لا بد من انتهاء إلى حد الوجود ؛ أمر خارج عن الذات ؛ امتناع الترجيح بلا مرجح ؛ وقبل العدم أولى بالأعراض السالبة

### Lower register — separate writer unassigned

**Lower register:** The writer distinguishes between an actual causal source and an attributed “priority” that may only be a relation between the two sides of a judgment. A further condition is not automatically a distinct, additional efficient cause, and therefore the alleged regress has to be formulated with precision. This records the dispute's central question, but it is not a complete translation of the long lower passage.

**Reading caution:** Technical sentence concerning the first alternative is partially damaged/compressed; this working English is not certified word for word.

**Coverage warning:** No complete all-register translation is claimed for I:103.

---

## I:104 — Temporal beginning versus the general causal need of the possible
*Original printed Volume I:104 · physical source PDF 117 · provisional mixed upper; lower separately marked; no independent proof.*

### Upper register — source-inspected working English

[Continuing:] the argument uses the **distinction between the occurrence of existence and its absence**, but neither time nor a prior absence should be taken as though it were itself an efficient cause. An assertion of an event's not existing at one time and existing at another must be distinguished from an explanation of why the relevant side is specified.

The commentator considers the need for another as arising from the possible thing's **not requiring either side through itself**. The side of existence is possible, and its absence is likewise not excluded by the bare quiddity. If one asks whether the effect needs the cause only **at its first occurrence** or also while it continues, the answer depends on whether one means initial production or dependence more generally.

In this debate the expression **“need for another”** must not be assumed to mean only movement out of a preceding state of nonexistence. On the other hand, if one asserts a continuing need while refusing every real determination beyond the quiddity, the assertion risks leaving unexplained how the one side obtains rather than the other.

The last printed contrast is drawn between a thing's **not being able to exist without the cause** and the separate claim that a cause must produce a *new* beginning at each moment. The next source leaf, I:105, completes the argument by relating this to the impossibility of preference without a preferrer. [I:105 is preserved as following context and not counted as translated here.]

### Arabic anchors (selected words; not a full transcription)

وجود الممكن محفوف بوجودين ؛ أحدهما الأول سابق على وجوده ؛ والثاني لاحق له ؛ انتفاء الأولوية الذاتية ؛ ما يجب

### Lower register — separate writer unassigned

**Lower register:** The separate note scrutinizes an argument that treats a first existence and the absence preceding it as distinct explanatory entities. The question is whether the purported plurality belongs to the world or only to our analysis of an event's temporal description. The full lower rebuttal has not been completed.

**Reading caution:** I:104 is continuous with I:105; an uncertain bracket covers the compressed causal-status contrast, not a supplied ending absent from the source.

**Coverage warning:** No complete all-register translation is claimed for I:104.

# Continuation: Volume I:105–110

## I:105 — Could a specified event occur without a new preferrer?
*Printed I:105 · physical PDF 118 · mixed upper first-pass source reading; lower separately identified; not independently certified.*

### Upper — first-pass English

[Continuing I:104:] The demand for an external determinant of one side does not mean that, whenever an actual effect occurs, the whole condition that makes it preferable must itself begin **at the same instant** as the effect. The text is opposing an argument which, from the absence of an additional preferrer at a specified time, infers the complete impossibility of the occurrence.

If neither of two sides had any priority at all, then the obtaining of one side rather than the other would indeed constitute the preference of an equal alternative **without any preferrer**. But the disputants disagree over what counts as the relevant priority. One view takes the continued specification supplied by a choosing cause to be enough; another argues that the occurrence of existence **at this moment rather than at another** demands an additional determinate condition. The absence of such a distinct new condition does not by itself prove that the act is without a preferrer.

The upper passage distinguishes the **absence of a fresh temporal cause** from the **absence of any determining factor**. It also warns against simply assuming the side of nonexistence has intrinsic priority and therefore making the transition to existence unintelligible by definition. [The line concluding the discussion of alternatives continues at I:106.]

### Arabic anchors (not a full original transcription)

لم يوجد امتناع الترجيح بلا مرجح ؛ اللازم على تقدير عدم الانتهاء إلى حد الوجود ؛ لاحد الطرفين أولوية ؛ المرجح

### Lower — different historical writer; author not verified

**Lower passage — a selected challenge, writer unassigned.** A need for a preferrer must be distinguished from a need for a newly originated preferrer. If a choosing agent can fix a particular time for an event, the argument cannot exclude that explanation merely by asking for another change immediately before the time chosen. Conversely, a temporal selection cannot be given explanatory force by merely renaming it “priority.” The printed writer develops further objections regarding whether the condition that selects one time is itself necessary or contingent; that continuous lower argument is not yet completely translated.

**Reading caution:** The beginning and end of this leaf are mid-argument. Some source-internal connective references are only approximately rendered.

**Completeness warning:** The whole printed lower register and certain upper clauses remain unfinished.

---

## I:106 — A preference, and the impossibility of combining existence and nonexistence
*Printed I:106 · physical PDF 119 · mixed upper first-pass source reading; lower separately identified; not independently certified.*

### Upper — first-pass English

[Continuing:] if one side is to obtain at its appointed time, the act of preferring it must not be portrayed as a preference that lacks a preferrer. The next argument refers to the **impossibility of joining the contradictory sides**, existence and nonexistence, in one subject under the same consideration.

**“The choice”** is explained as the preference of the side brought about by a selecting agent. The author then distinguishes the impossibility of **simultaneous existence and nonexistence** from a demand that the possible entity, considered in itself, be intrinsically determined to one side. The choosing relation cannot be treated as if it were the same thing as the logical impossibility of a conjunction of contradictories.

He next turns back to **necessity, impossibility and possibility**, considered as kinds of modal description. They belong to the argument in different ways: a thing's possible quiddity can remain unchanged when external causes bring it into being, while a proposition about the actual occurrence at a given time may be necessary under a fully specified condition. [The discussion introduces definitions of what is *ancient* and what is *originated*, continued on I:107.]

### Arabic anchors (not a full original transcription)

امتناع الجمع بين الوجود والعدم ؛ وهذا الإنفاق الاختيار ؛ ثلاثة أي الوجوب والامتناع والإمكان ؛ بل كلما

### Lower — different historical writer; author not verified

**Lower passage not completely translated.** The margin distinguishes an external selector from the mere impossibility of asserting contradiction. One may not infer from the law of noncontradiction that an intrinsically possible thing has a naturally preferred side. The printed note also comments on the meaning of modal predicates as intellectual considerations. Remaining clauses await a direct source collation.

**Reading caution:** The printed phrase concerning the choosing act and the first modal description is compressed; the English is an explicitly provisional connected rendering.

**Completeness warning:** The whole printed lower register and certain upper clauses remain unfinished.

---

## I:107 — Ancient and originated: a mode of being, not a separate species
*Printed I:107 · physical PDF 120 · mixed upper first-pass source reading; lower separately identified; not independently certified.*

### Upper — first-pass English

The text now considers the expressions **“ancient”** (*qadīm*) and **“originated”** (*ḥādith*). When a thing is described as ancient, this may be understood as something whose existence **has no beginning**. It is not thereby established that there must be an additional independently existing reality called ancientness attached to it. Nor does every use of the word make what is ancient a distinct species of being.

Likewise, an originated thing is described in relation to the **nonexistence preceding its existence**. The author draws attention to different usages of the words and the scope of the description. A thing can be described as ancient or originated **according to its own essence** or under a condition relating it to another; the two classifications must not be confused.

The discussion also warns against converting **ancientness** into a positive, separable attribute whose subject must first be found. What is being asserted may only be the denial of a beginning. **“Origination”**, conversely, can designate the event's obtaining existence after its absence. [The next page develops the relations between these predicates and the subject's quiddity.]

### Arabic anchors (not a full original transcription)

وصفا أي قديم ؛ عمومه ؛ كالقدم ؛ قدم الذات ؛ والحدوث ؛ وجود الحادث ؛ الموصوف بالقدم

### Lower — different historical writer; author not verified

**Marginal note:** One objection asks whether, if “ancient” and “originated” apply to existence, they are being taken as constitutive types or merely predicates of an existent under the relevant temporal relation. The printed discussion treats individuation and universal predication separately from the denial or affirmation of a temporal beginning. [Only the central disputed distinction is recorded here.]

**Reading caution:** The text distinguishes a universal description from a concrete existent; part of the printed line on the first usage is not fully resolved.

**Completeness warning:** The whole printed lower register and certain upper clauses remain unfinished.

---

## I:108 — Predication, rational consideration, and whether ancientness is a positive property
*Printed I:108 · physical PDF 121 · mixed upper first-pass source reading; lower separately identified; not independently certified.*

### Upper — first-pass English

[The argument continues:] if **ancientness** were something positive, existing alongside its subject as a distinct accompaniment, it would itself require an explanation of its own status. One would have to ask whether that accompaniment was likewise ancient and whether an endless sequence of predicates or accompanying realities followed. A negative meaning such as **“not preceded by nonexistence”** does not by itself require such an independently subsisting extra item.

Similarly, the description of a subject as **characterized by** ancientness or origination must be distinguished from the supposition that these attributes are additional existences outside the intellect. Some relevant expressions designate **intellectual considerations** (*iʿtibārāt ʿaqliyya*), and their ascription to the thing need not imply an external accident inhering in it.

If the thing's **existence** is necessarily connected with a particular predicate, that still does not show that the predicate and existence are two separate positive realities. The author tests the distinction between what belongs to the reality itself and what comes from the mind's mode of considering its relation to existence or nonexistence. [The sentence concludes on I:109.]

### Arabic anchors (not a full original transcription)

والتعين ؛ والموصوفية ؛ اعتبارات عقلية ؛ والأزلية ؛ في الأمور الوجودية ؛ لا يجوز أن يكون وجود الوجود

### Lower — different historical writer; author not verified

**Lower register:** A substantial note takes issue with multiplying existing attributes whenever a subject can correctly be described by several notions. It contrasts an objectively true predication with an additional component actually present in the object. The argument is incomplete here and cannot yet serve as a fully verified translation of the lower page.

**Reading caution:** This is an issue-sensitive rendering of the upper reasoning, not a complete literal transcription of the dense parenthetical syntax.

**Completeness warning:** The whole printed lower register and certain upper clauses remain unfinished.

---

## I:109 — Different senses of priority: outside the mind and in the intellect
*Printed I:109 · physical PDF 122 · mixed upper first-pass source reading; lower separately identified; not independently certified.*

### Upper — first-pass English

If the description were attributed to the subject merely because we regard it in a certain way, one must distinguish that mental mode of comparison from what is **affirmed of the object outside the mind**. The passage asks whether **ancientness and origination** are states that obtain in external reality or whether the mind's consideration of before and after introduces the relevant distinction.

The author replies by distinguishing **the being of the subject in the external world** from the **conceptual form** by which the mind judges it. Something can truly be said to possess existence in the world although the concept of existence is formed in the mind. Conversely, simply conceiving it as having a particular temporal predicate does not give that predicate an independent external existence.

The resulting judgment must specify the intended sense of **“before”**. A priority of being is not interchangeable with a priority of the intellect's consideration, nor with priority according to time. The author returns to the terms **ancientness** and **origination**, and their application to the reality, to avoid treating their temporal meaning as a separable property. [The last phrase carries over to I:110.]

### Arabic anchors (not a full original transcription)

معنى كون الذي واجبا ؛ في الخارج ؛ بحسب إذا عقل ؛ إلى الوجود ؛ الحاصل له قبل الأذهان ؛ القدم والحدوث

### Lower — different historical writer; author not verified

**Marginal register:** The gloss objects that a predication's being *true outside the mind* need not imply a positive extra constituent called “ancientness.” It also asks whether the time relation used to describe the thing existed before it was conceptualized, and what notion of priority is presupposed. The remainder of the dense argument has not been independently collated.

**Reading caution:** Some words near the first division of senses are uncertain. Do not treat the language about external being as a separate modern metaphysical thesis.

**Completeness warning:** The whole printed lower register and certain upper clauses remain unfinished.

---

## I:110 — Intrinsic and temporal ancientness, and what “preceded by nonexistence” means
*Printed I:110 · physical PDF 123 · mixed upper first-pass source reading; lower separately identified; not independently certified.*

### Upper — first-pass English

There are two relevant senses of **ancientness**. **Intrinsic ancientness** is understood as not being preceded by nonexistence in a sense concerning the essence itself. **Temporal ancientness** means not having a temporal nonexistence before it. The distinction matters because a beginninglessness understood under one respect does not straightforwardly establish beginninglessness under another.

Likewise, what is **originated** can be described by reference to a **preceding nonexistence**, but this expression admits an intrinsic and a temporal use. The *intrinsically originated* is that whose essence, considered in itself, does not entail existence. Temporal origination refers to nonexistence before the actual beginning of the thing. On this division, an event may be contingent in itself without its temporal starting point being the sole cause of its dependence.

The author notes that what is considered ancient or originated in these respects must not be defined simply by a number of elapsed years. Nor is the temporal sense to be confused with the philosophical assertion that every possible thing must have a measurable beginning. The terminology is deployed in the continuing dispute about whether a possibly beginningless created order would remain dependent upon a cause.

[The last upper phrase introduces the contrast with the Muʿtazilites and continues on I:111. The remaining lower text on I:110 has not been translated in full.]

### Arabic anchors (not a full original transcription)

قديما وزمانيا ؛ القدم المسبوقية بالغير ؛ عدم المسبوقية بالعدم ؛ الحدوث ؛ بالعدم الزماني ؛ القدم الذاتي

### Lower — different historical writer; author not verified

**Lower text — partial selection only.** The marginal writer distinguishes a thing's intrinsic dependence upon its causal ground from being preceded by an interval of time. Something can have a cause without the explanation being a temporal sequence. The writer also questions the application of “nonexistence before existence” where the alleged before is not temporal. [Specific counterexamples and a long polemical sequence remain untranslated.]

**Reading caution:** This division is a working source reading; the exact school attribution on the following page is deliberately not added before inspecting I:111.

**Completeness warning:** The whole printed lower register and certain upper clauses remain unfinished.


---
