# New direct-source, denser translation: I:111–116

## Original I:111 · physical PDF 124

### Upper text — continuation of the debate over what can be eternal

**U111.01 — «ووصف بالقدم ما سوى الله تعالى»**

[The text discusses] describing something other than God Most High as beginningless. The expression is rejected at least according to its apparent import, as the author explains by citing cases where others have used *beginninglessness* of more than one thing.

**U111.02 — «وزعم المعتزلة كثيرا من الأحوال»**

The Muʿtazilites, it is alleged, posited numerous *states* (*aḥwāl*). In particular they affirmed four states in relation to God: His being knowing, powerful, living and existent (*al-ʿālimiyya, al-qādiriyya, al-ḥayyiyya, al-mawjūdiyya*). They claimed that these were established with the divine essence from eternity.

**U111.03 — «وأثبت أبو هاشم منهم حالة خامسة»**

Abū Hāshim, from among them, affirmed a fifth state beyond those states common to the discussion of the essence: namely *divinity* (*al-ilāhiyya*). [The concise source phrase describing the relation of these states to the essence needs a second lexical check.]

**U111.04 — «وأجيب من جانب المعتزلة بأن القديم موجود لا أول له»**

The answer given on behalf of the Muʿtazilites is that an *eternal* thing, in the strict sense being discussed, is something **existent without a beginning**. The states they affirm are, on their own account, not described as possessing existence. Consequently they do not become eternal entities merely by being said to be established with the essence.

**U111.05 — «لكن الكلام في المعنى المشهور»**

But the issue is the common or familiar meaning [of beginninglessness], rather than that restricted technical definition. The restriction therefore does not by itself settle the dispute.

**U111.06 — «وعند الفلاسفة كثير»**

According to the philosophers, too, there are many things held to be without beginning. They claim that the intellects, the celestial spheres and other things, according to the details to be explained later—God Most High willing—are eternal.

**U111.07 — «وليس هذا القديم إلى المختار» [reading of connector provisional]**

[The point is that what is beginningless cannot be] an effect proceeding from a freely choosing agent **as an effect of that agent’s act of choice**. The argument is not that God lacks power over an eternal object; it concerns what the expression *an effect issuing from choice* entails.

**U111.08 — «لأن فعل المختار مسبوق بالقصد»**

For the act of a choosing agent is preceded by an intention. The intention in question is an intention **to produce** the thing, not merely a general awareness of it or the possession of power with respect to it.

**U111.09 — «القصد إلى الإيجاد يقارن العدم»**

An intention directed to bringing something into existence is accompanied by that thing’s **nonexistence**; what is already obtained is not, under the same description, the object of an intention to *bring it about for the first time*.

**U111.10 — «ضرورة» / «لا يجوز أن يكون تقدم القصد»**

The author presents this as necessary: the intention cannot be conceived as directed toward first producing what is already existent. The next leaf, with an extensive independent marginal intervention, examines the qualifications needed for that argument.

### Lower register — an independent critical gloss, continuing onto I:112

**L111.01 — «حتى إنهم نفوا القدم الزماني عما سوى ذات الله تعالى»**

The Muʿtazilites pursued divine unity to the point of denying temporal beginninglessness to anything other than God’s essence; they also did not affirm additional eternal attributes.

**L111.02 — «هذا جواب مأخوذ من كلام نصير الطوسي نصرة للمعتزلة»**

This reply [about states not being existing entities] is taken from the discussion of Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī in defence of the Muʿtazilites. The accusation of a plurality of eternals depends on what is being counted as *existent* rather than merely *established*.

**L111.03 — «فإنهم يفرقون بين الوجود والثبوت»**

For they distinguish existence (*wujūd*) from establishment (*thubūt*), and do not make the aforementioned states existent things. Their states therefore cannot simply be included in a count of eternally existing entities.

**L111.04 — «فلا ... لزوم تعدد القدماء الموجودة»**

Accordingly, the attempted refutation does not impose on them the multiplication of eternal **existents** in the manner alleged. The difficulty arises when the opponent changes from their technical use of existence to a different and broader meaning of *being established*.

**L111.05 — «لا واسطة بين الوجود والعدم في الواقع»**

[On the view considered by the glossator,] there is no intermediary between existence and nonexistence *in reality*. On that view the opponent could insist that the states are really existent, and thus reintroduce the charge of plurality. But that objection employs premises the parties do not share; it does not state the Muʿtazilites’ position in their own terms.

**L111.06 — «لكن الكلام في المعنى المشهور ليس على ما ينبغي»**

Hence the commentator’s remark, “But the question concerns the common meaning,” is not, in this writer’s judgment, quite as it should be. One must distinguish what the Muʿtazilites actually claim from the conclusion pressed upon them by an opponent.

**L111.07 — «من الهيولى والصورة الجسمية والنوعية»**

The other entities counted by the philosophers include *prime matter*, the *form of corporeality*, and *specific substantial forms*. The list is not exhausted by separate intellects and celestial spheres.

**L111.08 — «إن القصد إنما يتوجه إلى تحصيل ما ليس بحاصل»**

On the assertion that intention is directed only toward obtaining what is not yet obtained, the glossator reports the *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid*: philosophers and theologians commonly agree upon this point, and to quarrel about the impossibility of producing what is already produced is ordinarily to dispute something obvious. Nevertheless, a qualification attributed to al-Āmidī must be examined. **The marginal argument continues directly onto I:112.**

---

## Original I:112 · physical PDF 125

**Physical and authorial boundary:** this photographed leaf has **no fresh upper Sanandajī passage**. It is a long **lower-register continuation** of the previous argument. It must not be counted as an additional upper commentary page.

### Continuing lower register — intention and the already obtained

**L112.01 — «كسبق الإيجاد إيجابا في جواز كونهما بالذات دون الزمان»**

The preceding claim [about intention] must be considered alongside the possibility that *the necessitating ground* and what follows from it have a priority relation **by essence and not by time**. The latter kind of priority does not by itself establish two distinct moments.

**L112.02 — «وفي جواز كون أثرهما قديما»**

On that hypothesis, it has been argued that the effect too may be beginningless. This is cited in the mode of an objection, not adopted as the glossator’s unqualified conclusion.

**L112.03 — «ولا يخفى أن الفريقين وإن اتفقوا ...»**

Even if the two sides agree that the *beginningless* cannot be an effect of a choosing agent **under the relevant conception of action**, there remains a real disagreement concerning the Necessary Existent and the production of the effect. The philosophers may acknowledge divine necessity while denying that the effect must begin in time; the theologians challenge that consequence.

**L112.04 — «فالفلاسفة على أنه تعالى موجب والصادر منه قديم»**

The philosophers maintain [in the account being discussed] that God Most High is a necessitating cause and that what issues from Him is eternal. The theologians maintain that He is a choosing agent and that the effect issuing from Him is originated.

**L112.05 — «لم يجز ... الإشارة إلى ما نقله الآمدي»**

Thus the objection to the earlier claim cannot simply be disposed of by appealing to what al-Āmidī was reported to have said. The glossator distinguishes the *fact that the end is attained* from the *act by which a particular agent brings that end about*.

**L112.06 — «إنما الممتنع تحصيل الحاصل بتحصيل آخر»**

What is impossible, in the relevant strict formulation, is to **obtain something already obtained by obtaining it again as the same effect under the same respect**. Merely forming an intention directed toward an end already achieved [by another route or under another description] is a different issue. The printed objection is concerned with whether the two have been improperly identified.

**L112.07 — «فلا امتناع في مفارقة التحصيل والتأثير للمحصول والموجود»**

It therefore does not automatically follow that the obtaining and the action producing it are inseparable in every sense from the result that has already been obtained. One must distinguish the resulting state, the causal action and the agent’s direction toward that state.

**L112.08 — «والتأثير كما هو ظاهر ولا شبهة»**

The two parties’ agreement about *producing* what is already produced is obvious; yet their apparent agreement about *intending to obtain* it has to be stated more carefully. Otherwise the objection is answered by changing the sense of “obtaining” midway through the argument.

**L112.09 — «اتفاقهم على امتناع مع توجه القصد إلى تحصيل الحاصل»**

The difficulty is now explicit: it was previously reported that they agree upon the impossibility of intention directed to producing something already produced, and that intention toward production must accompany the object’s nonexistence. How can that report be reconciled with the concession just made?

**L112.10 — «والجواب أن تحصيل الحاصل غير توجه القصد إلى تحصيله»**

The answer is that **actually producing the already produced** is not the same as **the intention directed toward its production**. Granting the possibility of one does not contradict agreement about the impossibility of the other. [The pronoun attached to “one” in the compact original is a local reading to verify.]

**L112.11 — «ورده المنع بأنه لم يجوز القصد إلى تحصيل الحاصل»**

The counterreply denies that the earlier writer really allowed intention *to produce the already produced* in the same sense in which producing it is prohibited. The apparent disagreement may therefore rest on how the intention’s object is specified.

**L112.12 — «كما يجوز تحصيله بأن يكون التقدم بالذات»**

A further possibility is that the relevant precedence is **essential rather than temporal**. As noted earlier, something may be prior to another by nature or causal dependence without occupying an earlier moment of time.

**L112.13 — «القصد إلى الإيجاد يقارن العدم ... لا يستلزم كون سبق الإيجاد سبقا بالزمان»**

The contention that an intention to produce something is considered together with its nonexistence does **not**, without a further argument, prove that intention and production must be separated by temporal succession. The asserted *priority* still needs to be classified.

**L112.14 — «إنما يكون بالذات لو كان الإيجاب دون القصد والاختيار»**

Conversely, if one removes intention and choice from the proposed production altogether, one has returned to a **necessitating** cause. That is precisely the view from which the theologians intended to distinguish divine agency; it cannot be imported back silently as the explanation of their position.

**L112.15 — «فهذا إنما يمكن ... لو كان الإيجاب دون القصد والاختيار»**

The gloss concludes this stage by testing whether an eternal effect can be maintained under an account involving no intention or choice. It leaves the theological opponent’s argument intact unless the supposed transition from eternal will to temporal effect has been explained. **The discussion continues on I:113.**

---

## Original I:113 · physical PDF 126

### Upper text — completion of the discussion of choice

**U113.01 — «على الإيجاد كتقدم الإيجاد على الوجود في انصياح بحسب الذات بل نقول»**

[The disputed priority] is like the priority of bringing-into-existence to the resultant existence, insofar as the former has priority **by essence**. But, the author says, the matter must be put more carefully: this analogy by itself does not establish temporal precedence.

**U113.02 — «إذا كان القصد كافيا في وجود المقصود»**

If the intention is sufficient for the existence of what is intended, and the intention occurs at the same time as that intended thing, one still needs to state how the act’s occurrence is to be understood in relation to its object.

**U113.03 — «وإن لم يكن كافيا فقد تقدم عليه زمانا»**

If it is **not** sufficient, then [on the formulation under examination] it has preceded the thing **in time**. The two alternatives are deployed to contest an unexplained appeal to the simultaneity of will and effect.

**U113.04 — «وليمكن عدمه أي عدم القديم لكونه واجبا»**

Its nonexistence—namely, the nonexistence of the alleged beginningless thing—would have to be possible. Yet the discussion introduces the case where this is impossible because what is beginningless is said to be **necessary**.

**U113.05 — «وامتناع عدمه ظاهر ... أي إلى الواجب إيجابا»**

The impossibility of its nonexistence is evident under that hypothesis, and thus the argument turns to what proceeds from the Necessary Existent **by necessitation**. Whether that is a legitimate model of free divine choice remains the disputed issue. [The short upper paragraph’s final phrase joins I:114.]

### Lower register — two separate printed notes

**L113.01 — «قوله كافيا في وجود المقصود ...»**

On the phrase “sufficient for the existence of what is intended”: if God Most High intended the originated thing’s existence **in eternity**, it would follow—under the argument being criticized—that the originated thing too had to be beginningless. If the intention were sufficient, its object could not fail to be present.

**L113.02 — «فلو كان المقصود كافيا لزم قدم الحادث هذا خلف»**

Hence the intended object could not be treated as present from eternity without contradiction, if the point being established is precisely that it is *originated*. This is the reductio of a proposal which conflates eternal intention with an eternally present effect. [This short note ends separately; a new ruled gloss begins.]

**L113.03 — «بل نقول إذا كان القصد الخ ... هذا رد تحقيقي»**

On the new formulation, “Rather we say, if the intention …”: this is a substantive counterargument, aimed at showing that the statement **“intention necessarily accompanies nonexistence”** is unsound if taken without qualification.

**L113.04 — «فإن من القصد والاختيار ما هو كاف في وجود المقصود»**

For some intentions and choices suffice for the existence of what is intended, while others do not. It is not enough to treat them all as temporally preceding their objects merely because the objects are specified by choice.

**L113.05 — «وهو لا يقارن العدم بل يكون مع المقصود ضرورة»**

When intention is sufficient, it accompanies the intended result, **not its nonexistence**; its object must then be present. If the intended result does not exist, some further condition for efficacy is missing.

**L113.06 — «وأما أن يراد في الجملة فلا يستلزم المدعى»**

If the claim is made only “in some respect” or “in general,” it does not establish the universal conclusion that is being asserted. The proof must specify the precise sense of intention and what counts as its effect.

**L113.07 — «إذا قيل إذا كان قصد الواجب بما لا يتخلف عنه أثره»**

It may be said: if the Necessary Existent’s intention is such that its effect cannot fail to follow, and one also supposes the divine attribute of intention to be eternal, then the effect would have to be eternal. The respondent replies that the temporal occurrence of the effect need not be described as another newly arising condition within God.

**L113.08 — «لكن ... المراد من قصده الكافي هو تعلق القصد وكفايته»**

The point is rather the **relation of the intention to its specific object** and its sufficiency with respect to that object. One must not count the intention, its relation, the existence of the object and the absence of that object as interchangeable items.

**L113.09 — «وهذا كما يتصور بعدم المقصود ...»**

The absence of the intended object can likewise be conceived while the question of how intention relates to it is still open. If the same intention is described as eternally effective and its effect as absent, a further explanation is required. Otherwise the proof begs the very question at issue.

**L113.10 — «لا يكون مضرّا بالدعوى المذكورة التي هي أن القديم لا يستند إلى المختار»**

The glossator limits the conclusion: this refinement does not automatically overthrow the claim being debated, namely that a beginningless effect cannot *in the relevant respect* be grounded in a choosing agent. It shows that the way that claim was argued needs correction. The last sentence continues onto I:114.

---

## Original I:114 · physical PDF 127

### Upper text — the nonexistence of the Necessary, then the claim of a time before every event

**U114.01 — «عدمه لأنه من مقتضيات الذات ولوازمه»**

Its nonexistence would be impossible, for the [alleged necessity] is among the entailments and necessary consequences of the essence.

**U114.02 — «لزم من إمكان عدمه عدم الواجب وهو محال»**

If its nonexistence were possible under that supposition, the nonexistence of the Necessary Existent would follow—and that is impossible. This closes the preceding branch of the reasoning.

**U114.03 — «وزعمت الفلاسفة أن كل حادث أي موجود بعد العدم مسبوق بالزمان»**

The philosophers claim that every *originated thing*—that is, everything which exists after nonexistence—is **preceded by time**. The source is reporting their position, not establishing that time itself must have a first moment.

**U114.04 — «وبنوا على ذلك قدم الزمان»**

From this they argue for the beginninglessness of time: if there were a first time, what is meant by the event’s being preceded by nonexistence would require clarification without already assuming a prior time.

**U114.05 — «وبينوا أن سبق عدم الشيء على وجوده لا يعقل إلا بالزمان»**

They argue that the precedence of a thing’s **nonexistence** over its **existence** can be understood only through time; the two cannot be ordered as earlier and later without a temporal measure.

**U114.06 — «ونبه ... على مذاهبوا إليه من أن أقسام ...»**

The author signals that these claims involve more than one sense of “before” and “after.” The next section enumerates the types of precedence; the language should not be restricted in advance to temporal precedence alone.

### Lower register — objection about divine choice and the eternity of time

**L114.01 — «كان يمتنع عدمه ...»**

The first marginal argument objects that if nonexistence is ruled out merely because something is an eternal effect of an eternal source, we must ask **what renders that effect necessary**. It is one thing to say God cannot cease to be, and another to say that an alleged effect must itself be without beginning.

**L114.02 — «لم لا يجوز أن يتوقف صدوره عن الواجب على حادث»**

Why may its proceeding from the Necessary Existent not be made to depend upon an **originated** condition? The objection is directed against the unqualified denial of such a dependence.

**L114.03 — «قيل ... إذا امتنع عدمه كان واجبا»**

It may be replied: if its nonexistence is impossible, it is necessary. Yet the writer then asks whether the reason for that necessity has already been assumed rather than demonstrated.

**L114.04 — «قالوا لما كان الواجب عند الأشاعرة ...»**

The gloss invokes the Ashʿarī treatment of the divine attributes: a thing can be spoken of as beginningless in relation to God without thereby making its mode of dependence identical with that of God’s essence. The precise scope of the claim about additional attributes is disputed.

**L114.05 — «فلا بالاختيار ... لا يكون شيء من معلولاته قديما»**

On one interpretation, if God produces through free choice, none of His effects can be eternal **as effects of such production**. The author of the marginal response notes that this is precisely the claim whose proof is being tested; restating it does not solve the problem.

**L114.06 — «لكن لقائل أن يقول قد سبق ...»**

A disputant can answer that some theologians already affirm eternal divine attributes. One must therefore be careful not to infer that every attribution of an eternal property to God entails an eternal effect separately originating from Him.

**L114.07 — «فإذا حمل الاحتياج المؤثر عندهم هو الحدوث لا الإمكان»**

The claim changes depending on whether the *ground of requiring an efficient cause* is considered to be **temporal origination** or **possibility**. On the first account, an eternal thing is not treated in precisely the same way as an originated effect; the second account requires a further distinction.

**L114.08 — «فإن كانت مفتقرة إلى ذاته تعالى ... لزوم الذات للآثار»**

If the attributes depend upon God’s essence, that dependence must not be equated without argument with the production of an external effect. Otherwise the necessary connection between the essence and the attributes would be misdescribed as a temporal process of producing what had not existed.

**L114.09 — «فالتأثير والتأثر إنما يكون بين المتغايرين»**

Action and reception of an action are ordinarily distinguished through the **difference between what acts and what is acted upon**. What relation does that distinction have to an eternal attribute that is not an external effect? The commentator leaves that question open for the following analysis.

**L114.10 — «أن الفلاسفة ادعوا قدم الزمان واستدلوا عليه»**

Regarding the philosophers’ argument for the eternity of time: they reason that every originated thing is preceded by nonexistence, and that this precedence is by time. A rejoinder insists that the alleged priority may be a **conceptual** or **non-temporal** priority.

**L114.11 — «كل حادث مسبوق بعدمه وكل مسبوق بالعدم مسبوق بالزمان»**

The argument is stated as two premises: **every originated thing is preceded by its own nonexistence**, and **everything preceded by nonexistence is preceded by time**. The second premise is precisely what requires proof.

**L114.12 — «العدم مسبوق بالزمان إذ لا يعقل ...»**

The marginal writer challenges the move from saying that absence is “prior” to saying that an **actual stretch of time** precedes the thing. Otherwise the proof for time’s eternity risks assuming what it purports to establish. [The final sentence joins I:115.]

---

## Original I:115 · physical PDF 128

### Upper text — five kinds of priority

**U115.01 — «إما أن تكون بالعلية أو بالطبع أو بالزمان أو بالشرف أو الرتبة»**

Priority may be **by causality**, **by nature**, **by time**, **by nobility**, or **by rank/order**. The divisions concern different meanings of *taqaddum* and must not be treated as interchangeable.

**U115.02 — «فالقدم والتأخر بالعلية كتقدم حركة اليد على حركة المفتاح»**

Priority and posteriority **by causality** are exemplified by the movement of a hand being prior to the movement of a key. The two may occur together in time, while one is the cause of the other.

**U115.03 — «وبالطبع هو تقدم المحتاج إليه على المحتاج لا بطريق العلية والتأثير»**

Priority **by nature** is the priority of that which is needed to the thing needing it, but *not* by way of efficient causation or the production of an effect.

**U115.04 — «وبكون بينهما اقتران واجتماع»**

Such terms can coexist or be together. Their conjunction in existence does not erase the dependence or ordering indicated by natural priority. [The example of part and whole is developed in the accompanying gloss.]

### Lower register — precise analysis of the five kinds

**L115.01 — «قال المصنف يبين بذلك ...»**

The author of the lower note explains that these divisions clarify the difference between kinds of being before and after. The operative contrast is whether two things must be successive in time or may coexist while remaining ordered in another way.

**L115.02 — «وقد يقال وجه الضبط هو أن التقدم والتأخر إن لم يجتمعا في الوجود فبالزمان»**

One proposed rule for organizing the list is that if the earlier and later terms **cannot coexist in existence**, their precedence is **temporal**. If they coexist, the division is determined by the type of relation between them.

**L115.03 — «وإن اجتمعا فإن كان بينهما ترتيب بحسب الاعتبار فالرتبة»**

If they coexist and their ordering is due to an arrangement considered under some reference point, then it is **priority by rank** (*rutba*), not temporal priority.

**L115.04 — «وإلا فإن لم يحتج المتأخر إلى المتقدم فبالشرف»**

If the later does not require the earlier, yet one is accorded higher worth or distinction, the priority is **by nobility or excellence** (*sharaf*).

**L115.05 — «وإن احتاج فإن كان بالتأثير فالعلية وإلا فبالطبع»**

If the later does need the earlier, and the dependence is one of actual **efficient influence**, the priority is **causal**. If the dependence is not by such efficacy, the priority is **natural**.

**L115.06 — «التقدم بالرتبة وبالشرف راجعان إلى الزمان ...»**

A further analysis says that priority by rank and priority by nobility can in some examples be described in terms of a temporal reference; but that does not make time the definition of either. Their intended classifications depend on the comparison being made.

**L115.07 — «تقدم مكان على آخر هو أن زمان الوصول إليه قبل زمان الوصول إلى الآخر»**

For instance, when one place is said to be “before” another along a route, the intended point may be that one reaches it earlier. That precedence arises from the ordering of **arrival times**, although the places themselves coexist.

**L115.08 — «ومعنى تقدم الجنس على النوع أن زمان الأخذ والشروع في ملاحظته قبل زمان الأخذ في النوع»**

Likewise, an example of a genus being prior to a species can be explained in terms of the order in which one begins to consider or apprehend them: attention to the genus occurs before attention to the particular species. This is one manner of explaining the comparison, not the only metaphysical meaning of generic priority.

**L115.09 — «وحركة اليد كحركة المفتاح ...»**

When the hand moves a key, the hand’s movement is said to come before the key’s movement **as cause**, not because two distinct, separated instants must be assigned to them. The modal account of what requires what differs from an account of clock time.

**L115.10 — «فالمتأخر منهما يكون محتاجا إلى المتقدم في التحقق من غير عكس»**

The later term depends upon the earlier for its actuality, but not conversely. This **asymmetry of dependence** explains why simultaneous things can nevertheless be called earlier and later in a causally relevant sense.

**L115.11 — «وأما بالنظر إلى وصف التقدم والتأخر ...»**

Yet if one considers only the **relations** of being earlier and being later, the two descriptions stand in correlation: an earlier thing is called earlier only with respect to a later one, and conversely. The existence of the causes is not thereby reduced to the existence of the correlative names.

**L115.12 — «ويكون بينهما اقتران ...»**

Consequently, saying that two ordered things are together does not refute causal or natural precedence. The meaning of their being “together” must be specified, and the analysis passes into I:116.

---

## Original I:116 · physical PDF 129

### Upper text — remaining examples and transition toward unity

**U116.01 — «على الكل ... وبالزمان كتقدم الأمس على الغد»**

[The part has a certain natural priority] to the whole; and priority **by time** is like the priority of *yesterday* to *tomorrow*.

**U116.02 — «وبالشرف كتقدم العالم على المتعلم»**

Priority **by nobility** is exemplified by the learned person’s priority over a learner, in the respect of knowledge or excellence, even though the learner may exist at the same time.

**U116.03 — «وبالرتبة بأن يكون المتقدم أقرب إلى مبدأ محدود»**

Priority **by rank** is obtained when the earlier term is nearer to a specified beginning or point of reference.

**U116.04 — «وهي إما الحسّية»**

That ranking may be *sensible*—as in spatial arrangement—or *intellectual*, as when intelligible items are placed in a determinate order.

**U116.05 — «أو العقلية»**

Or the ordering may be **intellectual**, judged by the intellect because the things concerned are intelligible. The following example concerns the precedence of some questions or topics within a science to others, when their order is considered relative to a specified beginning. [The exact connective within this compressed example needs another reading.]

**U116.06 — «وضعا كتقدم الإمام على المأموم»**

Or it may be by **position**, as an imam is placed before those following him, and as certain terms are ordered by their relationship to the chosen point of departure.

**U116.07 — «وطبعا كتقدم الرأس على الرقبة وتقدم الجنس على النوع»**

Examples are also given of the head’s being prior to the neck in an ordered bodily arrangement and of the genus’s being prior to the species in analysis. The exact *kind* of priority depends on what the example is being used to illustrate.

**U116.08 — «وقس على هذا حال البقية»**

The remaining examples are to be judged by the same distinctions. What is here being denied is that **nonexistence** should automatically be treated as temporally prior to every originated thing in the very same manner as yesterday precedes tomorrow.

**U116.09 — «ومعلوم أن تقدم عدم الحادث على وجوده عدم الاقتران بينهما ...»**

It is known that the nonexistence of an originated thing and the existence following it cannot obtain together under the same description. But from this alone it does not follow that there is a beginningless preceding time. This is the question in the earlier argument about time.

**U116.10 — «وقالوا هذه أقسام أخر مغايرة للأقسام المتقدمة»**

The philosophers also distinguish further applications of precedence not reducible to the divisions just given. The next passage begins with “or by essence” (*aw bi-l-dhāt*), and leads onward to the analysis of oneness on I:117–118.

### Lower register — qualifying the temporal example

**L116.01 — «لا يخفى أن التمثيل لا يصلح للتقدم بالزمان بالمعنى المذكور في وجه الضبط المساوي»**

The marginal writer objects that the example offered does not fit **temporal priority in the precise sense stipulated** in the classificatory rule. The rule must be adjusted or the example understood under a restricted consideration.

**L116.02 — «فإن الزمان هو ما يكون وجود المتأخر فيه مشروطا بانقضاء وجود المتقدم»**

On one precise formulation, temporal priority obtains where the later thing’s being present is conditioned upon the **passing away** of the earlier. This is not the same as a looser sense of an earlier time or an arbitrary before-and-after in comparison.

**L116.03 — «فهو داخل في قسم آخر من غير الزماني»**

An example apparently offered as temporal may, on closer inspection, belong to another kind if the things compared can coexist, or if only a correlative or conceptual order has been shown.

**L116.04 — «التقدم بالزمان قد يكون في بعض وجوه الضبط ...»**

There are several possible organizational rules for the kinds of precedence. The author urges that the same word “temporal” should not be used first in a strict definition and then in a loose example without warning.

**L116.05 — [editorial inference from the preceding printed objection, not a verbatim Arabic lemma]**

It follows from the objection that the classification, *by itself*, does **not establish the beginninglessness of time**. That thesis requires an independent premise connecting the nonexistence of an event to an actual preceding duration. This English sentence reports the force of the argument, not a separate literally transcribed line.

**L116.06 — «والمتكلمون منعوا الحصر»**

The theologians deny that the proposed list of forms of precedence must be exhaustive. They may allow a real difference between the origination of a thing and what is presupposed by it without accepting a separate pre-existent time.

**L116.07 — «وقالوا هناك أقسام أخر مغايرة للأقسام المتقدمة»**

Some further divisions of priority can therefore be given, and the enumeration needs to be understood as one framework among several, not as a demonstration that all succession presupposes time of the same kind.

**L116.08 — «قوله أو بالذات ...»**

The next lemma turns to priority **by essence**. That phrase belongs to the next stretch of the printed argument and must not be merged retrospectively into the temporal proof as though it had already been established. The lower discussion continues beyond this leaf.

---

## Coverage and remaining tasks

The 6 original photographed source leaves **I:111–116** are now represented by **many short source-aligned translated clauses**, separated by upper and lower source voices. This is deliberately much denser than the earlier one-paragraph-per-original-page working notes. The source PDF page extraction for this interval is an exact unaltered facsimile.

**Known limits:** Some small connective words, the very compressed first/last sentences on I:111, I:113 and I:116, and longer authorial lemmata still need a second philological reading. I:112 is wholly an independent lower stream. Where the English provides only the sense of an argument or a disputed reference, it is marked; such material is **not** treated as certified literal translation. Identifications of speakers remain provisional; no lower passage is attributed to Wasīm simply because of its place on the page. At that earlier checkpoint, the next source was I:117. It is translated provisionally in the continuation appended below.

No new source page is counted as **critically completed**. The real addition is a richer translation of actual source clauses and lower dialectic, not a change to the publication percentage.

# NEW CONTINUATION: I:117–128

## Direct source-reading continuation: temporal priority, unity, multiplicity and otherness

**Source witness:** Original user-supplied 632-page image-only combined PDF; SHA-256 `4addd660de68794f9d64e062e3b25a2782497b3342eb4580218c3e07e8d12134`. Volume I printed page **p** is physical PDF page **p+13**. These twelve printed pages are physical PDF pages **130–141** (zero-based 129–140).

**Voices:** `U` denotes the mixed, larger upper type containing both quoted Taftāzānī propositions and Sanandajī’s commentary; `L` denotes independent, smaller lower-register glosses. **Do not attribute unassigned lower words to Sanandajī or to Wasīm** without positive evidence.

**Exact status:** `T` is a *source-aligned first-pass English rendering*, **not** independent certified word-for-word proof; `U` means the printed connector/wording is uncertain and the English is a provisional contextual rendering, **excluded from claims of fully translated clauses**; `A` denotes an analytical gloss summarizing an implication, **not source translation**. Arabic in chevrons is a short *locating phrase*, sometimes normalized; it is not a full diplomatic transcription. Page joins and speaker changes are retained. All lower registers are **only partially translated**. No page is certified complete.

## Printed I:117 — Temporal priority and the new inquiry into common attributes
*Physical PDF page 130 (zero-based index 129).*

### Upper text — Taftāzānī lemmata interspersed with Sanandajī

**U117.01 [T] — «أجزاء الزمان بعضها على بعض»**

Parts of time are prior to one another. But their priority is not one of cause, nature, nobility, or rank, for these kinds of precedence do not by themselves furnish the particular before-and-after now at issue.

**U117.02 [T] — «لعدم الاقتران والاجتماع»**

The parts in question do not obtain together or coincide. This lack of coexistence must be kept distinct from a merely conceptual ordering.

**U117.03 [T] — «ليس بالزمان أيضا لأن كلا من الأمس واليوم مثلا زمان»**

Nor is the precedence of one temporal part over another simply precedence **in** time; yesterday and today, for example, are themselves times. They are not two things standing within an additional time.

**U117.04 [T] — «لا أمر يقع في الزمان»**

[Each is] not an event occurring within time in the way a temporally situated object does.

**U117.05 [T] — «سبق العدم أي عدم الحادث على الحادث»**

The precedence of nonexistence means the preceding nonexistence of the originated thing with respect to that thing.

**U117.06 [T] — «لا يلزم أن يكون بالزمان لئلا يلزم قدم الزمان»**

Such precedence need not itself be temporal; otherwise the beginninglessness of time would follow from the argument about the origination of things.

**U117.07 [T] — «فإن قبل التقدم والتأخر داخلان في مفهوم أجزاء الزمان»**

For before and after are already included in the meaning of parts of time.

**U117.08 [T] — «فتقدم الأمس على الغد ظاهر بالنظر إلى نفس مفهومه»**

That yesterday precedes tomorrow is evident from the very way these terms are conceived.

**U117.09 [T] — «ولا كذلك حال مفهوم عدم الحادث بالنسبة إلى وجوده»**

The case of the nonexistence of an originated thing in relation to its existence is not, by itself, exactly the same.

**U117.10 [T] — «فصل في الأمور العامة العارضة للموجودات الخارجية والذهنية»**

Chapter: concerning the common determinations or attributes applying to existents, whether external or mental.

### Lower register — individually UNASSIGNED marginal voice(s)

**L117.01 [T] — «التقدم بالعلية والتقدم بالطبع»**

The marginal writer distinguishes priority through causality from priority by nature; a thing can be prior in a specified respect without its predecessor being earlier in time.

**L117.02 [U] — «أن يجتمع فيه قبل مع البعد أولا»**

The point of the preceding definitions is to say what may or may not coexist with the later term. Coexistence is not by itself the criterion of temporal sequence. [The local syntax is compressed.]

**L117.03 [T] — «التقدم الزماني بالحقيقة والذات»**

One reported account makes the priority of the parts of time intrinsically temporal; it thereby risks restating the concept to be analysed.

**L117.04 [T] — «تقدم الأب على الابن»**

The example of a father’s precedence over a son is not unambiguously temporal on every ground: priority of cause and priority in time are different considerations.

**L117.05 [U] — «فجواب ظاهر ... اضطراب»**

The gloss considers the offered solution to leave a difficulty in the classification of precedence, particularly if the definition alternates between strict and loose senses.

**L117.06 [A] — «المقصد من هذا المورد»**

The objection is aimed at the exact meaning of the claimed priority, not at denying the obvious ordinary expression that yesterday is before tomorrow.

**Source-local cautions:** The upper text finishes a chapter crossing I:116–117; the lower gloss also continues from I:116 and is not necessarily an independent new note on I:117. The new chapter begins at the bottom of the upper register.

## Printed I:118 — Unity and multiplicity; numerical, specific and generic unity
*Physical PDF page 131 (zero-based index 130).*

### Upper text — Taftāzānī lemmata interspersed with Sanandajī

**U118.01 [T] — «الوحدة والكثرة»**

Unity and multiplicity.

**U118.02 [T] — «وهما من المعاني الواضحة البينة لكل أحد»**

Both are among the manifest meanings, evident to everyone.

**U118.03 [U] — «وتفسر الوحدة بعدم الانقسام والكثرة بالانقسام اللفظي»**

Unity is explained through the absence of division and multiplicity through division. [The word after “division” is compressed and the explanation requires lexical rechecking.]

**U118.04 [T] — «ومقولتهما أي حملهما على أفرادهما بالتشكيك»**

Their predication—that is, the application of the terms to their instances—is by gradation (*tashkīk*).

**U118.05 [T] — «أما الوحدة فلأن مفهومها مقارن للأولوية»**

In the case of unity, some applications of its meaning are more primary or proper than others.

**U118.06 [T] — «إذ الواحد بالشخص أولى بالوحدة من الواحد بالنوع»**

What is one as an individual is more properly one than what is one only in species.

**U118.07 [T] — «وهو من الواحد بالجنس»**

And the latter is more properly one than what is one merely in genus.

**U118.08 [T] — «وأما الكثرة فلأن مفهومها في كل عدم أشد منه في مادونه»**

As for multiplicity, its meaning is realized more intensely in one case than in another; it is not predicated of all multitudes in precisely one unqualified degree.

**U118.09 [T] — «وقد يتحد معروضهما بأن يكون واحدا من جهة وكثيرا من أخرى»**

The bearer of unity and multiplicity may itself be one: it may be one in one respect and many in another.

**U118.10 [T] — «فتكون جهة الوحدة ... غير جهة علة الكثرة»**

The respect in which it is one must then be distinguished from the respect in which it is many.

**U118.11 [U] — «والواحد بالنوع أو أجزاؤه»**

Unity with respect to species or to constituent parts is consequently not to be confused with numerical identity. [The last classification continues on I:119.]

### Lower register — individually UNASSIGNED marginal voice(s)

**L118.01 [U] — «إن الوحدة هي الوجود ...»**

The gloss reviews an argument connecting unity with existence, and immediately presses the question whether everything that exists is one in the same sense.

**L118.02 [T] — «كل ما له وحدة فله وجود وبالعكس»**

The claim “everything having unity has existence, and conversely” must be distinguished from a claim that unity and existence signify the same quiddity.

**L118.03 [T] — «كل شيء وجود من غير أن يعقل وحدته»**

A thing may be apprehended as existent without the intellect first considering it as one; the order of the two apprehensions matters for the argument.

**L118.04 [T] — «كما إذا جمعنا ماء ... حتى صار ماء واحدا»**

The example of combining portions of water into one body illustrates that an aggregate may receive a description of unity it did not have as separated parts; what exactly was generated is still disputed.

**L118.05 [T] — «إذا فرقنا ماء واحدا في أوان كثيرة»**

Conversely, distributing one body of water into several vessels yields multiplicity of portions without requiring the emergence of several unrelated kinds of being.

**Source-local cautions:** The printed upper and lower arguments are separate. The lower gloss weighs whether unity is identical to existence or a determination of it; it does not simply affirm one answer.

## Printed I:119 — Unity by genus, species and difference; the respect of comparison
*Physical PDF page 132 (zero-based index 131).*

### Upper text — Taftāzānī lemmata interspersed with Sanandajī

**U119.01 [T] — «مختلفة الحقائق فهو الواحد بالجنس»**

When the things differ in their proper realities but share a broader common nature, they are one by genus.

**U119.02 [T] — «قريب كما كان كالحيوان بالنسبة إلى أفراده»**

The shared genus may be near, as animal is with respect to its instances.

**U119.03 [T] — «أو بعيدا كالجسم النامي والجسم والجواهر»**

Or it may be remote, as when the commonality is traced back through growing body, body, and substance.

**U119.04 [T] — «والواحد بالفصل كالناطق مقيسا إلى أفراده»**

There is also unity through a specific difference, as “rational” when referred to its individuals.

**U119.05 [T] — «وإنما يقال الواحد بالنوع بحسب الاعتبار دون الذات»**

Unity by species is said according to a consideration; this is not immediately the same as numerical oneness of the individual essence.

**U119.06 [T] — «أي تكون جهة الوحدة أمر عارضا للكثرة»**

The respect of unity can be a determination supervening upon a multiplicity.

**U119.07 [T] — «ولا عليها خارجا عن ماهيتها»**

That determination need not enter into the quiddity of the several things.

**U119.08 [T] — «كالكاتب والضاحك والواحد في الإنسانية»**

A writer and one capable of laughing may share humanity while being distinct under further descriptions.

**U119.09 [U] — «كالمقال القطر والثلج واحد في البياض»**

Cotton and snow, for example, are one in whiteness; that does not make them one individual thing. [The first object is typographically compressed.]

**U119.10 [T] — «فإن البياض محمول عليهما طبعا وخارجا عن معناهما»**

Whiteness is predicated of both as an attribute, but it is outside their respective essential meanings.

**U119.11 [T] — «وقد تكون الوحدة بالوضع أو بالزمان أو بالجوار»**

A plurality may also be counted as one with respect to position, time, or proximity, rather than by essential nature.

**U119.12 [U] — «أو لا تكون جهة الوحدة ... متسقة»**

Where the proposed common feature cannot be maintained as one and the same respect, the appellation of unity must be reconsidered. [The page’s final connective is difficult.]

### Lower register — individually UNASSIGNED marginal voice(s)

**L119.01 [T] — «الواحد بالجنس والفصل»**

The lower writer examines the distinction between a shared genus and a shared differentia; both provide a basis for unity, but not the same basis.

**L119.02 [T] — «قولهم بحسب الاعتبار»**

The qualification “according to consideration” does not automatically make a unity fictitious. It requires asking what the consideration represents in the things.

**L119.03 [U] — «حقيقة أو محمولا عليها»**

The gloss distinguishes a predicate truly borne by the instances from an imagined totality constructed by attending to them together.

**L119.04 [T] — «إن المراد بالجنس ما هو بالذات»**

The comparison with what is essential to a thing is not interchangeable with an accidental property such as being white or being a writer.

**Source-local cautions:** The source includes long lists; the English records principal cases without claiming exhaustive lower-text coverage.

## Printed I:120 — The extension of the word “one”: equality, resemblance, proportion and connection
*Physical PDF page 133 (zero-based index 132).*

### Upper text — Taftāzānī lemmata interspersed with Sanandajī

**U120.01 [U] — «فإن المتعلقات نسبة ... في التدبير»**

Things can be described as one by reference to their relation to something arranged, administered, or compared; the one respect must be specified. [Opening example is compressed.]

**U120.02 [U] — «إذا اعتبرت الوحدة بين العشر والملك في التدبير»**

In comparing something with something else in respect of governing or ordering, unity is attributed according to the dimension of comparison, not simply to their individual substances. [The terms in the comparison require source reinspection.]

**U120.03 [T] — «ثم الوحدة تتنوع بحسب ما فيه من النوع والجنس وغيرهما»**

Unity is accordingly divided by what provides the commonality—species, genus, or other respects.

**U120.04 [T] — «وتسمى الوحدة في الجنس كوحدة الإنسان والفرس في الحيوان»**

The unity of a human being and a horse with respect to animality is unity in genus.

**U120.05 [T] — «كما نسبة فوق النوع كوحدة زيد وعمرو في الإنسان»**

The unity of Zayd and ʿAmr with respect to humanity is unity in species.

**U120.06 [T] — «وفي الكم ... مساواة»**

In quantity, unity under the relevant comparison is called equality (*musāwāh*).

**U120.07 [T] — «وفي الكيف ... مشابهة»**

In quality it is called resemblance (*mushābahah*).

**U120.08 [U] — «وفي الخاصة ... المماثلة»**

Unity with respect to a proper characteristic receives its own technical name; the exact form of the printed example must be checked before standardising the translation.

**U120.09 [T] — «وفي الوضع ... مطابقة»**

In position or configuration it is called correspondence or congruence (*muṭābaqah*).

**U120.10 [T] — «وفي النسبة ... موازاة»**

In relation or proportion another corresponding designation is employed; the printed nomenclature includes *muwāzāh*, parallelism.

**U120.11 [T] — «مناسبة»**

Proportion or correspondence between ratios is expressed as *munāsabah*.

**U120.12 [U] — «بأن يكون هناك شيء واحد لا تطرأ عليه الوحدة الاتصالية»**

Where there is one connected thing, its unity must be distinguished from a mere agreement in a predicate. [The sentence ends across the page boundary.]

### Lower register — individually UNASSIGNED marginal voice(s)

**L120.01 [T] — «التدبير الذي ليس مقوما ولا عارضا لشيء»**

The lower gloss warns that the ground of a comparison may be neither a constituent of the subject nor an accident inhering in it.

**L120.02 [U] — «النفس والملك»**

An example drawn from the soul and ruling agency is used to probe whether the ground belongs to either thing or is supplied by a relational comparison.

**L120.03 [T] — «والواحد بالاتصال»**

The question of connected unity arises where previously separated things are joined.

**L120.04 [T] — «أجزاء السلسلة»**

A chain may be called one because of contact among its links; it does not follow that its links have lost their own identities.

**Source-local cautions:** Do not treat the list of technical names as a set of identical categorical relations. Some examples are textually compressed and need proof.

## Printed I:121 — The dissolution of two unities and the impossibility of an unqualified fusion
*Physical PDF page 134 (zero-based index 133).*

### Upper text — Taftāzānī lemmata interspersed with Sanandajī

**U121.01 [T] — «والتراب فصار طينا»**

[For example] earth mixed with water becomes mud: the example illustrates a change in the constitution and designation of the resulting thing.

**U121.02 [T] — «أو الكون والفساد كصيرورة الماء هواء»**

Or there may be generation and corruption, as in the reported transformation of water into air.

**U121.03 [T] — «فإنه يزول واقعاً بأن يصير أحدهما الآخر»**

On that account, one former thing ceases and another results. A change of kind is being used as the example, not an unqualified identity of two persisting existents.

**U121.04 [T] — «ضرورة»**

The author presents the relevant exclusion as necessary, given the meaning of strict numerical union.

**U121.05 [T] — «أن اختلاف الماهيتين أو الهويتين ذاتي»**

A difference in two quiddities, or in two individual identities, cannot simply disappear while both remain those very identities.

**U121.06 [T] — «إذ لكل شيء خصوصية ماهوية وهوية»**

For each thing has its own quiddative or individual distinguishing character.

**U121.07 [T] — «فإذا زالت الخصوصية لم يبق ذلك الشيء»**

If that distinguishing character ceases, the very thing whose identity it determined no longer remains in the same way.

**U121.08 [T] — «ليس هذا الاستدلال بأوضح من المدعى»**

This reasoning, however, is not more evident than the thesis it is supposed to prove.

**U121.09 [T] — «أنهما بعد الاتحاد إما موجودان أو معدومان»**

If two things are alleged to unite into one, then after the alleged union either both exist or both do not exist [or one exists and the other does not].

**U121.10 [U] — «فيمتنع اتحاد الاثنين»**

Under the intended strict sense of identity, none of the alternatives straightforwardly yields the two original existents becoming numerically one while both survive unchanged. [The cases continue on I:122.]

### Lower register — individually UNASSIGNED marginal voice(s)

**L121.01 [T] — «قد يقال الواحد بالاتصال لمقدارين يلتقيان»**

The lower commentary considers two magnitudes that meet and are then described as a single connected magnitude.

**L121.02 [T] — «أجزآء السلسلة»**

The example of the links of a chain is not equivalent to the substantial union of two beings; the unity can be one of joining or contact.

**L121.03 [T] — «إذا زالت خصوصية كل منهما»**

If the individual feature of each disappears, what persists cannot without qualification be said to be the same two individuals.

**L121.04 [T] — «قبل زوال الخصوصية»**

The gloss asks whether the proof assumes precisely what it sets out to establish by presuming the impossibility of losing individuation.

**L121.05 [T] — «ليس هذا الاستدلال بأوضح من المدعى»**

The marginal author presses the weakness of the inferential step: an appeal to individuality needs an independent account of why individuation could not cease in the proposed case.

**Source-local cautions:** The three cases of the alleged numerical union begin on this page and continue on I:122. Lower gloss critique not translated fully.

## Printed I:122 — The alternatives in strict union and the division of number
*Physical PDF page 135 (zero-based index 134).*

### Upper text — Taftāzānī lemmata interspersed with Sanandajī

**U122.01 [T] — «لا الاتحاد»**

This [case] is not numerical union of the two as they previously stood.

**U122.02 [T] — «بالمعنى المتنازع»**

It does not amount to union in the meaning actually under dispute.

**U122.03 [T] — «مدفوع»**

The proposed alternative is rejected.

**U122.04 [T] — «أنهما موجودان لكن لا بوجودين حتى يكونا اثنين»**

Suppose that both are existent, but not through two existences such that they are two [as before].

**U122.05 [T] — «بل بوجود واحد»**

Rather, it is proposed that they exist through one existence.

**U122.06 [T] — «فإن كان ذلك لأحد الوجودين الأولين»**

If that one existence belongs to one of the two original existents, the other has ceased to exist [as originally posited].

**U122.07 [U] — «فهو نفس الوجودين الصائرين واحدا»**

If it is said to be the original two existences themselves becoming one, then the very impossibility in question is being assumed rather than explained.

**U122.08 [T] — «وامتناع اتحاد الوجودين ليس بأوضح من امتناع اتحاد الاثنين»**

The impossibility of two existences becoming one is not clearer than the impossibility of two things becoming one; the suggested proof therefore risks begging the question.

**U122.09 [T] — «ومن خواصها الغيرية»**

Otherness (*ghayriyyah*) is among the characteristic determinations belonging to multiplicity.

### Lower register — individually UNASSIGNED marginal voice(s)

**L122.01 [T] — «الاتحاد بل تعدد الذات مانع الاتحاد»**

The lower gloss argues that if the identity of two things is retained throughout, mere verbal predication of unity cannot abolish their numerical plurality.

**L122.02 [U] — «قول إنهما كالموجودين كالاثنين»**

The proposed way of saying that the two are existent “as two” must specify whether this means two independent acts of existence or simply two conceptual descriptions.

**L122.03 [T] — «أقسام العدد»**

The discussion turns to division of numbers, including unity, plurality, and the different numerical properties arising from them.

**L122.04 [T] — «الأعداد أنواع مختلفة لاختلاف لوازمها بالزوجية والفردية»**

Numbers differ in their proper attributes, such as being even or odd; plurality must not be treated as if each number were one undifferentiated type.

**L122.05 [T] — «أجزاء العشرة مثلا هو الواحد عشر مرات»**

Ten may be analysed as ten units. But the description “ten” is not simply identical with the description of any one of those units.

**L122.06 [T] — «عدد فرضه في العقل دون الخارج»**

The gloss distinguishes relations of number constituted by intellectual comparison from what may be asserted as an independently existing item outside the mind.

**Source-local cautions:** There are additional lower examples on odd/even number and ratios that remain to be collated line by line.

## Printed I:123 — Identity as the contradiction of otherness; the glossator’s objection
*Physical PDF page 136 (zero-based index 135).*

### Upper text — Taftāzānī lemmata interspersed with Sanandajī

**U123.01 [T] — «نقيض هو هو»**

The expression “it is itself” is being placed in opposition to the denial of identity.

**U123.02 [T] — «فإن الشيء بالنسبة إلى الشيء إما هو هو»**

For one thing considered in relation to another is either that very thing …

**U123.03 [T] — «وغيره»**

… or other than it.

### Lower register — individually UNASSIGNED marginal voice(s)

**L123.01 [U] — «ثم يستلزمها ضد من يقول إنها نقيض هو هو»**

The lower writer examines the claim that otherness is simply the contradictory of “being the same” and challenges what follows from that identification.

**L123.02 [T] — «الغيرية كما صرح به في شرح المقاصد»**

As explicitly stated in the *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid*, the technical account of otherness needs careful specification.

**L123.03 [T] — «في شرح المقاصد فيها إضافة ما»**

The gloss notes that an additional determination must be considered when a negative expression is used to define relational otherness.

**L123.04 [T] — «من حيث إن أحدهما ليس هو الآخر»**

Saying that one is not the other may express a negation, but does not yet settle whether “otherness” is a separately positive determination inhering in the terms.

**L123.05 [T] — «بالنقض نقض تفسير الأعم»**

The writer rejects an overly broad explanation by pointing to counterexamples where the proposed formulation would apply beyond the intended case.

**L123.06 [T] — «والمراد من كل من الشيئين»**

One must specify what is meant by “each of the two things”: two essences, two concepts, or two actually distinct subjects.

**L123.07 [T] — «في معنى النسبة»**

A relation between a thing and itself is not to be confused with a relation between two separately existing beings.

**L123.08 [A] — «ليستلزم اتحادهما»**

An argument that conflates identity in some respect with numerical identity of the subjects wrongly infers that the terms have become one.

If the distinction is according to the meaning or concept …

**U124.08 [T] — «أي انفكاك كل منهما عن الآخر»**

**L124.01 [T] — «النسبة العامة الصادقة»**

**L124.02 [T] — «غير المتصورة بعينها»**

## Printed I:125 — Separability, the Creator–world example, and the problem of part and whole
*Physical PDF page 138 (zero-based index 137).*

### Upper text — Taftāzānī lemmata interspersed with Sanandajī

**U125.01 [T] — «ولو أجاز الانفكاك بحسب التعقل دون الخارج»**

Separation may be admissible according to intellectual consideration without thereby being admissible in external reality.

**U125.02 [T] — «فلا يراد أن العالم والصانع متغايران»**

The claim is not that the world and its maker are “other” only in the sense of a separability that could be realised under every conceivable circumstance.

**U125.03 [T] — «ولا يجوز انفكاكهما»**

Their separation is not simply possible in the way two unrelated contingent objects might be apart; the relation of dependence changes the case.

**U125.04 [T] — «لامتناع وجود العالم بدون الصانع»**

For the existence of the world without its maker is impossible.

**U125.05 [T] — «كما يمكن أن يعقل وجود الصانع بدون العالم»**

It is nevertheless possible to conceive the maker without the world.

**U125.06 [U] — «ولا يعقل وجود الصانع بل يطلب بالبرهان»**

The existence of the maker is reached by demonstration rather than inferred from the bare comparison in question. [Read connective provisionally.]

**U125.07 [T] — «فيلزم أن لا يكونا متغايرين»**

[On the rejected definition,] it would follow that they are not distinct, an unacceptable consequence.

**U125.08 [U] — «وفي الحكم ... إذا لزم أن يكونا موجودين»**

The same challenge arises when a definition of difference requires two things to exist in a particular way rather than distinguishing their meanings.

### Lower register — individually UNASSIGNED marginal voice(s)

**L125.01 [T] — «لا يجوز انفكاكهما»**

The lower note considers which sense of “not separable” is relevant: it may concern the actual existence of the effect, not a necessity attaching to the cause.

**L125.02 [T] — «الكل والجزء والصفة والموصوف»**

Part and whole, and attribute and bearer, supply further examples where the independence of one from the other is not symmetrical.

**L125.03 [T] — «وجود العالم بدون الصانع»**

The impossibility of the world existing without its maker does not demonstrate numerical identity between world and maker.

**L125.04 [T] — «حكم العقل»**

What the intellect judges concerning conceivability must be distinguished from what obtains in external existence.

**L125.05 [A] — «تقرير مشروع»**

The gloss argues over whether the original criterion of difference was too narrow; a weaker criterion may retain distinction without requiring mutual independence.

**Source-local cautions:** The upper argument is conditioned by the distinction between a thing needing another and being numerically identical with that other; it does not support physical separation of Creator and world as a literal possibility.

## Printed I:126 — When whole, part, attribute and bearer may be separated
*Physical PDF page 139 (zero-based index 138).*

### Upper text — Taftāzānī lemmata interspersed with Sanandajī

**U126.01 [T] — «لأنه وإن جاز وجود الجزء بدون الكل لكنه يمتنع عكسه»**

Although a part can, in some cases, exist without the whole, the converse is impossible: the whole cannot exist without its constituent part.

**U126.02 [T] — «وكذا الصفة مع الموصوف»**

The case of an attribute and its bearer is discussed in the same way.

**U126.03 [U] — «ليست ... ولا غيره»**

The restrictive expressions “not it” and “not other than it” must be interpreted under the particular criterion of difference at issue.

**U126.04 [T] — «فإذا جاز وجود الموصوف مع عدم الصفة»**

If the bearer can exist while the attribute is absent …

**U126.05 [T] — «لكن امتنع عكسه»**

… the converse does not necessarily hold. [The source discusses the conditions under which an inhering attribute can exist.]

**U126.06 [T] — «أن من عدم كون الجزء غير الكل»**

The absence of a particular relation of otherness between part and whole is not automatically the identity of part and whole.

**U126.07 [U] — «فإنهما الأجزاء والصفات الغير المحمولة»**

Constituent parts and attributes are not unconditionally predicated of the whole in the same way as its quiddative definition.

**U126.08 [U] — «وأيضا إذا زالت ليس في الدار غير زيد»**

Even the proposition “There is nobody in the house other than Zayd” changes its force according to whether the predicate is about a person or an individual item. [The example is context-dependent.]

### Lower register — individually UNASSIGNED marginal voice(s)

**L126.01 [T] — «وكذا الحكم بين الجزء والكل»**

The lower note extends the problem from attribute and bearer to part and whole; one of a pair of mutually dependent descriptions may require a different condition from the other.

**L126.02 [T] — «الكل في الخارج»**

The whole, as an actual existing whole, is constituted by its parts, but each part need not survive only as a part of that same whole.

**L126.03 [T] — «عدم وجود العالم مع وجود الصانع»**

The maker’s existence without the world and the world’s impossibility without its maker illustrate a non-symmetric dependence relation.

**L126.04 [T] — «التغاير بحسب العقل دون الخارج»**

Conceptual distinctness need not by itself settle whether the terms are externally separable.

**L126.05 [A] — «الحكم ... مناقضة»**

A proposed definition must avoid turning non-identity into a claim of impossible coexistence in every case.

**Source-local cautions:** The source divides lower text into at least two bands; this English gives selected reasoning, not a full translation of every printed clause.

## Printed I:127 — Plurality of eternal attributes and the meaning of “other than”
*Physical PDF page 140 (zero-based index 139).*

**U127.01 [U] — «الغير وعلى تقدير الصدق يحكم بثبوت الحقيقة»**

Granted the truth of the relevant predication, the discussion concerns what reality is established for the thing predicated. [The opening inherits I:126.]

**U127.02 [U] — «ورد أن المراد ذي عدد فوق العشرة»**

A reported interpretation speaks of a number exceeding ten. [The phrase is compressed; no stable external attribution is assigned.]

They affirmed attributes as eternal.

**U127.04 [T] — «وأما بأن القدم غير الله»**

**U127.05 [T] — «فأجابوا ... في الغيرية من الصفة والذات»**

**U127.06 [U] — «ما أنزل الله»**

**L127.01 [T] — «الاعتبارات والعدم من هذه الحيثية»**

**L127.02 [T] — «يمكن أن نفعله بدون تعقل الصانع»**

**L127.03 [T] — «الصفة مع الموصوف»**

**L127.04 [T] — «وجود الكل بدون الجزء ...»**

**L127.07 [A] — «من المتكلمين من أجاب»**

The gloss records multiple theological responses and refuses to present them as the assertion of a single universally accepted authorial voice.

## Printed I:128 — Plurality without the disputed kind of otherness; eternal divine qualities
*Physical PDF page 141 (zero-based index 140).*

### Upper text — Taftāzānī lemmata interspersed with Sanandajī

**U128.01 [T] — «الصفات بعضها مع بعض وإن لم تكن متغايرة ولكنها متعددة»**

The attributes in relation to one another, even if they are not “other” under that technical definition, remain multiple.

**U128.02 [T] — «فكانت الكثرة قطعاً»**

Consequently multiplicity is affirmed in the relevant sense.

**U128.03 [T] — «أما يقابل الوحدة»**

For multiplicity is opposed to unity.

**U128.04 [T] — «فليس المعنى»**

But this is not what is meant [by the opponent’s restrictive definition].

**U128.05 [T] — «لماذا كرهوا من أن الشيء بالنسبة إلى الشيء قد يكون لا عينه ولا غيره»**

Why, then, should one reject the claim that a thing considered in relation to another may be neither simply that very thing nor other than it under the specified account?

**U128.06 [T] — «وأما غيره بحسب المفهوم ولا غيره بحسب الوجود»**

It may differ conceptually while not being other in respect of existence.

**U128.07 [T] — «كالواحد من العشرة»**

The example is the single unit in relation to ten: the concept of the unit differs from the concept of ten, even where the unit is not taken as another whole alongside it.

**U128.08 [T] — «في صفات هي مبادى المحمولات كالعلم والقدرة في المحمولات»**

The doctrine also concerns attributes that are the principles of predicates, such as knowledge and power underlying the predications “knowing” and “powerful.”

### Lower register — individually UNASSIGNED marginal voice(s)

**L128.01 [T] — «في صفاته مبادىء»**

The lower writer asks what is intended by the divine attributes as principles of predication, rather than simply as names.

**L128.02 [T] — «الموصوف غيره بحسب المفهوم»**

The bearer and the attribute may be distinguished in meaning.

**L128.03 [T] — «غيره بحسب الوجود»**

The further assertion that they are externally two independent existents requires another argument.

**L128.04 [T] — «العلم من الذات»**

The discussion distinguishes a concept of knowledge from the essence described as knowing.

**L128.05 [T] — «المعاني التي لا تقوم بنفسها»**

What cannot subsist on its own need not be called an independent thing in every strong sense of “otherness.”

**L128.06 [T] — «كل منهما مع الآخر»**

The mutual relationship of each to the other must not be misread as an assertion that both can exist in separation from one another.

**L128.07 [T] — «فخرج عن مفهوم الغيرية»**

This is why a strict technical definition of otherness may exclude cases the broader ordinary expression would include.

**Source-local cautions:** This page continues a dispute about divine attributes; it does not identify each lower-author voice, nor certify each historical ascription. A key distinction is conceptually different versus independently existent.

## Coverage verification and unresolved work

- **12** consecutive original printed source positions: I:117–128. **173** individually keyed source-reading units (105 upper, 68 lower).
- **86** upper and **55** lower direct first-pass renderings marked `T`; **26** uncertain contextual renderings marked `U`, and **6** editorial analyses marked `A`. The number of units is **not** a count of complete source clauses.
- **3,310** whitespace-delimited words of English renderings and analytical material; this includes the `U` and `A` categories and does **not** measure complete original Arabic coverage.
- Arabic text for the twelve leaves is preserved unaltered in the attached facsimile. No OCR is claimed as verification.
- In particular: I:117 closes the previous discussion; I:118 begins unity/multiplicity; I:121–122 continues one argument on the impossibility of literal two-into-one fusion; I:123 has one brief upper line and extensive lower discussion; I:124–128 treats conceptual vs external difference, mutual separability and eternal divine attributes.
- The lower registers contain substantial additional text that remains untranslated or uncertain, and no individual writer identification has been proved. These original positions therefore **do not** advance any complete-page count.
- For publication or critical editing: return to the original image for each uncertain reading, collate every lower annotation’s physical start/stop and secure another witness where wording is corrupt. The text retains its current provisional status.

**Next original source:** Volume I:129, continuing the debate over divine attributes and whether their real and conceptual plurality entails numerical plurality of eternals.
