# New continuation · I:129–148

## Volume I:129 — Do distinct eternal attributes entail several necessary beings?
*Original physical PDF p. 142 · Upper: continuous first-pass reading of the visible opening · Lower: extended working translation, some compressed clauses uncertain.*

### Upper text (mixed lemma and Sanandajī's explanation)

**U129.1** — On the expression concerning predicates which are not borne by each [subject]: it is not concealed that the affirmation of attributes additional to the divine essence **in existence** entails an objection which sound intellect will not readily accept.

**U129.2** — For it would follow either that the Necessary Existent is a necessitating cause with respect to His attributes, or that necessary existents are multiplied. Exalted is He above that. The correct position is what the author of *al-Mawāqif* stated. [The sentence introducing his view continues into the lower discussion and the next leaf.]

### Separately printed lower argument — author unassigned

**L129.1** — If the attributes are described as eternal, the question is whether they possess a complete self-subsistence like distinct necessary realities, or whether one of the two [essence and attribute] stands in need of the other. It is only the former which immediately constitutes a plurality of independent eternals.

**L129.2** — When one is dependent upon the other, the mere use of the predicate “eternal” does not produce several independent necessary beings. There is a difference between several things existing without a beginning and several things each existing *through itself*.

**L129.3** — This is the intended response of the Shaykh and those who follow him to the philosophers in the question of the agenthood of the Maker and the accounts of substances, bodies, parts, and wholes: a common expression must not be allowed to conceal the different kinds of dependence.

**L129.4** — Thus the theologians' effort to deny the philosophers' plurality of eternal entities does not require them to deny every distinct consideration attached to the divine attributes. What must be excluded is the independence proper to a second necessary being.

**L129.5** — One might say that the attributes of God are additional to the essence, and that nothing comparable belongs to Him otherwise. But the wording “additional” must be specified: it may be used of a distinct intelligible meaning without asserting an externally independent subject.

**L129.6** — If the addition is understood as addition in the sense of two fully existent and independent entities, then the objection returns. If it is a distinction of what is apprehended in one and the same divine subject, it does not entail that consequence.

**L129.7** — The commentator appeals here to the different ways one may conceive a predicate, its subject, and their conjunction. This point governs the subsequent distinction between an attribute's **meaning** and its supposed **separate existence**.

**Unresolved:** Several lower clauses debating the philosophers' specific list of eternal principles and the exact scope of a cited *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid* passage remain compressed. L129.1–7 render the connected discernible argument; they are not a certified word-for-word transcription of the entire lower register.

**Arabic anchors:** «القول بزيادة الصفات على الذات بحسب الوجود» · «كون الواجب موجبًا بالنسبة إلى الصفات» · «تعدد الواجب» · «تعالى عن ذلك».


---

## Volume I:130 — Difference in meaning and identity in existence
*Original physical PDF p. 143 · Upper: connected first-pass translation · Lower: sustained dialectical translation with local uncertainties.*

### Upper text

**U130.1** — The discussion concerns the principles of the predicates, because the sense of *knowledge* is not the sense of the name of the essence. For the sense of knowledge is the principle of disclosure, whereas the sense of the name of the essence is different.

**U130.2** — But they do not differ **as to existence**. In the Necessary Existent the [divine attribute] is the essence itself with regard to existence, as are the remaining names of the attributes in relation to the essence.

**U130.3** — And the attributes stand to one another in this way too, while differing in what their expressions indicate. Accordingly a dispute over the affirmation or negation of attributes may, when thus understood, be largely a dispute about expressions and their conceptual content.

**U130.4** — The text nevertheless warns against treating every terminological accord as a solution to the ontological difficulty. It proceeds to distinguish the remaining types of difference and comparison.

### Lower text — author unassigned

**L130.1** — The glossator asks whether the predecessors' denial of numerous eternals concerned only entities independent in themselves, or also the distinct meanings through which predicates are applied to one necessary subject.

**L130.2** — He points out that their statements about the unrestricted attributes have to be compared with the manner in which they speak of unity and of distinction. If the attributes truly possess separate independent existence, mere agreement in naming them “attributes of God” cannot settle the objection.

**L130.3** — Nor, however, does an unrestricted denial of all additional attributes follow from the proofs cited against a plurality of necessary beings. The assertion that one thing is understood as knowledge and another as power may be sound even if the two are not independent existents.

**L130.4** — In the explanation taken from the commentary on the *Maqāṣid*, the scope of the denied multiplicity is principally the multiplication of self-subsisting necessary natures, not every differentiation of intelligible predication.

**L130.5** — A further illustration concerns a *whole* and its *parts*. The whole can be spoken of as one, and yet the parts are many. But one must distinguish a whole in the sense of a collection from the existence of a further independent subject over and above its constituents.

**L130.6** — In saying that all of the parts are present, one has not thereby established that their *collective unity* is another externally subsisting object. If one takes “the whole” to mean nothing beyond all the parts, their existence does not entail an independent extra entity.

**L130.7** — Conversely, the name of the whole may have a meaning which is not simply the meaning of each part severally. Distinctness in **concept** is therefore insufficient to demonstrate distinctness in **existence**.

**L130.8** — The same distinction is relevant when the attributes are said to be other than the essence or to differ among themselves. One must specify whether “other” indicates nonidentity of what is understood, separability, or an externally added reality.

**L130.9** — The final issue is not whether language permits many predications, but whether their differing contents are enough to ground many ancient beings. The glossator denies that the conclusion can be obtained without an additional premise.

**Unresolved:** The bottom of the lower page uses a difficult illustration of whole and collection; L130.5–9 are an argued continuous reading, not a diplomatic transcription. The initial two brief separately ruled lemmatic notes have been kept distinct in these paragraphs.

**Arabic anchors:** «مفهوم العلم هو مبدأ الانكشاف» · «بخلاف مفهوم اسم الذات» · «فالواجب هو الذات بحسب الوجود» · «لا اختلاف من نفي الصفات ومن أثبتها إلا بحسب اللفظ» [last phrase requires another reading].


---

## Volume I:131 — Essential predicates, attributes of meaning, and opposition
*Original physical PDF p. 144 · Upper: connected visible-span first pass · Lower: partly translated argumentative commentary.*

### Upper text

**U131.1** — The **essential attributes** (*al-ṣifāt al-nafsiyya*) are the positive attributes which indicate the essence itself without signifying a meaning additional to it, as when one says of a substance that it is a substance.

**U131.2** — Corresponding to them are the **attributes of meaning** (*al-ṣifāt al-maʿnawiyya*): positive attributes indicating a meaning additional to the essence, as in [the cited example of] the substance being [described by a further determination]. The distinction concerns what the predicate signifies, not merely the grammar of the name.

**U131.3** — Hence the shared application of such predicates must be distinguished from genuinely opposite predicates. Not every one of two items which differ is therefore the *contrary* of the other.

**U131.4** — This matters in judgments about what is possible, necessary, or impossible. The authors differ concerning how far the mutual exclusion of the opposing expressions follows from their being opposed.

**U131.5** — In particular, the *impossibility of conjunction* of opposites must be distinguished from their *impossibility of absence together*. The page begins the corresponding division of opposed cases.

### Lower text — author unassigned

**L131.1** — The gloss cites the explanation in *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid*: an essential predicate designates the essence without requiring the intellect to posit a further intrinsic attribute, as the human being's being human. On this account existence, thinghood, and similar expressions present a problem precisely because their conceptual contents are not uniform across authors.

**L131.2** — It then examines a rule about the opposed: if two opposites cannot coexist, does it follow that precisely one must always be present? That further inference is not valid for every type of opposition.

**L131.3** — For certain opposites both can be absent, as when a subject is not adequately qualified to receive either of two determinations. In other cases, both cannot be absent, because the terms exhaust the alternatives under the specified consideration.

**L131.4** — The glossator tests the inference by comparing mutually contradictory propositions with positive contraries. Contradiction excludes not only their joint truth but also their joint falsity, provided the subject and respect are held fixed. Contrariety need not have that second property.

**L131.5** — The question arises whether the simultaneous absence of two attributes counts as their “conjunction” in the relevant sense. The reply turns on the terms' being existent determinations or merely privative judgments.

**L131.6** — The printed commentator also warns against moving from different possible meanings of an expression to a logical conclusion without fixing the intended meanings first. A word used of different respects may make an apparent exception which is not an exception at all.

**L131.7** — Thus the particular mode of opposition must be determined before the possible combinations can be assessed. It is not sufficient simply to call the two items “opposites” and reason as though every type of opposition were contradictory opposition.

**Unresolved:** The long lower discussion includes an appeal to the “four states” of two predicates and examples of mistaken restrictions; several specific connectors are uncertain and have not been silently supplied.

**Arabic anchors:** «الصفات النفسية» · «الدالة على نفس الذات دون معنى زائد عليها» · «الصفات المعنوية» · «واختلف في لزوم تغايرهما» · «امتناع اجتماعهما».


---

## Volume I:132 — Contrariety and restrictions on a common subject
*Original physical PDF p. 145 · Upper: source-facing connected rendering · Lower: extended partially uncertain translation.*

### Upper text

**U132.1** — As for the contrary, if the two opposed items are positive realities, each considered in relation to the other, their opposition is **contrariety** (*taḍādd*).

**U132.2** — The relevant condition is that both cannot be brought together in one subject from one respect. The intention is not to exclude cases such as the coexistence of black and white in different bodies, or the existence of opposed predicates under different aspects.

**U132.3** — If one term is a positive state and the other a nonexistence specified with respect to a subject capable of receiving that state, the opposition is of **privation and possession** rather than contrariety.

**U132.4** — A privation is therefore not simple nonexistence without qualification: it is nonpossession of a perfection in a thing to which that perfection is naturally applicable, in the time or respect for which it is applicable.

**U132.5** — In the restriction “from one aspect,” the aspect is that under which the predicate is ascribed. The contraries' inability to coexist should not be converted into an impossibility of their appearing in the universe together.

### Lower text — author unassigned

**L132.1** — The writer asks whether the condition that two contraries be “of one subject” should be made part of their definition or only specified when speaking about their exclusion in actual predication.

**L132.2** — He illustrates the difficulty by terms which are opposed only under a certain respect. If two predicates are applied to one bearer under different qualifications, their conjunction in speech may be unobjectionable even though one cannot possess them under exactly the same description.

**L132.3** — An example concerning two colours is not enough without explaining whether it is the same part of the subject at the same time. If the subject is understood collectively, different parts may receive different colours; the original incompatibility concerns one determinate receptive subject.

**L132.4** — The text objects to defining contraries by simple nonidentity, since any two different things would then count as contraries. The positive opposition in question includes an exclusion of coexistence of their determining forms under a fixed respect.

**L132.5** — It also objects to equating contrariety with contradiction. Contraries may both be absent from a qualified subject when a third determination is possible, while contradictory terms, under an identical respect, do not admit this.

**L132.6** — Finally, the glossator raises a subtle point concerning the qualification “at the same time”: if a subject has one state at one time and the contrary at another, this succession is not the coexistence the definition excludes.

**Unresolved:** The lower example of substances differing in blackness/whiteness and several modal qualifications continues into I:133. The exact wording of its reported authorial attribution is unverified.

**Arabic anchors:** «التضاد» · «كون الضدين بحيث يستحيل ذاتهما اجتماعهما في محل واحد من جهة واحدة» [reconstructed segmentation; check print] · «أسباب مفارقة» [uncertain].


---

## Volume I:133 — Opposition is not mere difference
*Original physical PDF p. 146 · Upper: continuous reading · Lower: connected objection and response, incompletely proofed.*

### Upper text

**U133.1** — The meaning of *inability to come together* is that the two cannot occur together in a single subject. The text does not mean that any two things whatsoever differing in character must be unable to occur anywhere together.

**U133.2** — Nor are every two things counted as opposed merely because one is ancient and the other originated. For what is ancient cannot bear what is originated in the relevant way, and the nonexistence of a positive state is removed when the positive state supervenes.

**U133.3** — The author adduces motion and rest, knowing and ignorance, and similar expressions only after fixing the subject and the mode in which each is said of it. It would be a mistake to infer opposition from the words alone.

**U133.4** — Two descriptions may therefore be mutually exclusive with respect to a certain body while another pair of distinct descriptions are compatible. The definition must mark the difference.

**U133.5** — Finally, when the condition is that opposites cannot occur “in one subject from one respect,” the phrase *from one respect* excludes cases where the same subject is considered under two differing determinations.

### Lower text — author unassigned

**L133.1** — The marginal writer comments that the theological use of “contrariety” often includes nonstrict oppositions, including some privative judgments; a logician's narrower use should not be read into every earlier passage.

**L133.2** — He questions whether the example of the nonexistence of an accident belongs under contrariety at all. If nonexistence is not a positive form subsisting in the subject, the restriction appropriate to two positive qualities may not be applicable.

**L133.3** — To say that smallness and largeness cannot coexist without qualification is also misleading. In a comparison with one object a body can be large, and in comparison with another it can be small; the relation must be fixed before alleging a contradiction.

**L133.4** — Hence he proposes that the relevant exclusion concern *one and the same respect* in one and the same receptive subject. The subject should not be understood merely as a general kind capable of including different individuals.

**L133.5** — A similar point applies to nearness and distance. These are not inherently incompatible when related to different termini. Likewise a colour attributed to one part is not automatically the contrary of a colour attributed to another.

**L133.6** — The text returns to the distinction between an opposition established by reason of the actual meanings and an opposition arising only by the way they are compared or predicated. The latter requires an explicit account of the respect of comparison.

**Unresolved:** The printed lower passage includes a discussion of “exclusion from a genus” and some contradictory and relational cases; these are only partially rendered here.

**Arabic anchors:** «معنى امتناع الاجتماع» · «لا يتواردان على محل» · «السواد والحلاوة» · «الصغر والكبر» · «القرب والبعد».

## Volume I:134 — Four kinds of opposition and the philosophical definition of contrariety
*Original physical PDF p. 147 · Upper: substantially connected first-pass · Lower: argument reconstructed with local uncertainty.*

### Upper text

**U134.1** — For example, the two blacknesses [in different subjects] may be called contrary by one who holds that their conjunction is impossible. The reply is that identity of the receptive subject is a condition of contrariety; one does not infer a contradiction from two determinations existing in different subjects.

**U134.2** — In the philosophers' terminology, the contraries are two existent states opposed to each other, the conjunction of which in one subject and in one respect is impossible.

**U134.3** — Among theologians the word “opposition” may be employed more widely, for modes of exclusion involving different descriptions and even certain privative states. The author sets out the four familiar opposed relations: contradictory affirmation and negation; contrariety; possession and privation; and relational opposition.

**U134.4** — The contrast between *difference* and *opposition* should not be lost. Two different objects may coexist without being opposites. Even two predicates one normally calls contrary can coexist if the conditions of sameness of subject and respect are not satisfied.

**U134.5** — Thus the restriction to one receptive subject and one respect does genuine logical work: it avoids turning into contradiction what is merely diversity across parts, times or relations.

### Lower text — author unassigned

**L134.1** — The glossator raises an objection to requiring “unity of subject” as though this alone established contrariety. For an opposing term may be considered as a form existing in the subject, or as a judgment expressed about it; the conditions for the two are not verbally identical.

**L134.2** — It is possible to say that one subject is not in motion and not at rest *under certain considerations*, without thereby refuting the proper exclusion between motion and rest where their receptivity and the relevant time are fixed.

**L134.3** — The distinction between two opposed descriptions and their simultaneous actual occurrence also matters. The definition should not imply that the opposed objects can never be spoken of together or cannot both be found anywhere in reality.

**L134.4** — The example of a small body compared with a larger and a smaller object clarifies the role of relation. Smallness and largeness are not absolutely inconsistent when the standards of comparison differ.

**L134.5** — The glossator examines “from one respect.” If this means that contraries exclude one another by *one same consideration*, the qualification is sound; but if it is used to evade all apparent opposition by shifting the respect at will, the definition becomes empty.

**L134.6** — He also distinguishes what the philosophers call contrariety within the category of quality from the theologians' wider discussion of negation, exclusion, and disparate predication.

**L134.7** — The disagreement over terms should not be allowed to erase the substantive question: what sort of determined subject, under what condition, could bear the supposedly opposed realities?

**Unresolved:** The glossator's commentary on “unity of subject as a condition” includes several alternatives attributed to the author of *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid*; I have retained the logical progression but not asserted a diplomatic translation of each alternative.

## Volume I:135 — Opposition in existence versus opposition in one subject
*Original physical PDF p. 148 · Upper: source-reading first pass · Lower: extended source-argument reading.*

### Upper text

**U135.1** — For two opposed things may both exist: the whiteness of one person and the blackness of another. Mere existence of both in the world is not the conjunction which the definition denies.

**U135.2** — Regarding the restriction to one respect, exclusion is not established by taking a predicate ascribed from different points of view. This has already been explained in the discussion of why opposites cannot be joined.

**U135.3** — If both were nonexistences, then [under the relevant definition] their exclusion would not constitute the same kind of contrariety. Thus negations require treatment distinct from the opposition of two positive forms.

**U135.4** — The author consequently returns to the types of opposed pairs and to the difference between the subjects to which they may be attributed.

### Lower text — author unassigned

**L135.1** — The lower writer warns that the qualification “in existence” must be interpreted carefully. If it merely means that both positive terms can be found in some individuals, this does not contradict their exclusion in one subject.

**L135.2** — Some discussions require the unity of the *thing which receives the determination*, while others speak of unity of the *judgment's subject*. The two are not always interchangeable.

**L135.3** — It is therefore possible to say of one collection that it contains a white member and a black member, without saying that the same receptive part is both white and black.

**L135.4** — The question also arises whether the absolute words “white” and “black” are themselves in contradiction. They are not: a third colour may be present, or the subject may lack the qualification that makes either applicable.

**L135.5** — This illustrates why a contradiction of propositions must be distinguished from an opposition of qualities. An affirmation and its negation admit neither conjunction nor joint absence under exactly the same subject and respect; two positive contraries may lack the latter property.

**L135.6** — On the expression “from one respect,” the author of the marginal text argues that a relative predicate has an additional condition concerning the term of its relation. Two differences of relation are not, without further restriction, contradictory.

**L135.7** — The discussion of “one” and “many” is also relevant. Unity and multiplicity can be predicated with different scopes and of different aspects of one subject; their coexistence under different respects cannot be used to erase their opposition under the very same respect.

**L135.8** — A reported argument in the *Maqāṣid* is therefore qualified: the stronger the definition's conditions, the narrower is the class of examples it correctly covers. An unduly broad definition may wrongly sweep in relations, negations and even cases where only nonidentity is meant.

**Unresolved:** Some lower examples of animals, humans and different categorical predicates are visible but not translated here line for line. The author's conclusion is rendered; the precise examples need further collation.

**Arabic anchors:** «كالبياض الرومي وسواد الحبشي» · «قيد وحدة الجهة» · «امتناع اجتماعهما» · «العلم بالاتحاد».

## Volume I:136 — Contraries, privation, contradiction and relational opposites
*Original physical PDF p. 149 · Upper: comparatively long and directly translated · Lower: selected connected critical discussion.*

### Upper text

**U136.1** — The two are **contraries** (*mutaḍāddān*) when each is compared with the other as a positive determination, as whiteness is compared with blackness.

**U136.2** — But if they are not both existent determinations, and one of them is a positive state while the other is a nonexistence, a further distinction must be made.

**U136.3** — If the nonexistence is restricted to a subject *prepared or naturally apt to possess* the positive state, the opposition is **possession and privation**: thus blindness is the privation of sight in the being naturally capable of sight, not merely the absence of seeing in any thing whatsoever.

**U136.4** — The author illustrates the qualified privation with the lack of a beard in a person of the appropriate age, compared with the absence of it where there is no natural aptitude. The qualification prevents every bare absence from becoming a privation.

**U136.5** — Where the absence is not so restricted, the opposition is **affirmation and negation**. Thus the opposition between the presence and nonpresence of a specified determination is not identical with that between the possession and privation of a natural capacity.

**U136.6** — A further class is **relational opposition** (*taḍāyuf*), as father and son; each term is understood in relation to the other.

**U136.7** — Some authors require, in the account of contrariety, the *greatest possible divergence* between the two qualities in the same relevant genus. This is why whiteness and blackness are often called contraries more strictly than two near shades of a colour.

**U136.8** — In certain usages the word *contrariety* is restricted to this narrower class, while the wider name *opposition* includes all the kinds just enumerated.

**U136.9** — The author next specifies that **unity of the subject** is required for opposition in the strict sense; this qualification matters differently in the different classes.

### Lower text — author unassigned

**L136.1** — The glossator asks what it is for a privation to be properly grounded in a subject. It is not enough simply to negate an attribute: one must ask whether the thing is within the natural field of application of that attribute.

**L136.2** — Accordingly the nonseeing of a stone is not blindness in the same sense as nonseeing in a creature constituted to see; nor is every present absence a defect, since the proper time and capacity may not yet have been reached.

**L136.3** — The lower discussion challenges the unqualified application of “preparedness.” Where the capacity exists only in potential, or depends on further conditions, does its absence count as privation already, or only after the appropriate occasion? The definition must state which meaning of natural preparedness it employs.

**L136.4** — In the case of relational opposites, by contrast, both correlatives may exist together. The father and son are not prevented from existing simultaneously; rather, one cannot understand the relational meaning of the one without reference to the other.

**L136.5** — This is a reason to avoid equating *every* type of opposition with inability to coexist in the world. The opposition is in the formal meanings and their directions of reference.

**L136.6** — The text also criticizes the assumption that the negation of a positive quality automatically becomes a second quality. Negation does not stand in the same ontological category as whiteness and blackness.

**L136.7** — In the case of contradictory judgments, exclusion belongs to the judgment's truth or falsity under the required conditions, not to the empirical inability of two independent objects to exist.

**L136.8** — The distinction between absolute opposed terms and opposed *predications* is therefore essential. A single expression may be understood differently once the subject, time, and respect are specified.

**Unresolved:** The lower discussion of the two types of negation and of the exact possibility of partial preparedness continues on I:137; several small-print examples need re-reading.

**Arabic anchors:** «متضادان» · «فإن قيد العدم بكون الموضوع مستعدًا للوجود» · «ملكة وعدم» · «كالعمى» · «التضايف» · «كالأب والبنوة» · «غاية الخلاف».

## Volume I:137 — Is opposition conditional upon a single subject?
*Original physical PDF p. 150 · Upper: visible connected first-pass · Lower: substantive commentary, with unresolved endings.*

### Upper text

**U137.1** — Unity and multiplicity, in opposition, are considered under a unity of the subject. But the fact that the unity of one member might be opposed to its multiplication does not establish that multiplicity in general is opposed to every unity wherever it is found.

**U137.2** — Nor is it required in the case of *contradictories* that the negative term be a separate positive thing standing against the affirmative. The negation is not itself existent in that sense.

**U137.3** — As for the opposing terms in *relation*, their meanings are correlative: if one is absent [under its relevant respect], the other is not understood as that relational opposite. In *contrariety*, however, both positive terms possess independent determinate meanings.

**U137.4** — Thus the unqualified claim that no two opposites ever coexist must be divided according to the type of opposition at issue.

### Lower text — author unassigned

**L137.1** — The writer first remarks that if *opposition* were taken as an absolute relation between two concepts, the requirement that they occur in one subject would be misleading. Several pairs are opposed in meaning although they may be predicated of different subjects.

**L137.2** — If instead the requirement concerns their joint *actualization in one bearer*, then it is a condition for excluding the coexistence of positive contrary qualities, not a universal definition of every mode of opposition.

**L137.3** — The text discusses the possible unity of the receptive subject at different levels. It might be one *individual* (such as one human), one *species*, one *genus*, or one thing taken in a further respect. The breadth of the selected unity changes the conclusion.

**L137.4** — The example of one subject being an individual human and simultaneously belonging to a genus should not be confused with one contrary quality taking the place of another in that same subject and respect.

**L137.5** — To say that multiplicity is “constituted by unities” does not make it simply identical with unity. The components of an aggregate may each be one; the aggregate is many when considered as those components together.

**L137.6** — Thus an explanation of multiplicity's dependence on individual units is not itself an answer to the logical question whether “one” and “many” are opposed under a fixed subject and respect.

**L137.7** — If the terms are compared as one unity and a further numerically distinct unity, neither is the negation of the other; if unity is contrasted with multiplicity taken in that same respect, a different opposition appears.

**L137.8** — The writer concludes that the attempted definition is too indefinite unless the unity of the subject and the intended mode of predication are expressly identified.

**Unresolved:** The lower leaf contains a difficult qualification about whether the *subject of opposition* may be one by kind rather than by person; this clause and the terminal join to I:138 remain uncertain.

**Arabic anchors:** «أن الوحدة جزء الكثرة» · «وُحدة الموضوع» · «أما في التناقض» · «وأما في التضاد».

## Volume I:138 — Beginning of the chapter on cause and effect
*Original physical PDF p. 151 · Upper: connected first pass · Lower: extended dialectical rendering.*

### Upper text

**U138.1** — **Chapter: On cause and effect** (*faṣl fī al-ʿilla wa-l-maʿlūl*).

**U138.2** — Since cause and effect are among the most widely used terms of theoretical inquiry, it is necessary to explain their meanings. They belong to the relations and modes of reference considered by the intellect, and there is a correspondence between them in their formal consideration.

**U138.3** — Considered in one respect, a cause is that **upon which a thing's existence depends**. Yet causes divide according to how that dependence is exercised and the role of what is called a cause.

**U138.4** — The text begins by distinguishing what belongs to the existence or constitution of the effect from what is external to its essence. [The detailed fourfold division is developed on I:139.]

### Lower text — author unassigned

**L138.1** — The lower writer first revisits the preceding dispute about unity and multiplicity. He notes that multiplicity is a plurality of unities; this does not imply that the relevant **whole** exists independently over and above all of its members.

**L138.2** — If one takes *unity* as the absence of division in an individual, and *multiplicity* as division into several members, one must not assume that the same subject is said to be one and many in precisely the same respect.

**L138.3** — One could say that a group is one as a collective and many as a plurality of members. The difference of the two predications does not establish that absolute unity and multiplicity are compatible as strict contradictories.

**L138.4** — Conversely, the fact that multiplicity consists in plural units means that its description cannot be detached entirely from unity. An account of opposition which excludes one from all reference to the other fails to explain the actual sense of the terms.

**L138.5** — The glossator also raises an objection from a part and whole: if the whole consists of parts, the unity ascribed to the whole is not a new independent individual in the same sense as each part.

**L138.6** — This returns the inquiry to the relation of what depends to that on which it depends. The formal notion of an effect (*maʿlūl*) involves a dependence which is not exhausted by the mere numerical difference between two objects.

**L138.7** — A cause is not simply anything other than the effect. The cause is specified by the direction of dependence: the effect obtains being or determination from what functions as its cause.

**L138.8** — In the subsequent division, an internal cause is constitutive of what is caused, while an external cause is not a constituent of its quiddity. The terms “internal” and “external” must be read relative to the constituted thing, not merely spatially.

**Unresolved:** The lower passage begins as the conclusion of the opposition debate and turns toward causality; the exact point at which the author changes topics is not fully bounded in the printed lower stream.

**Arabic anchors:** «فصل في العلة والمعلول» · «العلة ما يحتاج إليه الشيء» · «والمعلول ما يحتاج إلى العلة».


---

## Volume I:139 — Internal and external, complete and incomplete causes
*Original physical PDF p. 152 · Upper: extended first-pass translation · Lower: selected source-led objections to the division.*

### Upper text

**U139.1** — A cause is either **complete** (*tāmma*) or **incomplete** (*nāqiṣa*). An incomplete cause is either a part of the thing—that is, something entering into its constitution—or a condition outside it.

**U139.2** — If it is a constituent, and the thing's existence with it is **in actuality**, it is the **formal cause** (*ʿilla ṣūriyya*). If the existence of the thing with it is only **in potentiality**, it is the **material cause** (*ʿilla māddiyya*).

**U139.3** — For the formal cause is that through which the caused thing has actual existence [as the relevant constituted thing]; the material cause is that in which its relevant existence is in potency, like the wood as material for a bed.

**U139.4** — One should not assume that the material cause, simply because it can receive a form, already possesses the completed effect in actuality. The distinction between potential and actual constitution is precisely why material and formal causes have different definitions.

**U139.5** — If the cause is external to the constitution, that **through which** the effect comes to be is the **efficient cause** (*ʿilla fāʿiliyya*), as the carpenter is for the bed.

**U139.6** — The external cause **for the sake of which** the effect occurs is the **final cause** (*ʿilla ghāʾiyya*), illustrated here by the purpose for which the bed is made. [The sentence identifying this cause crosses into I:140.]

### Lower text — author unassigned

**L139.1** — The gloss distinguishes the *more general* use of the word cause, in which it embraces everything that enters into an explanation, from the narrower use signifying that from which an effect proceeds.

**L139.2** — With regard to the latter, the efficient cause is singled out for attention. But the full enumeration includes matter, form, the maker, and the end, each called a cause because the caused thing stands in a distinct sort of dependence upon it.

**L139.3** — The question then arises whether the material cause is accurately called a cause of the *being* of the composite. Matter is not by itself the actuality of the composite, yet it is the receptive condition which makes the composite's constitution possible.

**L139.4** — The glossator considers the example of wood and the form of a bed. The wood has an existence before becoming the bed; the existence of the bed, however, does not follow merely from the wood's presence. The form in which the wood is ordered into a bed must also be specified.

**L139.5** — This raises a difficulty for the statement that the formal cause is always “present with the effect in actuality.” Matter too is present with the completed bed, yet we do not call it the formal cause. The relevant distinction is not simple simultaneous presence but the nature of the constituent's explanatory function.

**L139.6** — The writer also asks whether the word *potentiality* in the definition of matter concerns the receptive matter itself or the form it has not yet received. This is important when the same underlying matter successively bears different forms.

**L139.7** — If the material cause were defined only by the existence of the effect in potency, one might have to distinguish first matter from a subject already possessing an earlier form. The gloss does not permit the two to be conflated without argument.

**L139.8** — Nor may the efficient cause be defined merely by its being outside the effect's parts, since many conditions external to its essence—tools, obstacles removed, an appropriately prepared subject—are not efficient agents in the same sense.

**Unresolved:** The lower account of the actuality of form in the composite contains compressed terms from the Aristotelian division; L139.3–7 are a connected reading with some interpretive supply, not complete clause verification.

**Arabic anchors:** «وهي إما تامة أو ناقصة» · «إن كان داخلاً» · «فهي علة صورية» · «فهي علة مادية» · «وإن كانت خارجة» · «فاعلية».

## Volume I:140 — Why causes of essence differ from causes of existence
*Original physical PDF p. 153 · Upper: continuous first-pass · Lower: selected extended disputation.*

### Upper text

**U140.1** — The cause **for the sake of which** the thing is brought about is the **final cause** (*al-ʿilla al-ghāʾiyya*): as the bed is made so that someone may lie or sit upon it.

**U140.2** — Some of the earlier thinkers called the formal and material causes the **causes of quiddity**, because the thing depends upon them in its *whatness*, not merely in its being externally realized.

**U140.3** — Others called the efficient and final causes the **causes of existence**, since these are related to the thing's actual occurrence or its coming to be for an end.

**U140.4** — The broader inventory of conditions and instruments can be referred back to the fourfold division; the *conditions* and *instruments* associated with an agent are accounted for in the explanation of its operation.

**U140.5** — The classification must be understood by reference to explanatory dependence, not as a claim that every item called a “cause” is an independent producer of existence.

### Lower text — author unassigned

**L140.1** — The glossator contests whether matter and form can in every case be defined as “causes of quiddity.” If the cause is taken as the *genus* or *differentia*, the relation to the parts of definition differs from the relation of wood and its arrangement in a bed.

**L140.2** — He notes that in certain natural bodies form belongs essentially to what the body is, while in artifacts the imposed arrangement may be accidental to the material substrate. Hence the illustration of a carpenter's work does not settle every natural case.

**L140.3** — The text examines whether a matter-form composite is correctly said to have two internal “causes” of its quiddity. The two are constituents, not things outside the essence which independently confer a separate completed essence upon it.

**L140.4** — Another difficulty concerns the actual existence of matter without a particular form. It is not enough to say matter is already completely actual and then simply receives another additive component: that would render the dependence upon form unintelligible.

**L140.5** — Conversely, if matter is described as pure potency, it cannot be treated as an independently complete corporeal thing to which form is merely juxtaposed. The intended *potency* is a relational capacity within the constitution of the composite.

**L140.6** — The gloss explores cases of the parts of definition: genus and differentia are distinguished in the account of the species, but the genus is not an independently existing external thing waiting for a differentia to be physically inserted into it.

**L140.7** — It then asks what justifies calling the genus and differentia “causes” in the same sense as efficient and final causes. An answer requires distinguishing the cause of knowing a definition from the cause of the real existence of the defined object.

**L140.8** — Finally, the lower writer tests the classification of instruments and conditions as subordinate to efficient causality. An instrument may be required for the agent's efficacy, but that does not mean the instrument and the agent are identical in their formal causal functions.

**Unresolved:** Several lower alternatives concern genus and difference rather than matter and form. They have been rendered conceptually and require close re-reading before a critical edition.

**Arabic anchors:** «فهي علة غائية» · «كجلوس عليه» · «علة الماهية» · «علة الوجود» · «مرجع الشروط والآلات إلى الفاعل».

## Volume I:141 — The complete cause and the dependent conditions of action
*Original physical PDF p. 154 · Upper: connected first-pass · Lower: further objections and a partial reply.*

### Upper text

**U141.1** — The various conditions and instruments associated with the efficient cause are placed within this explanatory classification, since they enter the circumstances by which an agent brings about its effect.

**U141.2** — The totality of **everything upon which the thing depends**, such that nothing further remains required, is called the **complete cause** (*al-ʿilla al-tāmma*).

**U141.3** — The qualification “everything” means that each requisite aspect is included, whether it concerns the productive agent, the receptive subject, the instruments, the conditions, or the relevant absence of an impediment.

**U141.4** — What falls short of that totality is an **incomplete cause**. It may nevertheless be a genuine explanatory principle, for an incomplete cause can be one of the necessary elements in the complete causal account.

**U141.5** — Accordingly the complete cause may be a single indivisible source when considered from the intended explanatory respect, or an ordered collection of several principles and conditions. What matters is that nothing requisite has been omitted.

**U141.6** — The explanation admits a further distinction between the complete cause *taken with its end* and an account that treats the efficient and receptive conditions without independently including the end. [The sentence reaches the top of I:142.]

### Lower text — author unassigned

**L141.1** — The lower writer examines the temptation to refer every instrumental condition to the agent as though it were simply a part of the agent. An instrument is required for the operation without thereby becoming numerically identical to the one who employs it.

**L141.2** — If the agent is said to exist before the effect, this by itself does not give the effect actuality. The agent's existence must be joined with the conditions under which its power is exercised.

**L141.3** — The same question can be posed concerning the receptive subject: the presence of matter capable of receiving a form is distinct from the completed actuality of the effect. One should not silently count the presence of the receptive subject as though it were the presence of the form.

**L141.4** — The glossator proposes that the term *complete cause* should refer to the whole of the relevant causal requirements, not to the efficient agent alone while abstracting from conditions it needs.

**L141.5** — A difficulty is then raised: if the complete cause is understood as the agent in a fully determined exercise of its agency, does the effect follow necessarily? The reply depends on whether the agent acts by necessitation or by choice.

**L141.6** — The account of an agent of choice must distinguish a requirement for an action's possibility from a condition under which an already specified intention takes effect. Without this distinction the inference to the effect may be too quick.

**L141.7** — The lower passage also reconsiders the phrase “incomplete cause.” An item can be complete for *one* operation or respect while being incomplete for the total existence of the ultimate product.

**L141.8** — Consequently the status of instruments, prerequisites, and absence of impediments must be indexed to the precise effect to be explained, rather than discussed as interchangeable kinds of dependence.

**Unresolved:** The printed lower argument contains a comparison of three kinds of requisite dependence, and details of how instrumental operations are referred to an agent. The above is a first-pass connected rendering and not a full transcription of every clause.

**Arabic anchors:** «والآلات إلى الفاعل» · «وجميع ما يتوقف عليه الشيء» · «يسمى علة تامة» · «والناقصة هي بعض ما يتوقف عليه الشيء».

## Volume I:142 — When the complete cause is present, must its effect exist?
*Original physical PDF p. 155 · Upper: substantially connected, with explicit page joins · Lower: extended selected objection-and-reply.*

### Upper text

**U142.1** — The complete cause may be considered with its end or without it. If the complete cause includes matter and what constitutes the thing, it cannot, in every respect, be said to precede the effect as if the completed constituents were an external producer existing in isolation.

**U142.2** — If one instead speaks of the precedence of each causal part, that precedence requires its proper qualification. The material and formal constituents, for example, are not both prior to the resulting composite in precisely the same manner.

**U142.3** — But when the **efficient cause is complete**—that is, when all aspects of effectiveness, all conditions, instruments, and the appropriate receptive subject are present, and nothing prevents its action—**the existence of the effect is necessary** (*yajibu wujūd al-maʿlūl*).

**U142.4** — Otherwise the effect's occurrence would be preferred to its nonoccurrence with nothing specifying that preference. That is the prohibited preference without a preferrer (*tarjīḥ bi-lā murajjiḥ*).

**U142.5** — The next question is the converse: does the existence of the effect establish that a complete cause is present? This is introduced at the end of the upper text and answered on I:143.

### Lower text — author unassigned

**L142.1** — The glossator objects that “completeness” should not be construed as every item on which the effect depends also depending on the effect. That would confuse the order of causal explanation.

**L142.2** — The dependence of the effect upon its conditions does not require those conditions to depend upon the effect. The direction of dependence remains asymmetric even if some constituent parts are inseparable from the resulting whole.

**L142.3** — The question is whether the supposed complete cause includes all *real* requirements for the effect, or also the conceptual relation under which the cause is described as a cause **of this particular effect**.

**L142.4** — If the latter is included as an extra real condition, an apparent regress can arise: the cause would require a further determination in order to be the determinate cause, and that further determination would itself demand a causal explanation.

**L142.5** — The reply distinguishes what is required for the effect's **existence** from the manner in which the intellect represents the relationship of dependence. A relational qualification does not always constitute an additional externally subsisting ingredient.

**L142.6** — The argument for the effect's necessary occurrence after the complete cause is therefore scrutinized. It is one thing to deny an unexplained selection among alternatives; it is another to assimilate the act of an agent of choice to a cause acting by its nature alone.

**L142.7** — A further possibility concerns specifying the time of an effect. If the agent's act is deliberately directed to producing an effect at a determinate time, the requirement is not that it be produced at an earlier time merely because the agent exists then.

**L142.8** — The author of the gloss questions which *aspects of influence* were included in the expression “all aspects.” If it includes the temporal specification itself, the conclusion may become tautological; if it excludes it, the account may not yet be complete.

**Unresolved:** The lower text contains several nested replies—one invokes the impossibility of needing yet another preferrer—and a quoted response from *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid*. Their precise start and stop points require a second pass.

**Arabic anchors:** «وعند تمام الفاعل بجميع جهات التأثير» · «من الشرائط والآلات والقابل» · «يجب وجود المعلول» · «امتناع الترجيح بلا مرجح».

## Volume I:143 — The converse inference and the creation of an effect
*Original physical PDF p. 156 · Upper: comparatively long source reading · Lower: partial but sustained translation.*

### Upper text

**U143.1** — **Conversely**, from the effect's existence one may infer the completeness of its efficient cause. For the effect's **need of an efficient cause** belongs to the consequences of its possibility, and possibility belongs to the effect.

**U143.2** — If the effect existed while its complete cause were absent, the effect would obtain being without that upon which it depends. This is excluded by the very nature of its possibility.

**U143.3** — The text considers the distinction between an **agent's initial production** and the effect's **continuance**. A building may remain after its builder has ceased building it. Therefore the mere absence of the original maker does not settle what accounts for the building's continued existence.

**U143.4** — In speaking of an efficient cause **in existence**, one must distinguish that which produces the object's first coming-to-be from that which accounts for its continued subsistence. A cause of origination need not, under every definition, be numerically the cause of its persistence.

**U143.5** — An effect's continuance may depend on something other than its first producer: some natural processes continue through conditions which remain after the initial act has ceased.

**U143.6** — Thus one cannot simply infer from a building's continued existence that the builder's building action continues unchanged. [The discussion of conditions for continuance is carried into I:144.]

### Lower text — author unassigned

**L143.1** — The lower author distinguishes the claim “the effect must exist when the cause is complete” from the claim “the cause must exist *at every time* the effect exists.” These need not follow with the same meaning of cause.

**L143.2** — If by “cause” we mean the *complete conditions for originating the effect*, those conditions must precede or coincide with the origination as specified. The persistence of the result may have another explanatory relation.

**L143.3** — If the supposed cause includes an agent whose being is temporally prior to the effect, one should not require that the agent be numerically renewed at every instant simply to preserve the earlier explanatory link.

**L143.4** — The text discusses an agent's choosing to act at a certain time. The temporal specification is not necessarily an independently existent real thing which, once added to the agent, makes the effect occur by necessity.

**L143.5** — A distinction is proposed between essential priority, temporal priority, and priority in the order of acting. A cause can be prior in explanation without being temporally separated from the effect by an interval.

**L143.6** — The commentator presses whether the effect's temporally delimited beginning must itself have an additional cause over and above the effective will directed towards that delimited action.

**L143.7** — The example of a father and his son, and that of an artisan and the artifact, shows how the causal relationship can be described differently depending upon whether one speaks of the activity of producing or of the continued constitution of what has been produced.

**L143.8** — Hence the lower discussion does not permit the original objection about preference without a preferrer to be repeated unchanged unless the exact object of preference is held constant.

**Unresolved:** The lower text continues into the next leaf, particularly the relation between the persistence of a completed artifact and the cessation of the initial craft action; several technical connectors remain uncertain.

## Volume I:144 — A cause of origination need not be the cause of continuance
*Original physical PDF p. 157 · Upper: connected visible-span first pass · Lower: extensive critical discussion, unresolved clauses indicated.*

### Upper text

**U144.1** — The agent [spoken of earlier] may be an agent for **origination** while the continued **subsistence** of its product is connected with a different cause. Indeed the agent of origination and what contributes to continuance may be distinct.

**U144.2** — Continuance may depend upon other conditions: a lamp's illumination, for example, persists through the continued provision of what feeds the flame, and ceases when those conditions fail. The original action and the condition of persistence must not be confused.

**U144.3** — The text then considers whether the **numerical unity of an effect** entails the **unity of its efficient cause**, by reason of the impossibility of two independent complete causes each producing the very same effect.

**U144.4** — If two causes are each alleged to be sufficient and independent for the same single effect, the effect's dependence upon one must be distinguished from its dependence upon the other. An effect cannot be entirely and independently produced twice as numerically the same effect.

**U144.5** — The thesis is not that several agents cannot cooperate in producing one composite result, but that one single effect, insofar as it is precisely one, cannot be referred to several complete independent producers under the same consideration.

**U144.6** — The argument about *one effect* is distinct from the converse thesis that one agent can have only one effect. The latter is treated separately.

### Lower text — author unassigned

**L144.1** — The marginal writer distinguishes the motions, changes and preparatory operations existing before an artifact or generated body comes to be from the continued existence of the result once those operations cease.

**L144.2** — The labor of the builder belongs to the process of constructing the building; when the building stands completed, the motion of building has ended. Hence one cannot simply say that the absence of a builder's current action must entail the absence of the building.

**L144.3** — On the philosophers' account, the preparatory causes may be necessary for the **occurrence** of the relevant effect without being continuously necessary as the very same preparatory causes once the effect is obtained.

**L144.4** — The commentator distinguishes this from a position referring all occurrences ultimately to the Necessary through choice. The meaning of a thing's depending upon its cause is not thereby exhausted by the temporal sequence of instrumental actions.

**L144.5** — The note asks whether the persistence of an effect always requires a *new* producer at every instant. If “persistence” means repeated new existences of a subject, one account follows; if it means the continuance of the earlier constituted subject, a different account is required.

**L144.6** — The example of successive ignitions is discussed. A later flame may follow an earlier fire, but the earlier occurrence as such is not the same event as the later one. The causal language must not erase numerical differentiation among events.

**L144.7** — The claim “one numerical effect has one efficient source” is next challenged by cases of several agents acting together: if different hands carry one object, or different movements cooperate in one work, why is this not a single effect with plural producers?

**L144.8** — A reply distinguishes an **aggregate result** from a simple result under one consideration. Each agent may contribute something not wholly identical to the other's contribution, so that the cooperative work is not one simple effect of two independent complete causes.

**L144.9** — Even if one calls the product *one* by ordinary usage, its relevant causes may be different in number and function. The exact force of the principle therefore depends on what sort of unity one attributes to the effect.

**L144.10** — The writer also considers a chain of mutual dependence among effects and asks whether making every part depend upon another changes anything about the need of the whole arrangement for an adequate causal explanation.

**Unresolved:** The lower leaf includes several alternatives concerning *individual* versus *specific* unity and an argument about the different sorts of “need” (*iḥtiyāj*) of a completed effect. The major transitions are rendered, but individual small-type clauses require checking.

## Volume I:145 — One effect and many causes; the rejected converse
*Original physical PDF p. 158 · Upper: connected source first pass · Lower: substantial translation with some interpretive supplies.*

### Upper text

**U145.1** — Were two alleged complete causes each separately to account for one and the same effect, it would follow that the effect **needed each** of them insofar as each is declared its cause, and **did not need either** insofar as the other is already independently sufficient.

**U145.2** — But need and independence in the very same respect cannot both obtain. Accordingly two independent complete causes of the same numerical effect are excluded.

**U145.3** — **The converse does not follow.** The unity of the efficient cause does not require the numerical unity of what proceeds from it. The [whole cosmos] is not evidence that the one Necessary can produce only one thing directly under every possible account.

**U145.4** — The author therefore examines the philosophical assertion that **from one, insofar as it is one, only one proceeds**. This is an additional argument requiring proof, not a mere reversal of the prior demonstration.

**U145.5** — Its proposed demonstration argues that, if two distinct things proceeded from one absolutely simple source, there would have to be distinct explanatory aspects in that source corresponding to the two effects.

**U145.6** — If the aspects were distinct in themselves, some complexity would seem to follow. If they were only differing considerations, it would still have to be shown that conceptual distinctions impose a limitation on what one real agent can produce.

**U145.7** — The author refers to an argument from the **unity of a causal series** (*ittiḥād al-silsila*) and judges aspects of its use weak. Its consequences are explored on the following pages.

### Lower text — author unassigned

**L145.1** — The glossator begins by testing the inference from the effect's needing each of two putative independent causes to a contradiction. If a thing is dependent upon a *conjunction*, then neither component taken separately is a complete cause.

**L145.2** — The contradicted hypothesis is not therefore the cooperation of several partial causes, but the supposition that each cause is individually complete and independently sufficient for the identical numerical effect.

**L145.3** — He asks whether one could reply that the effect is caused by either one **alternatively**, without requiring both. Such an alternative changes the hypothesis: to say that either could cause it under different counterfactual circumstances is not to say that both are the complete actual producer of the same occurrence.

**L145.4** — The distinction between a cause's sufficiency and its *actual exercise* of causality must again be observed. A purported independent sufficient cause does not cease to be sufficient because another capable agent exists; yet the single effect's actual attribution still requires specification.

**L145.5** — The argument from the one Necessary does not establish the converse principle “from one, only one.” Philosophers have proposed an additional proof using the apparent need for distinct causal aspects in the source.

**L145.6** — They argue that if several effects proceeded from the same pure source under the very same respect, no sufficient explanation would distinguish their issuance. But this imports an account of agency and specification which the theologian need not grant.

**L145.7** — The glossator questions the chain of emanated intellects used to avoid plurality in the First. If the derivation of plurality is postponed from the First to the first effect, plurality still has to be explained somewhere.

**L145.8** — Moreover, the one first effect may be regarded as possessing several intelligible respects—its relation to its cause, its own possibility, its existence—without each being a distinct really subsisting component. Why could analogous distinctions not be considered in the First without multiplication of its essence?

**L145.9** — Conversely, if every distinct concept is treated as a distinct real aspect, the proposed argument generates an unnecessary proliferation. What begins as a proof of divine simplicity risks multiplying explanatory entities in the effects.

**L145.10** — The issue is therefore whether an *intellectual distinction* is a sufficient real basis for the differentiated production of effects. This is where the philosophical and kalām accounts of the First diverge.

**Unresolved:** The references to the union of an emanative series and to the effect's dependence on each cause include nested hypothetical objections. The complete logical tree requires independent source collation.

## Volume I:146 — Multiple conceptual respects and the principle of one effect
*Original physical PDF p. 159 · Upper: connected first pass · Lower: extended dialectical reading.*

### Upper text

**U146.1** — The philosophers explain the origination of multiplicity from the First by referring to a multiplicity of *aspects and considerations* (*jihāt wa-iʿtibārāt*) in what proceeds from Him. They avoid assigning distinct real parts to the First itself.

**U146.2** — Their claim is that from the first principle, in which no plurality of aspects inheres [as real composition], a single effect first proceeds; multiplicity of effects then arises through the different considerations attached to that first effect.

**U146.3** — For a thing can be one in itself while the intellect apprehends it under different descriptions: existence, necessity through another, possibility in itself, its quiddity, and related conditions.

**U146.4** — If the distinction of these considerations is enough to explain a multiplicity of effects, there is the further question whether it also supplies a reason for the absolute restriction of the First's productive power.

**U146.5** — The author criticizes the proposed separation between what is held of the First and what is permitted in the first effect: the mere difference in conceptual analysis is not shown to make the latter productive of many while the former must remain restricted to one.

**U146.6** — The position of the opposing theologians then emerges: the Necessary is one in essence, but that unity alone need not prohibit the direct origination of various distinct possible effects by a choosing agent.

### Lower text — author unassigned

**L146.1** — The glossator distinguishes the plural respects in a **subject** from a plurality of parts *composing* that subject. A description can be many because of its intellectual relation to another thing, without the thing described being a collection of many independent existents.

**L146.2** — If this possibility is granted in the first emanated intellect, it is not obvious why it must be prohibited in every use of the account of the First; the needed difference must be independently demonstrated.

**L146.3** — The philosopher may answer that the aspects in the first effect are consequent upon its relation to its cause and upon the contrast between its possibility through itself and its necessity through another.

**L146.4** — But if these are primarily rational relations, the glossator asks by what principle they become enough to distinguish real effects. Their explanatory force seems different from a real plurality of powers or constituents.

**L146.5** — An agent of choice could be one in essence and produce distinct effects by distinct volitions or specified acts without thereby multiplying its very essence into several parts.

**L146.6** — The argument requiring separate **causal aspects** in the agent must therefore be compared with the doctrine that the act of willing selects its object. If the source's choice is left out, the purported proof may beg the question.

**L146.7** — The glossator takes up the analogy of a cause and its recipient: a difference of recipients may explain a difference of effects even though the productive source is treated as one. Such variation does not immediately establish composition in the cause.

**L146.8** — The effect may also vary because of its own circumstances, not merely because the cause consists of different intrinsic parts. This is a further reason to distinguish the complete causal explanation from the bare identity of one agent.

**L146.9** — The writer considers the claim that some of the First's attributes are *negative* or *relational* rather than distinct real perfections. That distinction has to be preserved when the apparent number of divine descriptions is invoked.

**L146.10** — What is decisive is not the raw number of verbal predicates but whether the supposedly distinct aspects are really additional beings, pure negations, rational relations, or genuine causal determinations.

**Unresolved:** The end of the lower text examines an extended example of a series of emanated agents and an objection about *sulūb* (negations). It is only partially translated here.

**Arabic anchors:** «كثرة الجهات والاعتبارات» · «المبدأ الأول» · «الوجود والوجوب والإمكان والماهية» · «وعند المخالف» · «شيئًا واحدًا».

## Volume I:147 — Does multiplying aspects destroy true unity?
*Original physical PDF p. 160 · Upper: source-inspected first pass · Lower: long selected objection and response.*

### Upper text

**U147.1** — If [the two things said to proceed from the First] are assigned distinct real principles in the source, then those principles themselves stand in need of explanation. The First's reality cannot be described as absolutely one while containing the supposed multiplicity as constituents.

**U147.2** — If, on the contrary, the source is one under exactly the same respect, the plurality of effects would have to proceed without those differing real intrinsic principles.

**U147.3** — **If one of the distinguished aspects, or both of them, enters into the reality of the one**, then that “one” involves **composition** (*tarkīb*), contrary to the asserted true unity.

**U147.4** — And if those aspects are not constituents of the source, the attempt to ground their issuance by appealing to further sources raises the difficulty of a **regress in the issuances** (*tasalsul al-ṣudūriyyāt*).

**U147.5** — The alternatives are therefore pressed: either real composition in the supposed simple first principle, or a chain of explanatory issuing relations which has not yet been grounded. [The reasoning continues on I:148.]

### Lower text — author unassigned

**L147.1** — The lower writer remarks that “one” may mean a thing numerically one in itself, or one considered without attention to the different respects the intellect can distinguish in it.

**L147.2** — A plurality of **conceived** aspects does not of itself prove that the subject is really a composite. But the argument under examination sometimes treats these aspects as if they were really distinct sources of separate effects.

**L147.3** — If the aspects are assumed really distinct, one is entitled to ask whether their conjunction belongs to the essence of the one or is due to something further. The former threatens composition, the latter introduces a new relation of dependence.

**L147.4** — The philosopher replies that the first effect may have one existence while being considered possible through itself and necessary through its cause. It need not possess multiple real pieces simply because these aspects differ in meaning.

**L147.5** — The glossator points out the consequence for the proof: if that is permitted, one cannot use the mere difference of conceptual respects as a reason why the First could not have several effects.

**L147.6** — The proof from composition would then apply only if the aspects were affirmed as genuinely real constituents. The premise that every plurality of effects needs such constituents remains unestablished.

**L147.7** — A further objection is addressed: even if several aspects are nonconstitutive, an infinite succession of prior aspects might still be posited. But this follows only if every conceptual distinction is mistakenly made into another real effect.

**L147.8** — The author then distinguishes a mere relationship of issuing from the existence of the producer and the produced. The relational description may be posterior in knowledge without being a new separately existing cause.

**L147.9** — The examples of a thing's being itself, its relation to its source, and its being described by what it produces are used to show the difference between relations of reason and extramental entities.

**L147.10** — Hence, a regress in the *words* or *intellectual descriptions* does not automatically yield a regress of real causes.

**Unresolved:** The lower page contains a technical dilemma using two alternative suppositions about which aspect “enters” the unity, followed by a distinction between entering in truth and entering in explanation. Its full logical scope remains to be collated.

**Arabic anchors:** «فإن دخل شيء منهما» · «ففيه» · «تركيب» · «وتسلسل الصدوريات أيضًا».

## Volume I:148 — The argument from issuing relations and its reply
*Original physical PDF p. 161 · Upper: substantial directly inspected first pass · Lower: connected critical discussion, several uncertain clauses.*

### Upper text

**U148.1** — **Otherwise**, if neither of the two differentiating aspects enters the thing's essence, and yet something is said to proceed from each, the discussion is transferred to that from which its own issuance proceeds. If that is again something additional, a **regress in the sources of issuance** follows.

**U148.2** — The objection is answered in two ways. First, if the alleged “otherness” of the differentiated source aspects means **otherness in extramental existence**, that premise is denied.

**U148.3** — For the respects on which the argument relies may be **intellectual considerations** (*iʿtibārāt ʿaqliyya*). They do not acquire external existence merely because the intellect distinguishes them or uses them in explanations.

**U148.4** — Thus what is attributed as the issuance of one thing from another need not be the issuance of an additional self-subsisting intermediary, and a regress cannot be generated merely from the repetition of relational descriptions.

**U148.5** — **Even if it is conceded** that the differing respects are real in the relevant sense, it remains possible that they are referred to a single true source, rather than to an indefinitely renewed hierarchy of independent sources.

**U148.6** — In this way the proposed argument for “from one, only one” is not established from the bare existence of distinct intelligible effects. It requires a stronger demonstration of the correspondence between multiplicity of effects and real multiplicity in the producing agent.

### Lower text — author unassigned

**L148.1** — The glossator examines what is meant by the “source of the issuing.” A cause can be considered as a being in its own right, and then under the description of being the cause *of this effect*. The latter description does not necessarily add another external substance.

**L148.2** — The argument for regress assumes that every such description itself has a real source from which it issues, and that the source is other than the previous source in the same way. This assumption is exactly what needs to be established.

**L148.3** — If one calls an intellectual relation an “attribute” or an “additional thing,” one may be speaking only of the mind's manner of comparing cause and effect. It is illicit to move immediately from this to an extramental causal chain.

**L148.4** — The lower author further challenges a proposed definition of the additional *relation*. To call a thing the source of another means that the latter's existence is ascribed to it, not that an independent third entity called “sourcehood” must be produced between them.

**L148.5** — A case is presented involving an artisan's motions: if a movement ceases, a later movement need not be related to it as to an independent efficient cause simply because both are included under one craft activity.

**L148.6** — Where the relation is treated as a **real determination**, its status has to be analysed: does it subsist in one relatum, in the other, or is it merely the rational description of their ordered dependence?

**L148.7** — The glossator does not allow the principle of a single origin to be asserted by reversing the burden of proof. The existence of multiple effects from one real source is not refuted merely by announcing that each effect must correspond to a really separate source-aspect.

**L148.8** — Moreover, even on the hypothesis of several distinguished respects, the source's unity might remain an essential unity. It would still need to be shown why the several possible effects should be incompatible with such unity.

**L148.9** — This is the importance of the final distinction: *difference by intellectual consideration* and *difference through really independent existence* cannot be exchanged in the middle of a demonstration.

**Unresolved:** The lower closing paragraph mentions an alternative account of *maṣdariyya* and a comparison to particular motions. The relation between a real effect and a real subsisting source must be independently collated before this can be called a critical translation.
