## Continuation I:149–198 (source-facing working first pass)

# Taqrīb al-Marām — Volume I, printed pp. 149–198

**Source-led first pass, 7 November 2026.** This document keeps the original printed page boundaries. **U** = upper, mixed embedded *Tahdhīb* lemma and Sanandajī commentary; **L** = separately printed lower gloss, authorship not assumed. English text is a working translation of identified portions, not an independently collated critical edition. Square-bracketed words are supplied for intelligibility. Where a long lower passage is only partially rendered, the omission is declared rather than silently compressed. The PDF source is image-only (632 physical PDF leaves); printed Volume I page *p* is PDF physical page *p+13*.

## Volume I:149 — The one and the many: formulations of the emanation principle
**U — upper running commentary (first pass).** If [further principles] were part of its very reality, composition and an infinite regress would follow. As for their saying that “from the one only one proceeds,” what is intended is that whenever the effect is multiplied its agent is multiplied. For the agent's being a cause of this thing is other than its being a cause of that thing; and if multiplicity of the effect necessarily entailed multiplicity of the agent, unity of the agent would entail unity of the effect by contraposition. Yet this statement does not settle the matter. It is remote from acceptable opinion also [to claim] that the absolutely one, being truly simple, cannot have more than one effect, or that a truly simple agent cannot also be receptive, on the supposition that acting and receiving are two effects. A distinction must be made between numerical diversity in what is produced and diversity of real constitutive principles in what produces it.

**L — separate marginal commentary (selected continuous argument).** The expression “if composition and regress would follow” applies when the plurality in question is placed in the very source of the effects. If two properties are held to be real and intrinsic, either each is a constituent of the source, or one is identical with the source and the other additional to it, or both are additional. The latter alternatives call for a cause of the additional thing, and thus renew the objection about composition or regress. The note then distinguishes a regress in the conceptual description of relations from a regress in the existence of real productive causes. **Remaining lower clauses not yet translated literally.**

## Volume I:150 — Agent, recipient, and the equivocation over possibility
**U.** It was argued that a simple thing, insofar as something issues from it, is acting; but it cannot in that same respect be receiving. The proof is expressed thus: the relation of agent to effect is by necessity, whereas the relation of recipient to what is received is by possibility. Necessity and possibility do not coexist in one thing [under one respect]. The answer: if the agent receives something when its effect exists, this does not require the recipient's existence to be conjoined with the existence of the received thing; conversely, if one takes both the agent and all that the effect requires, the existence of the effect necessarily accompanies that totality. There is thus no difference between the two descriptions simply in respect of necessity and possibility. In the first instance, the recipient's relation to what it receives concerns general possibility—not the negation of the possibility of existing. Moreover the agent is one; sometimes the effect exists independently under a supposed description, yet its receptor cannot be abstracted in the same way from the agent.

**L.** The marginal writer explains that if being a recipient entails the possibility of that which is received, the predication of possibility must be properly specified: does it mean that this particular received form may occur or not occur, or that the subject's existence is itself contingent? Likewise the claim of necessity in the agent may concern a complete cause or only something considered as efficient cause. A single simple thing is not shown to be both necessary and possible in precisely the same respect merely by assigning those two relations to it. The subsequent discussion turns on whether a bare receptive capacity has to be actualised with whatever it can receive. **Further lower qualifications remain to be translated.**

## Volume I:151 — Distinct respects; the finite powers of bodies
**U.** The meaning of general possibility is not one of its two species substituted for nonexistence—that is, [it is not] the kind called special possibility. It is the absence of necessity in what is receptive as receptive. There is no impossibility in the conjunction of necessity and nonnecessity under two different respects: something may be necessary insofar as it acts, and not necessary insofar as it receives.

**New chapter (فصل).** With respect to the divine power over possibles, it is permissible for acts of bodily powers to continue indefinitely in duration, in the sense that their action does not have to come to an end; this is the position of the people of the Sunna. What is denied, rather, is an infinite excess in the intensity of a bodily power, or its performance of infinitely many actions within a finite time. The philosophers hold that bodily powers necessarily come to an end in intensity, duration and number. At the objection of the philosophers, one must distinguish the different dimensions in which endurance and infinity are predicated.

**L.** The note stresses that a general concept of possibility is not the same thing as the particular possibility in which both existence and nonexistence are excluded from necessity. Possibility may signify the capacity to be affected, even though the efficient production of another effect is considered necessary under a different relation. In the debate about bodily powers, the philosophers suppose that every finite body has a finite capacity and a finite receptive constitution. The lower argument begins to distinguish finitude in degrees of intensity, the duration of the exercise of power, and the number of repeated acts. **The further proof remains only partly translated.**

## Volume I:152 — Intensity, duration, number, and the body's capacity
**U.** The point about the limit of intensity is that no motion produced by that power could have another motion faster than it [without limit]. The limit of duration means that it cannot perform its act through an infinite time. The limit of number means that it cannot produce a number of acts that is infinite. In a forced motion, the action of the power inhering in the body varies according to the thing being acted upon and its susceptibility; the greater the recipient, the weaker the action of the coercive mover may be. In natural motion the variation is traced instead to the acting nature: an increase in the quantity of body entails, on the assumed account, an increase in the strength of its natural power. Thus natural and forced motion are measured by different considerations.

**L.** The writer divides the proposed proof of finitude into three considerations. First, powers are ranked in strength, with the stronger power producing acts or motions that the weaker cannot match in the same conditions; if this ordering is taken to an actual infinite series, a contradiction is alleged. Secondly, actions may be ordered by the length of the interval during which they are exerted. Thirdly, one can count distinct acts, although numerical infinity is not simply equivalent to unlimited duration. In discussing duration, the gloss points out that a power need not be wholly exhausted merely because its acts are successive; the argument depends upon what is assumed about the diminution or increase of the capacity itself. **Remaining individual subarguments and counterexamples are not exhaustively rendered.**

## Volume I:153 — The objection from variation in natural and forced motion
**U.** Bodily powers of natural motion differ because their subjects are small or large; the larger may have the greater power. Suppose therefore that the smaller and the larger move, and that their motions are considered relative to the opposition of the other factor. In natural motion a large body has the stronger nature, so the smaller one's motion is not necessarily faster; with an imposed or coerced motion the resistance presented by the larger recipient may make the opposite ordering arise. From the assumption of unlimited duration one then draws, by considering the difference in intensity, an absurd consequence about the cessation of one motion and the continuation of another. Yet the objection itself turns on the precise conditions under which the alleged variation in velocity is established.

**U — continuation, explanatory remark.** The original expositor of *al-Maqāṣid* says that the proof of the impossibility of infinite duration by variation of intensity is that a motion which occupies the whole time required by one motion might be assumed to occur in half that time by a stronger power. It is then claimed that one of these motions would have to come to an end before the other, and an infinity would be divided into the parts of a finite ratio. This step needs careful qualification.

**L.** The gloss objects that the differences between the motions of the heavenly spheres, and between the actions of bodily powers generally, cannot be asserted to be simple quantitative increments unless their relevant conditions are identical. A forced motion and a natural motion differ not only by speed but by the dependence of one upon an external coercing cause and of the other upon the nature of the moving body. The objection therefore challenges the comparison of two infinities when the kinds of action and the grounds of their measurement have not been made uniform. **Remaining lower text not fully translated.**

## Volume I:154 — The proof from bodily powers; transition to circularity and regress
**U.** The proof divides corporeal powers according to their effects. If [infinite] strength were assigned to a finite body, then when the larger and the smaller body were compared, the larger's power and the other's power would have to be combined or diminished in a determinate ratio. The difference between the action of a small body and that of a great body would then require one to admit differences of strength where an absolutely infinite power was supposed. The argument is therefore about the dependence of bodily action on measurable magnitude.

**New chapter (فصل):** Regarding circularity and regress, the two proofs used here—the proof of dependence (*burhān al-taṭbīq*) and the proof of correspondence or alignment—are directed principally against an infinite regress of things that occur in an order. As for the circle, its impossibility is more immediate. A circle means that a thing depends upon something whose dependence in turn comes back upon the first. Since that would make the thing depend on itself, the dependent must be earlier than itself, which is impossible. One must distinguish the priority involved in a cause from temporal priority; the contradiction concerns the requisite priority of dependence even where no interval of time is posited.

**L.** The writer links the two arguments: if the same entity is made the basis of its own existence, whether through a single intermediate term or through several intermediaries, it becomes prior to itself. The supposed regress must be tested separately, since a series of simultaneous explanatory dependence differs from a succession of past events. **Subsequent lower clauses are incomplete.**

## Volume I:155 — What counts as the vicious infinite regress?
**U.** The regress at issue is not the mere indefinite succession of anything whatsoever. Rather, it is a series of things related by causality or by knowledge and the known, with no terminal member: everything that is assumed to be a cause has a further cause, and everything assumed to be known has something else by which it is known. This is not a claim that any unending sequence, whatever its arrangement, is impossible. The proof requires the relevant ordering and the requisite dependence of the members.

**L.** The marginal objection asks what is meant by a thing's being prior to itself. If “priority” signifies the priority of the cause required for the existence of what depends on it, the conclusion seems sound. But if the order is no more than a series in knowledge, where one proposition is understood through another, it must be shown that the dependence possesses the same demonstrative force. The marginal writer also distinguishes a totality assumed as a single object of consideration from an actually existing whole over and above its members; the subsequent proof must avoid simply presupposing that the whole has the type of unity it needs to establish. **The longer discussion of explanatory circularity is only partly translated here.**

## Volume I:156 — The totality of possible effects must have an independent cause
**U.** Suppose that the series did not end in a cause which was not itself an effect. There would then be a totality consisting of all the possible things, each of which is an effect. The totality is not identical with itself in the sense relevant to its cause: its existence cannot be explained merely by repeating that its members exist. The independent effective cause of the totality must be something other than the totality itself. Nor can it be one of its parts. If it were a part, it would already be included among the possible things for which a cause is sought. Consequently, one is compelled to posit a cause outside the series of effects.

**L.** The note asks whether the totality is itself something existing or merely the intellect's collection of several items. If it is not a distinct existent, an argument requiring a cause of the collection as one additional entity seems to fail. The answer offered is not that the totality has a real unity like a substance; rather, the claim concerns the causes of its members taken together. There is no independent cause of the whole from among the parts if each such part also depends on another. A further difficulty arises where the supposed “independent cause” is independent only of a given member rather than of the entire dependent order. **Several steps in this lower debate await close collation.**

## Volume I:157 — Why the first cause must be outside the series
**U.** Each of the things in the supposed totality is an effect; none is a cause without itself being caused. The circle (*dawr*) is impossible: it would require the same thing, directly or through intermediaries, to depend on itself. What is required is an independent efficient cause outside the whole series (*khārijan*), which is necessary. Thus the existent outside the series of possible things must be necessary, not merely another contingent item.

**L.** A marginal objection is that the collective whole may not be a thing separately caused at all: if its members are each accounted for, why demand another cause of their totality? The reply distinguishes the causation of the collection as an additional unit from the independence of the sum of dependents. An infinite number of parts, each of which is caused by another part, does not thereby furnish an independent cause of the dependent order. The writer considers whether a cause of each particular part might be independent of that part but still belong to the entire sum, and whether this suffices. The dispute turns on what “independent” means—independent with respect to one effect or independent without qualification. **The lower reply is longer than this excerpt.**

## Volume I:158 — Excluding a totality made of necessary and possible existents
**U.** The required cause cannot be assembled from two classes, necessary and possible existents: if one supposed the totality of both, the whole cannot be an efficient cause independently of the constituents which constitute it. [The printed conclusion reads:] “something other than its parts” (*shayʾ min ajzāʾihā* in the negative context), that is, no constituent within the totality meets the proposed requirement.

**L.** The note reports the objection that even if the cause must lie outside the series of possibles, one might imagine two nonterminating series, every member of one being dependent upon some member of the other. But the intended proof covers the collection of possible things without leaving out such an alternative series. Another objection says that a cause may be independent relative to one part while depending on another, so the predicate “independent” must not shift unnoticed between senses. The writer also tests whether considering all the parts collectively creates an illicit change from many distinct external existents to a single existent whole. **Most of the lower discussion is not yet literally rendered.**

## Volume I:159 — The collection is not a separately existing substance
**U.** [This printed page continues the previous sentence, with only a brief upper conclusion:] If the existence of each part, and of the whole qua totality, were considered, the relevant external cause would still not be supplied by what is merely included within the totality.

**L.** A single separate existence of the collection is not established merely by supposing its members; yet neither does this show that the members are self-sufficient. One may conceive the arrangement of many things as a unity of aggregation (*hayʾa ijtimāʿiyya*). This arrangement is not an extra thing standing beside every constituent like an additional independently existent substance. For this reason an argument that assumes the whole is a single existent, or that each part must contribute a separately subsistent cause of the whole, is objectionable. On the other hand, if every one of the constituents is dependent, their union does not produce an independently existing cause. The marginal writer repeatedly contrasts independence in being with the intellectual consideration of parts and whole. **The extended examples and the end of the objection are pending literal translation.**

## Volume I:160 — The proof from the totality is not the same as an infinite-chain correspondence proof
**U.** The alleged sum need not be a thing distinct from the sum of its parts. If the necessary cause stands outside the sum, then not every member of the whole collection is a possible dependent. If it is included in the sum as a part, there is no longer a sum solely of dependent possibles. The relevant series must therefore terminate in a single necessary cause; for if each member of the supposed series were still an effect of another, nothing in the series would provide the independent cause. The argument should be kept distinct from an attempted proof that simply places one infinite series alongside another and alleges that one exceeds the other by a finite part.

**L.** The marginal writer argues that a totality need not exist externally as something above its components in order for all its components to require explanation. The claim of a necessary source outside the series means outside the class of wholly dependent members, not necessarily outside every possible conceptual grouping. The objector may argue that the property of needing a cause does not transmit from each part to a collected whole. The reply shifts attention to whether any part is uncaused. In the absence of such a part, the explanatory demand remains. **The subsequent distinction between genuine composition and unity of aggregation remains partially untranslated.**

## Volume I:161 — Aligning two infinite series
**U.** Take two series such that one extends farther than the other, and align them from the end at which they coincide. One series contains an additional member or members. Apply their members one to another from that common end. If the series are finite, their lengths cannot be equal in the presence of the additional part; if they are infinite, the argument seeks to show a contradiction between the assumed excess and a one-to-one matching. The proof depends on the claim that either the two series cease together or one reaches a terminal point earlier.

**L.** The objection is that equality of two infinites cannot be inferred merely by pairing their terms from an end. An infinite series has no last term in the direction in which it is infinite, so a correspondence from the common finite end may persist without assigning a final unmatched member. Moreover the expression “the two series become equal” may mean equality of the number of matched terms, which is not obviously the same as equality of the whole magnitudes. The lower writer discusses the difference between an infinite potential progression and an infinite collection conceived as complete, and how the classical correspondence proof makes a stronger assumption than the mere possibility of constructing a pairing. **Full lower proof and objections still require a literal second pass.**

## Volume I:162 — The alleged necessary termination of the shorter series
**U.** In the comparison, every part of the shorter series is set opposite a part of the longer. If the shorter lacks the additional members of the longer, it is said that the shorter must end; otherwise one would allow the shorter to be equal to the longer despite the latter's excess. But the argument cannot simply presume that “not equal” already means “finite.” When an ordered series is taken in the sense of causes and effects, its direction of dependence is relevant; the question of termination may be argued independently of numerical correspondence. Because every effect is dependent upon a cause, the series cannot be treated as a disconnected aggregate.

**L.** The gloss says that the argument called the correspondence proof (*burhān al-taṭbīq*) can be applied to any class of infinites only if they are actually composed of discrete, ordered members; it fails if one uses the conceptual possibility of indefinite increase as though it were a completed infinity. An objector can imagine two series each without a limit, one with an additional first member; a pairing may then be effected without their being finite. The writer distinguishes this challenge from the causal proof: the latter asks whether an entire dependent chain has a ground, whereas the former concerns the arithmetic properties of ordered collections. **Additional passages are omitted from this first pass.**

## Volume I:163 — The correspondence of causes to their effects
**U.** For every member of the series, except what would be a first cause, there is another which is its cause. But if one aligns the series of causes with the series of effects and calls them equivalent, the claim of endless causation is not thereby vindicated. Every effect requires a cause, and so the series of causes has a member corresponding to each effect; a further causal priority appears on one side. The intended proof claims that a causal series must terminate, otherwise a difference between the two aligned series becomes unintelligible. In the consideration of quantity, it is also said that the infinite cannot be exhausted by equal subdivisions or multiplications: the finite addition of a term does not convert an infinite into a finite total.

**L.** The lower writer presses an objection to treating all the effects, taken together, as a single caused totality, and notes that the proof may use the term “whole” in different senses. The comparison of sequences produces questions about equalities and inequalities, not yet an independent proof that the entire dependent order exists by itself. There is also a difference between the infinite divisibility of a continuous quantity and an infinite number of actual discrete causes. **Most of the lower example is not yet rendered.**

## Volume I:164 — The infinite and the properties of division
**U.** An infinite series does not terminate through division; but it is not correct to treat every division of the infinite as though it yielded two equal infinities. Take the first part as one unit, the second part as two, and so on. Each further division may introduce a different relation of number or ordering. The proposed refutation of the infinite by dividing it into two equal halves therefore needs an account of what equality means. [Transition to a new chapter:] In explaining the difference between matter and form, what is meant by a form is that by which a thing is actually one in its essence and by which its constitution is determined.

**L.** The gloss distinguishes the claim that a quantity is divisible without end from the claim that it contains an actually existing infinity of separate parts. An infinite is not made finite merely by assigning a property to each stage of a division. The author then asks whether accidents of an aggregate belong to the whole as such or to its individual members. The discussion of form and matter begins by considering when they may properly be called two constituents and when the distinction lies in conceptual analysis. **The remainder of the lower text is still incomplete.**

## Volume I:165 — Matter, form, and the limits of their causal roles
**U.** By the consideration just explained, “matter” denotes the receptive aspect, as in the wood with respect to a bed or the body with respect to a form. The same two principles may be described differently when each is considered under the relation of composition: matter in one respect and form in another. If the aspect of part-hood is considered, their respective relations to the compound are not interchangeable. The final cause does not simply become the efficient cause; and the need of an effect for an efficient cause remains even where one understands what constitutes the effect's matter and form. In the case of a voluntary agent, an intention precedes the production; this does not abolish the distinction between an agent and what it produces.

**L.** The gloss warns that saying “the matter of the form” and “the form of the matter” is ambiguous: the words can indicate the intrinsic constituents of a genuine compound, or merely a description based on an intellectual relation. In the wax and seal example, the wax is the underlying receiver; the impressed character is not an independently existing substance that can be added to the wax as one adds two bodies. The illustration of a composite artifact likewise distinguishes the *shape* that makes it this artifact from a separately subsisting constituent. When form is the essential constituent of a natural thing, however, the problem is not identical with that of an artificial arrangement. **The extended marginal discussion is partially translated.**

## Volume I:166 — The four causes and two kinds of end
**U.** The final end is considered differently according to the preceding account. The attainment of a purpose may be posterior in existence to the effect and yet prior as the reason on account of which an agent acts; the “end” is therefore prior in the order of causation but posterior in realization. The efficient cause is the source of existence, whereas matter and form are constituents of the thing whose existence is explained. Some philosophers regarded certain possibles as causes of others in this order. For us, however, God creates the causal connection in the ordinary course of events. There is no necessity that the observed customary conjunction be a binding efficient relation within the created things themselves.

**L.** The marginal writer distinguishes the final end as *what is actually attained* from the end as *what is conceived and sought*. If the end is supposed to be prior in time to the work, it cannot be the completed result; if it is said to be prior in thought and intention, the priority is intelligible. The cause of an artifact need not be its final result. In another usage “form” can name the formal character of the effect rather than a detachable element. The gloss applies this to the common examples of a sword's shape, timber's construction, and a natural composite. **Lower continuation unfinished.**

## Volume I:167 — Book Three: Accidents; existence and the divisions of the existent
**U (concluding the previous discussion).** Reason may indicate that a thing's existence depends upon an agent, although it does not thereby establish which changes must occur in the nature of the alleged cause.

**New book — الباب الثالث في الأعراض — Book Three, On Accidents.** The claim that substance is primordial [with respect to accident] is supported by cases involving states of substances: motion, rest, the temporal generation of bodies, and the traversal of a distance in a finite time. These observations are employed to argue that bodies and their accidents are originated.

**Chapter One.** The existent is divided into the existent which is not preceded by nonexistence—the Necessary, possessing the attributes proper to it—and the originated. The originated is either a substance or an accident. Substance is what occupies a locus in itself (or is construed in relation to a locus as independent); accident is what depends upon a locus and does not occupy it in its own right. The verbal definition depends upon the school under discussion and is not simply interchangeable between theological and philosophical usage.

**L.** The gloss explains that a substance's being described as existing in its own right does not mean that its efficient causation has been fully settled; the division concerns its manner of being in a locus. If one says that an accident exists, one is not thereby granting it a separately occupied space. The following debate turns on whether the locus is itself a substance and whether an accident may subsist in another accident. **The end of the lower proof is not fully translated.**

## Volume I:168 — Why an accident requires a subject
**U.** It is fitting to distinguish accidents by what is grasped through the senses and what is apprehended intellectually. Some are known by the external senses, such as life, and some are known by their effects or by intellectual evidence. Among the categories are quantity, quality, relation, place, time, position and possession, together with acting and being acted upon as conceived by the philosophers. As for existence considered as an accident: the philosophers say that existence must be an attribute of an underlying existent, meaning that it does not exist independently of a subject. But the claim that existence is an additional thing is not the same as asserting that there is an extra constituent occupying another place beside the subject. The term *ḥāl* (“state”) has also been used in discussion of a subject's being qualified by an attribute. It does not necessarily posit a separate matter or form.

**L.** The lower writer distinguishes logical dependence upon a subject from physical composition. Life, for example, is not a freestanding body located adjacent to the animal whose life it is. Yet the conceptual distinction between “life” and “living subject” does not suffice to establish a second independent entity. In speaking of “existence as an accident,” the scholastic use of “accident” must be kept separate from the Aristotelian categories. The argument also distinguishes a concept that the intellect abstracts from the real condition of a thing. **Further definitions and examples remain untranslated.**

## Volume I:169 — Whether every accident needs a subject
**U.** The subject and what is predicated of it must not be confused. Among the accidents are the categories of quality and relation, including fatherhood, sonship, spatial position and possession. Certain accidents can be conceived as lasting through an interval, though their instantiation depends on the persistence of what bears them. The second question is whether a single accident can subsist in more than one subject. Some understand a “shared” attribute as a unity by species or by common notion; that is not numerical unity in an individual accident. An accident's occurrence in several subjects therefore cannot be established from the commonness of its designation.

**L.** The note says that a subject's relation to its accident must be distinguished from a relation between two already complete subjects. If a body is in one place and then another, the change of its place is not the migration of one independently existing accident in the manner that a body moves from location to location. It is possible to call an attribute the same by genus or species while its individual occurrences are distinct. When accidents are said to be “in” a subject, the preposition names a dependence, not a spatial enclosure. **The longer examination is incomplete.**

## Volume I:170 — Unity of an accident and plurality of its bearers
**U.** First, the existence of a single accident in two subjects is impossible if the accident is a single individual. For if it were present in both simultaneously, there would be a numerically single dependent thing with two distinct seats of inherence. This is different from the assertion that the two subjects have qualities of the same kind. A comparison is drawn with one body's being in two places. The philosophical dispute about common accidents has to specify whether what is common is a concept, a natural kind or an individual occurrence.

**L.** The note distinguishes several purported universals: existence is not a genus encompassing substance and accident in the same manner that animal encompasses its species; other modes of predication also cannot be collapsed into a genus. The claim that an individual accident belongs to one bearer must not be proved by assuming from the beginning that a universal concept cannot be multiply instantiated. Rather, its individual existence is tied to its subject. If a body changes place, the absence of an additional freely travelling accident is compatible with the body's continuing to be one body. **Additional argument about the subject of relations remains incomplete.**

## Volume I:171 — Fatherhood and sonship, proximity and distance
**U.** An accident differs from fatherhood and sonship as the relation of a father to a son differs from the relation of a son to a father; the relation of siblings or neighbours also involves different subjects. The concrete subject that is the father is one, but the fatherhood by which he is related to the son is not the son's sonship. Thus the relations are multiple even where their correlates have a common basis. Nor does a merely common relation imply that a numerically one accident subsists in more than one bearer.

**U — Third inquiry.** An accident cannot be transferred from one subject to another; for its existence is existence in this particular subject. When someone points to an accident in one place and subsequently finds a similar one elsewhere, that does not mean the same individual accident has changed its subject.

**L.** The gloss notes that, if one takes a relational predicate as numerically one in two persons, one risks making a single individual attribute inhere in several bearers. One can nevertheless allow multiple relations to arise together by one event. The issue is whether numerical unity of a relational accident is preserved when its two termini are interchanged, not simply whether two persons possess a relation of the same generic description. **Several lower objections are not yet fully rendered.**

## Volume I:172 — An accident does not migrate between subjects
**U.** The continued existence of the accident is tied to its original subject. Its absence from that subject when the body moves to a new place is not, by itself, the transfer of the identical accident to the other place. Nor can its individuation (*tashakhkhuṣ*) simply be identical with its bare essence, for otherwise distinct instances of the same accidental type would be impossible. It must not be supposed that pointing to the same predicate means pointing to the same individual accident. The result is that an accident cannot be separated from the subject in which it exists and transferred intact to another.

**L.** The marginal writer gives the example of a body located first here, then there: it is the body that moves, not the first accident of location which remains identical while travelling to a second subject. He challenges the formulation that an accident's very existence is nothing but its being in a subject, asking whether the account is explanatory or merely restates the definition of inherence. The response distinguishes its quiddity, its concrete instantiation and its dependence upon the bearer. **Detailed continuation omitted.**

## Volume I:173 — Why the transfer of an accident is incoherent
**U.** Its nontransfer is not established simply by a verbal prohibition. Rather, transfer would mean its occurrence after having ceased to exist in the first subject, or would require that its existence persist independently of the original locus. If the same accident remained as it passed from one bearer to another, it would have to be the same individual while its individuating condition changed. That would destroy the grounds on which its numerical identity was asserted. The discussion also considers whether the accident's inherence in a bearer is itself an additional accident: if so, a regress of inherences might result.

**U — Fourth inquiry.** The dispute about the inherence of an accident in another accident turns on the sense of inherence (*qiyām*). If inherence means qualification, it is one kind of relation; if it means the metaphysical dependence of what cannot subsist alone, the matter must be analysed differently.

**L.** The lower writer distinguishes two readings of transfer: the subject of an accident might move from one location to another while retaining its quality, or the accident might leave the first subject and inhere in a different subject. The first does not imply the second. He then questions whether an accident's relation of inherence can itself be treated as an accident without an infinite regress; the argumentative force rests upon whether one is positing an independently existing entity at each stage or only a dependent consideration. **Full lower dialectic remains incomplete.**

## Volume I:174 — Can an accident subsist in another accident?
**U.** An accident cannot subsist in an accident in the manner of an independent subject, since the second accident is itself dependent upon a substance. If one claims that substance is a bearer of a first accident, and the first accident the bearer of another, the very relation of inherence requires clarification. Some predicate an accident of another accident under the description of an attribute belonging to an attribute; that is not necessarily numerical inherence in a freestanding accident. The example of colour and its intensity, or blackness inhering in a body, shows that some relations of qualification are expressed by successive predications without creating a separate substantial subject at each level.

**L.** The gloss explores whether an accident may qualify a second accident only by qualifying their common bearer. It distinguishes “white body” from “intense whiteness”: the intensity is predicated of the whiteness, but the whiteness does not become a substance. Some authors allow an accidental feature to modify another accidental feature; others interpret that as a shortened predication about the primary subject. These disagreements must be resolved by stating whether “in” means dependence in being, qualification, or spatial occupancy. **Additional polemical discussion remains to be translated.**

## Volume I:175 — Accidental qualities, persistence, and the divine attributes
**U.** The attributes of God most high, according to the theologians, are among the attributes of the intellect; according to the philosophers they are treated differently. The question of whether an accident may subsist in a substance is not answered by saying that all possible inherence is permitted. If every inherence is possible, one may ask whether an accident inheres in an accident, in a substance, or in either. The philosophers deny that the same accident can occupy a succession of substrates. Consider the motion and speed of a moving body: motion is the actual change, and speed is a qualification of that motion. Some theologians therefore call both motion and speed accidents, while others understand the latter as an additional consideration rather than a separately occurring accident.

**U — Fifth inquiry.** The majority of theologians argue that an accident cannot persist through two instants. For an accident is something originated and renewed; if it persisted, it would be the same individual accident at successive moments. The argument is often stated by appeal to the difference between an origination that is caused at each moment and the persistence which the philosophers attribute to the same accidental form.

**L.** The marginal writer stresses that the inability of an accident to subsist independently does not prove, without further argument, that every one of its individual occurrences ceases at the next instant. One may distinguish the requirement of a subject for an accident from the requirement of a new efficient cause for its continuation. What the theologians seek to establish is that remaining in existence requires dependence on God, rather than the physical self-sufficiency of the created accident. **The remaining objections to this inference are not all rendered.**

## Volume I:176 — Why persistence is said to require another accident
**U.** The theologians say that the persistence of an accident would require its *remaining* (*baqāʾ*). But remaining is not identical with its initial origination; if remaining is another accident, the remaining of this accident would itself require another remaining, and the argument would generate a regress. They also reply to the philosophers that persistence can be understood not as a second positive entity added to an accident but as its being continuously present through the following time. The dispute therefore turns on whether *baqāʾ* is itself an accident, or is a term for the continued existence of the same occurrence.

**L.** The gloss distinguishes the disappearance of an efficient cause from the cessation of what was caused. If the occurrence of a thing required a cause, it does not follow from that fact alone that the cessation of the thing's cause immediately entails its nonexistence; an additional argument is needed about the kind of effect at issue. On the other hand, if persistence is construed as a new existential attribute, it requires an account of how that attribute is produced and maintained. The note asks whether the very concept of survival implies an entity over and above the thing that survives. **The lengthy lower controversy is only partly translated.**

## Volume I:177 — The extinction of accidents and the analogy with substance
**U.** It is not valid to conclude that an accident's subsistence in another accident is equivalent to its dependence upon a substance. The first relation is at issue, whereas the second is assumed in the account of an accident. One argument for the nonpersistence of accidents is that if an accident could persist, either its continued existence would be necessary or it could cease. But nonexistence does not require a further existent that produces it in the way that existence requires an efficient producer. The removal of the relevant condition, it is said, suffices for its disappearance. If this condition were itself an accident, a regress would follow, unless one specifies where the dependence terminates.

**L.** A marginal reply objects that the removal of an accident might itself depend upon an efficient change in the bearer. If the condition is merely the continued existence of the bearer, it does not account for the observed disappearance of one quality while the bearer remains. If the condition is the existence of the same accident, the explanation is circular. The distinction between a *cause of existence* and a *condition for continuance* must be maintained throughout. **The later lower comparisons with creation, cessation and duration are incomplete.**

## Volume I:178 — Does the destruction of an accident require an opposite?
**U.** It may be said that an accident disappears by the arrival of an opposite accident, for example, rest replacing motion. But if the disappearance of one accident depends on the appearance of its opposite, and the opposite's appearance presupposes the disappearance of the first, there is a circle. To say instead that one disappears first and the other is then produced does not give the explanation sought, because one still has to account for the prior disappearance. Another position treats disappearance as the nonproduction of a like accident at the next moment. The author examines whether such a nonproduction must itself have a positive cause or whether one can understand the absence as absence.

**L.** The lower text distinguishes removing a condition, producing a contrary accident, and simply not renewing a transient accident. The contradiction arises if each contrary is posited as the cause of the other's cessation in the very same respect. The proposal that successive opposed conditions occur in successive instants avoids one formulation of the circle but requires independent support for the assumption that both are numerically new. The discussion of a sufficient condition for cessation is not identical with the discussion of what produced the prior accident. **The full lower exchange remains incomplete.**

## Volume I:179 — Quantity: the divisible and the indivisible
**U.** The theologian's argument is directed against a possible claim that accidents and bodies have indefinitely divisible parts, or that an accident can remain without any inhering subject. The consideration of a body without qualities leads into the question of quantity.

**New chapter (فصل) on quantity (*al-kamm*).** Quantity is an accident whose very nature admits division, whether actual or according to supposition. The parts into which it can be divided may be supposed to have a common boundary or not. There are continuous magnitudes and discrete quantities. Division in thought is not necessarily actual disintegration of a real object; accordingly, a logical account of divisibility should not be taken to establish that all its possible parts already exist separately.

**L.** The gloss asks whether the common definition “that which is divisible” concerns the magnitude itself or an operation that the intellect can perform on it. A line, surface or body may be susceptible to division without being a heap of already separate points, lines or surfaces. The theologians' atomistic account and the philosophers' continuity account thus disagree at the level of what actually constitutes a magnitude. **The long lower discussion of the atom remains only partially translated.**

## Volume I:180 — Continuous quantity and its possible parts
**U.** When the recipient is present together with the possible quantitative division, it does not follow that every part has already been separated. A continuous quantity is not thereby actually composed of finitely or infinitely many independently existing parts. The definition of continuous magnitude concerns the possibility of a division into parts having a common limit, whereas numerical quantity concerns a number of discrete units. If a continuous body is divided, the actual division is an event affecting the measured body; it does not prove that the prior body already consisted of independent separated magnitudes.

**L.** The marginal debate turns on the difference between a subject's *capacity for division* and its *actual division*. If the separated parts do not pre-exist as separated parts, one need not accept that the body is composed of a completed infinity of discrete parts. Yet one can also say that any section of a continuum contains a further quantity liable to division. An objection asks whether such a division requires all the possible parts to be simultaneously present. A reply distinguishes potential parts in the continuous whole from actually distinct units produced by partition. **Further analysis of the definition of continuous magnitude is unfinished.**

## Volume I:181 — Discrete quantity and the parts of a surface
**U.** Number (*ʿadad*) does not require that its units be themselves extended: it comprises distinct unities. A continuous quantity, by contrast, has parts joined at a common boundary. The parts of a body must not be treated as already separate simply because they can be distinguished intellectually. The term *surface* applies to a quantity with two dimensions; *line* to one dimension; *mathematical body* to three. Magnitude is discussed by length, breadth and depth, not by the assumption that every ultimate part of a magnitude is a separately existing point.

**L.** The gloss explains that a number does not cease to be a number merely because its several units are thought together, but a line ceases to be one continuous line if its segments are actually separated by a gap. If one supposes the units of a quantity to be in perfect contact, one must still distinguish contact from numerical identity. The definition of mathematical body depends on three dimensions, whereas a natural physical body carries additional qualifications not expressed merely by quantity. **Selected technical clarifications remain untranslated.**

## Volume I:182 — Lines, surfaces, bodies, and numbered entities
**U.** The continuous quantities are the line, surface and body. A line has extension in one dimension; a surface in two; a body in three. The number, by contrast, is formed from units and is called a discrete quantity. These distinctions are not merely names for particular sensible objects: the mathematical treatment abstracts extension from the further qualities of bodies. The theologians, however, may regard number as a conceptual consideration (*iʿtibārī*) rather than a separately subsistent entity; philosophers treat mathematical magnitude as quantitative even where it is abstracted from a natural body. In this latter usage the *quantified* (*miqdār*) concerns the actual size of the body, not a heap of independent points.

**L.** The writer discusses whether a line can exist except as the boundary of a surface, or a surface except as a boundary of a body. One can consider the body as three-dimensional without positing independently existing planes as constituents of its natural substance. Conversely, mathematical abstraction does not forbid separately examining its boundaries. The debate about the endpoint of a line and the boundary of a plane is therefore not settled simply by the logical possibility of abstracting them. **Further detailed lower arguments remain pending.**

## Volume I:183 — Endpoints, intervals and the three divisions of time
**U.** The line and surface terminate in boundaries: a line at points, a surface at lines and a body at surfaces. A boundary does not itself have the same number of dimensions as that which it bounds. Time is also described through past, present and future. The existent past cannot be identical with the non-existent future; the present is treated as the division between them. But if the present were an extended interval in the same way as a span of past time, it too would admit division into earlier and later, and the inquiry into its boundary would arise anew. In such discussions one must distinguish the mathematician's conceptual boundary from an actual independent substance.

**L.** The gloss contests the inference that because each time-span has a beginning and an end, time must be built from a number of independently existing instants. A point of a line and an instant of time mark boundaries, not discrete component magnitudes occupying extension. The mutakallimūn may assert that successive temporal atoms occur; the opponent argues that if a duration were made entirely of such indivisible instants, the interval between them could not be accounted for in the ordinary sense of continuity. **Additional lower arguments on the present and passage of time remain unfinished.**

## Volume I:184 — Successive temporal parts and the question of a beginning
**U.** The nature of time and motion entails that their successive parts are not all present together. If one supposes time to be composed of indivisible and permanently present parts, one contradicts the succession that time is meant to measure. The earlier and later are ordered; a later time is not present in the earlier one. The arguments from the succession of parts and from their noncoexistence are brought against treating the continuum as a collection of simultaneously actual fragments. Yet the fact that a duration is continuous also does not license an actual infinite regress of completed temporal parts. A distinction is drawn between the absence of a first moment and the supposed completion of infinitely many already elapsed moments.

**L.** The writer asks whether an indivisible present is simply a conceptual boundary or an actually existing indivisible unit. If the former, successive instants do not compose time as stones compose a heap; if the latter, it becomes necessary to explain how adjoining indivisible units yield an interval. An opponent replies that the possibility of cutting a duration at successive positions does not establish that all its possible boundaries are already actual. The remaining controversy concerns the origin of motion and whether time can be supposed without the succession of bodies and their states. **The original lower text continues beyond this selection.**

## Volume I:185 — Past, present, and future are not three coexistent parts
**U.** If time were something existing in its own right, all the temporal differences would seem to belong to that existent. But the past has ceased, the future has not yet come to be, and the present cannot be divided indefinitely into the other two while remaining the present. An objection says that we divide time into past, present and future just as we divide a body into parts. The answer is that the division of a body concerns parts capable of existing together, whereas the parts of time are successive. The past and the future cannot both be present together in one temporal now. If the whole time is said to exist at once, this contradicts its supposed succession. The term “present” must therefore be taken in its proper relation to the temporal order, rather than as an extended container holding past and future simultaneously.

**L.** The writer observes that the alleged nonexistence of past and future does not mean that one cannot truthfully speak of past and future events. It means that their temporal presence is not identical with the existence of the present interval. If a thing once existed, its present nonexistence does not make its earlier existence unreal; equally, a possible future event need not exist now. The argument about time does not automatically settle whether time is an independent entity, a relation to motion or a conceptual measure. **Further lower qualifications unfinished.**

## Volume I:186 — Temporal succession cannot be an assembled simultaneous whole
**U.** It may be said that the temporal whole consists of parts joined in one order and has one common character. But if one part is present while the preceding part has passed and the following part has not yet arrived, the temporal whole cannot be a composite whose members coexist. To suppose that the parts coexist contradicts their order of succession; to suppose that they are separate presents raises the question of their continuity. Hence the division of time is not the same as the actual division of a body into simultaneous constituents. The question is not resolved simply by imagining an indefinite succession without considering what persists through that succession.

**L.** The gloss notes that a remembered past event and an anticipated future event may both be objects of the intellect now, but this is not the same as their being simultaneously present in external time. It distinguishes the mental consideration of a whole temporal series from the simultaneous external presence of each member. A critic of continuous time may appeal to the successive disappearance of indivisible instants; an opponent replies that the absence of overlapping instants does not prove that time is constituted of atoms. **The middle of the lower debate requires further collation.**

## Volume I:187 — Priority, posteriority, and the measure of motion
**U.** Temporal priority does not mean that an earlier and a later time exist together as two simultaneous things. Rather, the impossibility of conjunction is precisely what establishes their order. Priority by essence, however, may be predicated without temporal separation. In time, “before” and “after” are understood by considering the order of the parts of a movement. The argument must not infer a separate substantial existent called time from the fact that the intellect can compare motion before and after a certain event. It is the order of change that supplies the basis of temporal designation, although the nature of this ordering is disputed.

**L.** The writer criticizes the claim that time itself is another magnitude existing beside motion and its subject. One can distinguish duration, which is conceived as an interval, from the numerical or relational measure of changes. Yet the application of before and after cannot be reduced to the movement of a single arbitrarily chosen body, since several movements may be compared as contemporaneous. The question is whether some motion is the standard of measurement, or whether time has a more general basis than any one moving subject. **Not all lower replies are rendered.**

## Volume I:188 — Why time is described as an extended measure
**U.** The third [consideration] is that if a magnitude is to be attributed to past time, its nonexistence at the present does not preclude its having been a duration. Nor does the present instant need to be a part of time in the same manner as an interval. It is rather the boundary between past and future. The authors describe time as an extension (*imtidād*) distinguished by before and after: that description is intelligible even though successive parts are not all presently existent. If time were to consist of identical, independently present units, their conjunction would not explain the precedence of one to another. The appropriate conception is of a continuous order whose parts do not coexist.

**L.** A marginal objection says that the past and future, being respectively no longer existent and not yet existent, cannot be the parts of a presently existing substantial whole. The reply is that the denial of such a whole is compatible with describing a duration as continuous in a relevant sense. What must be demonstrated is not simply the possibility of enumerating its imagined boundaries, but the kind of existence belonging to its extension. **The subsequent proof against a substantial notion of time remains partial.**

## Volume I:189 — Time as the measure of motion
**U.** Time is the measure of motion in respect of before and after. Its existence is necessary for locating motions in temporal order, but this does not make time a substance independent of whatever is changing. “Greater” and “lesser” times measure different durations: the movement of the outermost sphere, taken by the philosophers as the principal measure, may be used to calibrate the rotations of smaller bodies. We distinguish months, years, days, and hours by reference to regular motions. The smallest units of a reckoning should not be supposed already to be indivisible constituents of all the measured motions. The disagreement concerns whether the measure is itself an external continuous quantity or a consideration of the motion measured.

**L.** The gloss returns to the astronomers' reckoning of days and hours, observing that the measurement can depend on one standard motion without making all other motions ontologically dependent on that standard. To compare two motions, one needs a common means of quantifying duration, but the selection of a particular celestial rotation does not by itself establish the metaphysical reality of time. **The lower examination of the use of number in temporal measure is incomplete.**

## Volume I:190 — Is the measure of the highest motion time itself?
**U.** Some define time as the measure of the motion of the highest sphere. If this definition is taken literally, one must determine whether the intended measure is the physical magnitude of the sphere's movement or an abstract quantity through which events are ordered. For if a change occurs in a thing other than the heavens, it is still temporal, even if one uses the heavens as the standard by which its duration is reckoned. The definition therefore needs a more exact account of what is measured and what serves as measure. It is not sufficient to identify the extension of the sphere with the universal concept of time. The philosopher may mean a quantity extracted by the mind from motion rather than an independently existing substance.

**L.** The note distinguishes the concept of time from one chosen clock. A measured change may occur independently of any observer who calculates its duration; that is not the same as proving that time is an absolutely separate existent. The phrase “the amount of the movement of the greatest sphere” can be read as an epistemic means of counting duration, or as an attempt to state time's very quiddity. The former is more modest and avoids some objections. **Longer discussion of conceptual versus real magnitude remains incomplete.**

## Volume I:191 — A philosopher's definition and its theological criticism
**U.** The philosophers' principles have been contested in three ways: the alleged eternity of motion, the claim that the motion is continuous without interruption, and the denial of a beginning of time. The theologians say that time is a determinate, known quantity, not an unlimited existent required by the nature of every created movement. If the question is when a given event occurred, reference to a known event suffices to specify its place in the temporal order. Thus one can say that an event occurred at sunrise without being committed to a separate body or substance of time. The appeal to the heavens as the universal measure does not establish an infinite series of prior motions.

**L.** The lower argument addresses whether a continuously existing heavenly mover would require an infinite past. One line of objection maintains that no present event can be reached after the completion of an actual infinity of earlier successive parts. Another distinguishes the eternity of the general capacity to move from the actual existence of an eternal motion. The theologians' criticism cannot be answered merely by saying that “time is motion,” unless one explains the relevant sense of equivalence. **Several paragraphs here remain to be rendered.**

## Volume I:192 — Time and the persistence of created things
**U.** The question is whether the existence of a subject remains the same while its movement varies. If the material subject exists through the change, the movement is not identical with the subject's bare existence. The philosopher may appeal to motion as continuous, while the theologian treats each temporal state as originated. The distinction between existence, nonexistence, and persistence must not be collapsed. If a thing has ceased to exist, the mere passage of time is not an additional agent that caused its cessation. The temporal order is a description of successive occurrences, not the producer of their existence.

**L.** The gloss distinguishes motion as the continuous passage from a starting point to an endpoint from motion as a sequence of positions or states. If a body is said to move through successive points of a distance, does it stand in each point for an instant, or is its movement continuous through them? One cannot infer from the possibility of dividing its path that it is actually at rest in each supposed part. The debate is connected with the theologians' principle that nothing created has an independent continuation in being. **Some lower technical transitions remain uncollated.**

## Volume I:193 — New chapter: Place (*makān*)
**U (conclusion of time).** The issue of priority and posteriority is related to the continuance of successive things, which is different from supposing the whole interval to exist at once.

**New chapter (فصل في المكان).** Place is that in which something is situated; it is the locus occupied by a body, not another body identical with the located one. Disagreement arises over whether place is the inner surface of the encompassing body or an extension distinct from bodies. The philosophers call it the inner boundary of the surrounding body, while other accounts understand place as the extent or space occupied. The distinction matters when a located body moves: the body remains the same while its place changes. The atomist instead analyses spatial location through the occupancy of separate positions.

**L.** The marginal writer questions whether a bare surface can be place if it is only the boundary of a surrounding thing, and whether an empty extension can be a real place where no body exists. The definition of place is not interchangeable with the definition of a body; a body may be in place without place being another body of the same sort. The differences between the philosophical and theological definitions turn on the meaning of surrounding, contact and separability. **The last lower clauses are not fully rendered.**

## Volume I:194 — The surrounding surface and the claims for space
**U.** One theory identifies place with the inner surface of the encompassing body. On this account the air surrounds a bird, and water surrounds a fish; the relevant place is the limit of the containing medium in contact with the contained body. Another theory identifies place with an extension imagined or supposed between the enclosing limits. The argument from the body's movement is invoked: as the body moves, its situation changes, but the mere fact of changing situation does not prove that an independently existing empty extension lies beneath the body. The proponents of the surface account appeal to what directly touches the located thing.

**L.** The note asks whether, if place is the innermost boundary of a surrounding body, the exact area of that boundary must equal the surface of the located body. Where a body is irregular, or where two possible surfaces are conceived, the definition raises further questions. The distinction between equality of magnitude and numerical identity must be maintained. If the surrounding air or water is itself moving, the definition must also explain in what sense the located body retains or changes its place. **Several linked objections are left for a later literal pass.**

## Volume I:195 — The surface of the container is not a fixed spatial receptacle
**U.** Opponents argue that, if place is the surrounding surface, the same place need not remain when the enclosing bodies change. Suppose a bird flies in air or a fish moves in water: at each position the surrounding parts differ. Some nevertheless say that the place is the same insofar as a common spatial extension is occupied. Others deny that there is a universal independently existing container; they understand place as the current contact of boundaries. If motion consists in the change of place, it may therefore be described as the replacement of one surrounding surface by another, without assuming that the surface itself travels with the moved body.

**L.** The marginal objection considers a moving bird and surrounding air: when both move, the surface in contact with the bird changes even if the bird is considered stationary relative to some wider framework. Conversely, the same body can remain in approximately the same location while the air adjacent to it is replaced. This challenges the claim that contact alone provides an adequate account of place. A more general spatial measure, however, must not be smuggled in merely by presupposing a fixed coordinate framework. **The longer comparison remains incomplete.**

## Volume I:196 — Does the possibility of place entail a vacuum?
**U.** The philosophers deny that place is an empty extension that subsists without a body; some theologians instead affirm a spatial interval capable of occupation. When we speak of the position of the body and the surrounding air, we must distinguish the measured location from the matter occupying it. The proposed extension may be regarded as a thing really existing, as merely imagined, or as an ordered relation between bodies. The two sides dispute whether a place can be said to remain when a body leaves it and nothing else fills it. A statement that “this location is empty” need not refer to a positive empty substance unless such a thing has first been established.

**L.** The writer raises the familiar difficulty of an alleged vacuum: if the interval is something existing, what kind of existent is it and what explains its dimensionality? If it is nothing, how can it be equal or unequal to another interval? The latter question may involve a conceptual measure rather than a thing subsisting without any body. The distinction between imaginable extension and actual extramental extension is therefore critical. The lower text further contests whether all change of location must involve the movement of surrounding matter. **Full lower controversy not yet translated.**

## Volume I:197 — The vacuum between smooth bodies and the movement of air
**U.** It is said that two bodies with perfectly smooth surfaces may be in mutual contact, and that when one is raised from the other there must at first arise an empty gap. The opponents of the vacuum deny this consequence: air may move into the intermediate region as the surfaces separate. But does the air have sufficient time to occupy the entire opening? The argument turns on whether it can move to the midpoint from every side without first leaving a gap. The theologians do not concede that a vacuum is impossible; they maintain that God may create the movement of air or its cessation as He wills. The proof is therefore not a strict logical demonstration of the impossibility or necessity of an empty interval.

**L.** The gloss asks whether separating two perfectly smooth surfaces entails a simultaneous motion of air from the sides, and whether that movement could occur without an intervening interval. The objector supposes that when the surfaces cease touching at the centre, no air can already be present there. The reply challenges the assumptions about the continuity and speed of the motion of the surrounding air. The exact problem depends on whether the surfaces are treated as mathematical planes or physical surfaces. **The remaining lower objections are uncollated.**

## Volume I:198 — Empty space and the timing of air's entrance
**U.** Suppose one smooth surface rises from the other. Every portion of its upward motion occurs in time and traverses a certain distance. In the very first part of the ascent, an intervening region is formed. To say that air immediately fills every part of it requires explaining how air traverses the required lateral distance. If it comes from the margins, the region at the centre cannot be filled before the corresponding movement has occurred. The response compares two different intervals: the time of the upward motion and the time of the motion of the air that would fill the new space. If one supposes the ascent to occur in a given duration and the air's movement to occur more rapidly, it may be claimed that the gap is filled; but it cannot simply be asserted without considering their respective distances and speeds.

**L.** The writer tests the example by supposing that the upper surface is lifted in half the time required for air to pass from the edge to the centre. In that case a gap would remain for part of the interval. The opponent may reply that air can move at the required speed, or that the assumed instantaneous generation of an empty region is misconceived. The mathematical comparison of times does not by itself decide whether the vacuum is metaphysically impossible. **The lower dispute continues beyond the end of this batch, on I:199.**
